## Code Style

<!-- rule:35 -->
- Use single quotes for Python string literals (`'x'`, not `"x"`) — matches the style used across `django/` and `tests/` and avoids noisy quote-flip diffs in reviews. — The codebase consistently uses single quotes in both the top-level `django/` package and the `tests/` tree, so mixing double quotes in new or edited strings creates inconsistency and churn when contributors' editors or `black`/`ruff` reformat quotes on save.
<!-- rule:387 -->
- Transform the raw rule into a concise, actionable guideline for AGENTS.md. — properties track live state; `setAttribute` only seeds the HTML attribute. — These three habits all stem from the same distinction: HTML attributes are initial/default values, while DOM properties reflect current live state. Drivers and widgets that read back `getAttribute` or `.attr()` after user interaction (e.g. checking whether an element is disabled, or its current `value`) get stale data when the two are set inconsistently. `setAttribute('disabled', true)` is doubly wrong — it writes the literal string `'true'`, which is non-empty and therefore technically "enabled" by spec semantics, even though browsers treat the attribute's presence as authoritative. Standardizing on property assignment keeps reads and writes symmetric across any frontend JS touched in the repo.
  - In jQuery, use `.prop()` over `.attr()` for boolean properties — `.attr()` reads the initial HTML value, so toggles silently desync from actual state.
  - When `setAttribute` is the only option for a boolean attribute (e.g. `disabled`), pass `''` not `true` — `true` stringifies to `'true'`, an invalid attribute value.
<!-- rule:4616 -->
- Use `textContent`/`.text()` or `createElement`+`append`/`replaceChildren` for dynamic text, never `innerHTML`/`.html()`/template literals — prevents XSS in Django admin JS (`django/contrib/admin/static/admin/js/...`) and in `docs/howto/javascript.txt`/`docs/ref/forms/api.txt` examples that copy-paste into user code. Prefer `element.textContent = value` over `appendChild(document.createTextNode(value))` — same effect, less code. — Treating interpolated values as HTML enables XSS; doc examples are especially risky because they get copied verbatim into downstream projects.
<!-- rule:572 -->
- Strip trailing whitespace on every line before committing — including blank lines, comments, docstrings, and multi-line strings — and enable editor trim-on-save. — Trailing whitespace is invisible in editors but shows up in diffs, so stray spaces/tabs create noisy patches, spurious merge conflicts, and failures in whitespace-checking linters/CI. Blank lines and lines inside strings and comments are the usual offenders because editors rarely highlight them. Automated trim-on-save keeps the rule out of code review entirely, so contributors only argue about actual code.
<!-- rule:3 -->
- Omit blank lines directly after `class`/`def` declarations — flake8 `E301` and Black reject them — Autoformatters and linters strip the blank line anyway, so leaving one in splits the diff from what `black`/flake8 produce and forces extra churn on the next format run.
<!-- rule:2527 -->
- Omit redundant parentheses — `func(x for x in xs)`, `if cond:`, `[expr for x in xs]`, not `func((x for x in xs))` or `[(expr) for x in xs]`; keep them only for grouping, precedence, tuples, or line continuation. Indent `if`/`elif`/`else` bodies one 4-space level. — Matches PEP 8 and eliminates noise that hides real grouping intent; paren-heavy diffs get flagged in review as style violations.
  Rationale: Matches PEP 8 and eliminates noise that hides real grouping intent; reviewers otherwise flag paren-heavy diffs as style violations.
<!-- rule:4753 -->
- Use plain string literals (`'text'`, `"text"`) — no `u''` prefix; Python 3 literals are already unicode. — String literal style must be uniform across `django/`, `tests/`, and `docs/` because mixing `u''` prefixes, `force_text()` conversions, and bare literals creates inconsistent type behavior between Py2 and Py3 and obscures which strings are unicode. A single `from __future__ import unicode_literals` per module is the least invasive fix and disappears cleanly when Py2 support is dropped.
  - For Py2/3-compatible modules needing unicode, add `from __future__ import unicode_literals` once at module top; skip it in Py3-only modules; never convert at runtime via `force_text()` — one module-level import beats per-literal prefixes and avoids mixed `str`/`unicode` bugs.
  - In docs, refer to `str` (not `unicode`) and use plain string literals in examples — matches the Py3 API users actually see in code.
<!-- rule:815 -->
- Run the project's linters/formatters (`black`, `flake8`, `pre-commit`, `tox`) on changed Python files before pushing. — Catching formatting, line-length, missing-import, and syntax issues locally — including continuation-line alignment one column past the opening parenthesis and lambda spacing like `lambda x: x` — keeps reviewers focused on logic instead of style, and avoids a CI round-trip. CI lint failures usually signal a real defect in your changes, not an environment problem, so fix and repush rather than attributing them to CI.
<!-- rule:282 -->
- Delete dead code after refactors — unused attributes, locals, helpers, imports, obsolete TODOs — the leftovers trip lint (flake8 `F401`) and leave stale paths that mask bugs. — Dead code hides real call sites, drifts out of sync with refactors, and fails lint checks like `flake8 F401` — especially when the same unused import lingers in parallel backend-specific files.
    - Remove unused class/instance attributes *and* their parsing/population logic, including class-level metadata like `name` on Django `Func` subclasses, consistently across every module that defines them.
    - Drop unused local declarations/assignments instead of partially rewriting them; reuse the existing variable rather than redeclaring.
    - Inline private helpers with one remaining call site — e.g. fold `QuerySet._create_object_from_params()` into `get_or_create()` once `update_or_create()` stops calling it.
    - Delete imports with no referenced symbol (native async replacements, direct imports over aliases, unused namedtuple imports) and scrub the same import from parallel files (e.g. backend-specific test variants) so lint passes everywhere.
    - When swapping to a decorator or abstraction, remove the old inline implementation rather than commenting it out; if kept briefly to verify in CI, delete before merging.
    - Don't add such attributes to new classes unless actually consumed, and skip cleanup machinery like `addClassCleanup(delattr, ...)` unless `hasattr` checks or correctness need it.
    - Exception: documented public APIs get an explicit deprecation path instead of immediate removal.
<!-- rule:5273 -->
- Use one space around `=` in Python assignments (`x = []`), even in docs code samples. — PEP 8's single-space assignment style must hold uniformly in both `django/` source and `docs/` code samples so readers see consistent formatting; manual alignment introduces churn in diffs whenever a variable name changes length.
    Manual alignment with extra whitespace breaks PEP 8 uniformity across `django/` and `docs/`, causing inconsistent style and noisy diffs.
<!-- rule:1274 -->
- Mirror pattern fixes across all sibling implementations in the same change — avoids partial migrations and divergent behavior — Sibling implementations that drift out of sync (e.g., `handle_m2m_field` vs other `handle_*_field` methods, `xml`/`json`/`yaml` serializer backends, sibling decorators, Django admin attribute-resolution paths like `__str__` guards, or parallel Django/Jinja2 and HTML/text templates) cause inconsistent rendering and force repeated fixes. Keep structure, control flow, guards, filters, and formatting in sync; factor shared assignment logic into a common helper rather than duplicating between save phases.
<!-- rule:373 -->
- When modifying a CSS selector, class, or asset reference, apply the change consistently to every occurrence and related usage site throughout the stylesheet — including duplicated rules in media queries (e.g., `forced-colors`) and directional/reverse variants — rather than only the immediate diff hunk. — Applying a CSS change to only the diff hunk leaves duplicated rules in media queries like `forced-colors` and directional/reverse variants stale, causing style regressions in those contexts.
<!-- rule:375 -->
- Use `===`/`!==` in JavaScript; allow `== null`/`!= null` only — loose equality coerces types (e.g. `0 == ''`, `'1' == 1` are `true`), causing subtle comparison bugs, and `== null` is the idiomatic check for both `null` and `undefined`. — Type coercion in loose equality produces surprising `true` results across numbers, strings, and booleans; the sole exception `== null` is intentional because it covers both `null` and `undefined` in one check.
<!-- rule:4821 -->
- Indent Python with 4 spaces per level (never tabs). For wrapped/continuation lines, add one extra 4-space level rather than aligning under the opening delimiter or receiver; keep all hanging indents inside parentheses at multiples of 4 spaces. In comments, align bullet continuations under the bullet's text — consistent 4-space indentation avoids tab/space mixing errors and keeps diffs minimal across the repo. — Standard 4-space indentation (PEP 8) with explicit continuation levels prevents tab/space mixing bugs and keeps formatting deterministic across `django/`, `tests/`, and other top-level areas. Aligning wraps under delimiters produces fragile indents that shift whenever identifiers are renamed, causing noisy diffs; fixed 4-space hanging indents stay stable. Aligning comment bullet continuations preserves readability of long docstring/comment lists.
<!-- rule:251 -->
- Write regex patterns as raw string literals (`r"..."`, `br"..."` for bytes), including patterns embedded in docstrings. — Non-raw string literals silently process backslashes, so `"\d"` or `"\b"` reach the regex engine mangled — patterns match the wrong text, or emit `DeprecationWarning`/`SyntaxError` for invalid escapes like `'\d'`. Docstring patterns hit the same trap and are easy to miss. Raw literals keep the pattern byte-for-byte what the regex engine sees; switching delimiters avoids noisy `\\` runs that obscure intent.
  Drop the `r` prefix only when the pattern has no backslashes or escape sequences worth preserving.
  Pick the quote delimiter that minimizes escaping (e.g. `r'..."...'`).
<!-- rule:3379 -->
- Be explicit about newline matching in regexes: use `re.DOTALL`, `(?s:...)`, or `[\s\S]` when a match must cross newlines, and exclude `\n` or anchor to line boundaries when it must not — `.` without `re.DOTALL` silently fails to span lines, while a greedy `.*` on optional-terminator line patterns (common in `django/template/base.py`, `django/test/utils.py`, `django/contrib/staticfiles/storage.py`) silently over-matches across lines — both bugs that show up as wrong parses rather than errors.
<!-- rule:267 -->
- Prefer `startswith()`/`endswith()` (tuple for multiple) and `in` over `re.match`, `.find() != -1`, `split()` tricks, or chained `or` — idiomatic and avoids regex overhead. — Native string methods are faster and more readable than regex or manual manipulation. Using the same prefix variable in checks and stripping prevents divergence. Direct indexing is only safe when non-emptiness is guaranteed.
  - Use a single regex alternation for multiple forbidden substrings; strip prefixes/suffixes with `removeprefix()`/`removesuffix()` (same variable) over manual slicing.
  - Reserve `s[0]`/`s[-1]` for benchmarked, guarded hot paths where the string is guaranteed non-empty.
<!-- rule:266 -->
- Insert new or relocated entries at their correct sorted position in alphabetized lists; never append. Covers `django/conf/global_settings.py`, `docs/ref/settings.txt` and other alphabetized reference/index pages, `LANGUAGES`/`LANG_INFO` in `django/conf/locale/__init__.py`, `pyproject.toml`/`setup.cfg`/`setup.py`/requirements/`MANIFEST.in`, model field and option enumerations, backend field-type keys, `data_types` in `django/db/backends/*/base.py`, and lookup classes in `django/db/models/fields/json.py` — appending breaks the ordering readers rely on. — These are the most valuable parts for an AGENTS.md because they tell the assistant exactly which files follow the convention (settings modules, reference docs, locale registries, packaging files, backend `data_types`, field lookups) rather than relying on a general 'keep things sorted' statement.
  - Define lookup classes alphabetically and call `register_lookup()` in the same top-to-bottom order — the registration list should mirror the definitions.
  - Leave pre-existing out-of-order entries and unrelated lines alone — incidental reordering adds diff noise.
<!-- rule:4880 -->
- Order dict keys and iterations deliberately — alphabetize keys in dict/mapping literals for readability, and use `sorted(...)` (e.g., `sorted(d.items())`) whenever iteration order affects identity, hashing, equality, caching, tests, or user-visible output; rely on raw insertion order only when it is semantically intended and the project guarantees it. — Unordered dict iteration leaks nondeterminism into hashes, caches, test assertions, and rendered output — causing flaky tests and platform-dependent diffs. The rule makes ordering contracts explicit at the call site.
  Rationale: Unordered dict iteration leaks nondeterminism into hashes, caches, test assertions, and rendered output, causing flaky tests and platform-dependent diffs; explicit `sorted(...)` (or deliberate insertion order) makes the ordering contract visible at the call site.
<!-- rule:1714 -->
- Reuse or mirror existing implementations — check upstream (Django, Python stdlib) and in-repo helpers before writing new logic — When a behavior already exists upstream or elsewhere in the repo, a fresh implementation silently diverges from it, causing inconsistent results and bugs. Copying the upstream approach with a comment linking the source means fixes and behavior changes upstream can be tracked and back-ported instead of rediscovered. In-repo, reuse helpers like `get_locator` in `docs/_ext/github_links.py` rather than reimplementing `CodeLocator` construction or AST traversal — duplicated traversal logic drifts and creates two places to fix. Keep copied code close to the original so diffs against the source stay meaningful.
<!-- rule:2125 -->
- Name comprehension/generator loop variables after the element, not the source (`field`, `error`, `node`, not `attrs`, `e`, `temp`) — the variable is the item, not the collection — Reading `for e in errors` obscures what `e` is at every use site and invites the wrong name being reused for a different type elsewhere; naming it `error` keeps comprehensions self-explanatory and greppable.
<!-- rule:442 -->
- Use early-exit loops (`break`/`return`) to find first matches; express no-match fallback with `for...else`, omitting `else` when it just returns implicit `None` — Single-pass scans stay lazy and readable, and avoid sentinel boolean flags, building full candidate lists for `any()`/`all()`, and `try`/`except StopIteration` around `next(gen)` — which is hard to read when a domain-specific error must be raised on no match (e.g., a failed `isinstance` check). Add a brief comment when a `for...else` isn't obvious.
<!-- rule:4695 -->
- Use `yield from iterable` in sync generators instead of `for x in iterable: yield x` — delegates iteration cleanly and preserves laziness — These conventions keep generator delegation idiomatic and lazy while avoiding two common bugs: eager container materialization that breaks streaming, and `yield from` in async generators that fails to compile.
  - Don't wrap iterables in `iter()`, `tuple()`, or `list()` unless the function contract requires a concrete container — eager materialization defeats laziness and wastes memory
  - In `async` generators, use `for`/`async for` with `yield` — `yield from` is a `SyntaxError` in async functions
  - Confirm the value is iterable and yields the intended items before delegating — normalize single-item-or-iterable inputs rather than silently yielding a `dict`'s keys
<!-- rule:777 -->
- Order class members: attributes before `__init__`, then dunders (`__repr__` before `__str__`), then `@property`, then methods — keeps classes scannable and diffs minimal — Without this consistent member order, sibling models, views, and app configs drift into conflicting layouts and diverge from the order Django docs teach, making classes harder to scan, diffs noisier, and framework-expected structure inconsistent across the codebase.
  - Sort attributes alphabetically unless a logical grouping or framework-required order applies; use the same scheme across related classes (Django class-based views, `django/contrib/*/apps.py`) — mixed schemes make sibling files look unrelated
  - Order methods by: base-class order when overriding, helpers adjacent to the methods they support (reference/call order), otherwise alphabetical with public/primary/overridden methods before private helpers
  - In Django models use the documented order: DB fields → manager attrs (`objects = SomeManager()`) → `class Meta` → `__str__` → lifecycle methods (`save()`) → `get_absolute_url()` → custom methods — matches the layout Django docs teach readers to expect
<!-- rule:1709 -->
- Derive class names in `__repr__` and error/warning messages from `self.__class__.__qualname__` or `type(obj).__qualname__`, not hardcoded literals — subclasses (especially in base/mixin classes) must report their own name. — Hardcoded class names lie in tracebacks once a subclass inherits a `__repr__` or warning path, and `__name__` alone drops enclosing scope for nested/inner classes, making diagnostics ambiguous. Reserve literals for classes that are intentionally non-subclassable.
<!-- rule:4062 -->
- Order `__repr__` fields to match `__init__` parameter order, mirroring analogous classes in the same module or package — consistent reprs make debugging and diffing predictable. — Following it prevents leaking database credentials through reprs captured in logs and crash reports, prevents bloated or confusing debug output from internal/derived state, and prevents inconsistent field ordering that makes reprs and diffs across analogous classes unpredictable.
  For general classes, include every `__init__` parameter in `__repr__`; for Django framework/internal classes (model/admin/sql compiler internals, database backend/connection wrappers), include only primary identifiers like `model`, `model_admin`, `alias`, or `vendor` and match the repr format of analogous existing classes. Omit internal/derived state (`opts`, `lookup_opts`, `root_queryset`, full SQL/query strings) — these bloat reprs used in logs and debugging output.
  Never include sensitive config such as `settings_dict` in `__repr__` — logging and crash reporting capture repr strings and could leak database credentials.
<!-- rule:1780 -->
- Use `@property` / `@<name>.setter` decorators (not `property()` or `@abc.abstractproperty`); access properties without parens — Matches the modern Python idiom used across `django/contrib`, `django/db`, and `tests/`, keeping property definitions consistent and avoiding deprecated `@abc.abstractproperty` and verbose functional syntax.
<!-- rule:3687 -->
- [ — ]
<!-- rule:3865 -->
- Preserve spacing, underlines, margins, padding, and backgrounds when refactoring HTML tags or layout CSS (`display`, flexbox→grid) — swapping element types or display modes silently imports browser default styles, causing visual regressions unless the change is intentional and documented. — Element swaps like `<div>`→`<p>` and `display` changes inherit user-agent styles (margins, underlines) that break existing layouts; explicit overrides keep rendering identical.
<!-- rule:4346 -->
- Trace why current CSS/layout behavior occurs before patching — make the narrowest change on the affected component, since broad properties or hardcoded pixel offsets silently break unrelated layouts in shared stylesheets and the Django admin UI — Because shared stylesheets and the Django admin UI have many consumers, broad properties, hardcoded pixel offsets, shared-utility overrides, and unverified responsive states cause cross-component regressions and maintenance debt, so the rule prevents unrelated layouts and alternate UI states from silently breaking.
  - Reuse the existing alignment/spacing mechanism for component fixes — one-off pixel offsets drift as shared styles evolve and accumulate as maintenance debt
  - Scope alignment to the component instead of overriding shared utility classes — utility changes leak to every consumer, not just the one being fixed
  - Verify CSS fixes across responsive and alternate UI states — regressions surface in views the original bug didn't touch
<!-- rule:3302 -->
- Scope `:hover`/`:focus`/`:active` selectors to match their base selector (e.g. `#toolbar`) — mismatched specificity means state styles silently fail to apply. — State selectors must carry the same scope/specificity as the base rule, otherwise the state style loses the cascade and interactive feedback breaks.
<!-- rule:1880 -->
- Use the shortest unique CSS selector (`#site-name` over `#branding #site-name`, `.selector-chooseall` over `button.selector-chooseall`, `#changelist tr` over `#changelist table tbody tr`) — ancestor/element qualifiers silently break when wrapper markup or nesting changes — Django admin CSS is shared across templates (project CSS, global stylesheets, and admin static assets), so over-qualified selectors like `#branding #site-name` or `button.selector-chooseall` stop matching as soon as the surrounding markup or element types change. Shortest-unique selectors keep styling resilient to markup churn and avoid specificity inflation that makes intentional overrides harder to reason about.
  - Add those qualifiers only to prevent style leakage or reach required specificity, and simplify consistently across the whole stylesheet, including `django/contrib/admin/static/admin/css/` files like `base.css`, `changelists.css`, and `nav_sidebar.css` C mixed conventions there make overrides unpredictable
<!-- rule:2026 -->
- Delete CSS that is unused, redundant, or restates defaults; before removing a selector whose effect is uncertain, grep templates, JS, and UI components for references and confirm visual equivalence — dormant CSS that goes live again can cause color-contrast/accessibility regressions, which block merges. — Dormant CSS can be re-activated by template or JS refactors and unexpectedly apply, causing contrast/accessibility regressions that block merges.
  - Merge duplicate selectors and declarations in CSS instead of repeating them — keeps stylesheets minimal and prevents divergent edits from drifting out of sync.
<!-- rule:5501 -->
- Write JS control-flow with a space before the paren (`if (`, `for (`, `while (`), not `if(`/`for(`/`while(` — matches the style used throughout `django/contrib/admin`, `django/contrib/gis`, and `js_tests` — Keeps JS style consistent across the repo's scattered JS files and avoids noisy reformatting diffs when assistants edit existing code.
<!-- rule:3697 -->
- Keep `QuerySet` chains single-line when they fit; otherwise break inside the first call (one arg per line, trailing comma) and chain as `).annotate(` — matches `black` output, so reformatting won't churn and adding a chained call yields a one-line diff. Never use leading-dot breaks or `\` continuations. — Following this keeps QuerySet chains consistent with `black` output, preventing formatter churn and noisy multi-line diffs when a new chained call is added, and avoiding leading-dot or backslash continuations that conflict with the codebase's formatting.
<!-- rule:2704 -->
- Use `transaction.atomic()` only for real atomicity or `IntegrityError` handling; put non-DB state (counters, accumulators) and `BEGIN`-ordering backend calls before the `with transaction.atomic(...)` line, and never nest atomic blocks redundantly — wrapping read-only logic adds overhead without snapshot isolation under READ COMMITTED, and side-effecting inside the block breaks statement ordering — Under READ COMMITTED, `transaction.atomic()` around read-only code provides no snapshot isolation while still incurring BEGIN/COMMIT overhead; non-DB state and backend operations that must precede BEGIN get incorrect ordering if performed inside the block.
<!-- rule:161 -->
- Extract head/tail via unpacking: `first, *rest = seq`, `(key, value), *rest = fields` — works on any iterable without materializing a list — Indexing/slicing requires a real sequence and forces a full copy of lazy iterables (e.g. `dict.items()`, generators, `QuerySet`s), which can be expensive or impossible; unpacking and `next(iter(...))` express the same intent in O(1) memory. Use the built-in `iter(obj)` rather than `obj.__iter__()`, and when a guard already fixes which loop variables are defined, reuse them instead of tracking redundant state.
  - For a few leading items use `next(iter(seq))` (with default: `next(iter(seq), fallback)`), `it = iter(seq); next(it)` to skip, or `deque(maxlen=N)` for fixed-N — avoids `list(seq)[0]`/`[1:]` copies that break on non-sequences and duplicate memory
<!-- rule:3044 -->
- Add `__slots__` (or `@dataclass(..., slots=True)`) only to internal, frequently-instantiated classes with a fixed attribute set — public containers, proxy/handler classes, and anything meant to be monkey-patchable must stay open for arbitrary attribute attachment. — `__slots__` blocks downstream code from attaching attributes or monkey-patching, breaking third-party extensions that rely on the public surface. In Django connection handling (e.g. `django/utils/connection.py`, `django/db/backends/postgresql/base.py`), proxies and handlers must remain patchable for testing and downstream overrides — the perf gain is negligible next to the compatibility risk. Likewise, prefer bound method references over lambda wrappers in these paths; lambdas are only worth adding once a concrete reference-cycle problem is actually reproduced.
<!-- rule:4789 -->
- When migrating jQuery to native DOM APIs, replace each call with a correct native equivalent and finish the migration across the whole file: — Explains why each sub-rule prevents a concrete failure: `SyntaxError` from invalid selectors, `jQuery is not defined` after removal, mixed-API inconsistency, and obscured flow from dead shims.
  - Pass standard CSS selectors to `document.querySelector()`/`querySelectorAll()` — jQuery pseudo-selectors like `:visible`/`:hidden` are invalid there and throw `SyntaxError`; test visibility via properties such as `element.offsetParent` instead.
  - Don't wrap a DOM element in `$()` just to read native properties — use the raw element directly and the `dataset` API for `data-*` attributes. Redundant wrapping defeats the point of migrating and invites `jQuery is not defined` runtime errors once the library is removed.
  - Apply the replacement to every equivalent occurrence in the file, not just the line under review — leaving mixed jQuery/native code behind produces inconsistent behavior and keeps the jQuery dependency alive.
  - Drop jQuery-era indirection (custom-event `trigger()` patterns and their `FIXME`s) when native DOM events plus direct state/DOM updates already cover the behavior — leftover shims obscure the real data flow.
<!-- rule:565 -->
- Use `gap`/`column-gap` on flex containers for spacing, not `margin`/`padding` on children. — Spacing a child (e.g. `margin-left` on `.selector-available input`/`.selector-chosen input`) fights the parent's layout: it double-counts with the container's own alignment, leaks spacing into non-flex reflow, and breaks when items wrap. Setting `gap` on the flex container (e.g. `.selector`) keeps spacing owned by the layout and consistent whether the widget renders in Django admin, forms, or tests.
<!-- rule:4455 -->
- Keep security-sensitive code explicit, robust, and auditable — prefer inline logic over helper abstractions, handle all invocations (not just the common path), comment non-obvious invariants (e.g., intentional constant-time compares), and apply flags like `usedforsecurity=False` consistently across analogous calls — opaque, inconsistently-applied security logic hides bugs from reviewers in `django.contrib.auth`, sessions, `views.decorators`, `utils.cache`, and `core.signing` — 
  - Never replace `constant_time_compare()` with `==` or a library call whose timing properties are unverified when refactoring security-sensitive verification (password hashes, signatures, tokens) — the timing leak reveals which secret matched
  - When verifying against multiple candidate keys/secrets, choose short-circuiting vs. evaluating all comparisons deliberately and document the timing tradeoff — an undocumented early exit leaks which key matched
<!-- rule:1726 -->
- Use `collections.Counter(iterable)` for frequency/duplicate detection and `list(iterable).count(value)` for single-value counts — avoids O(n²) repeated `.count()` calls and manual accumulation loops. — Manual counting loops and repeated `.count()` calls are both slower and easier to get wrong; `Counter` and `list.count` are C-optimized and match idiomatic Python, keeping counting logic consistent across Django modules like `contrib/admin`, `core/checks`, and `tests/mail`. When you need more than one value's frequency, call `Counter` once rather than `.count()` per value to keep the algorithm linear.
<!-- rule:3849 -->
- Use built-in list methods (`remove`, `insert`, `append`) instead of index lookup and slicing — sidesteps `IndexError` and off-by-one bugs — Manual index lookup and slicing is error-prone (`IndexError`, off-by-one) and duplicates elements when a move target is already present.
    - To move an element to the front, `remove` it if present then `insert(0, x)`; inserting only when absent duplicates entries
    - To replace an element when order doesn't matter, `remove` the old value before `append`ing the new one
<!-- rule:5315 -->
- Prefer `reversed(seq)` to `seq[::-1]` when iterating in reverse — slicing copies the whole sequence, so iteration wastes memory — Using `seq[::-1]` allocates a complete reversed copy before iteration, which wastes memory and can cause performance regressions or out-of-memory failures on large sequences, whereas `reversed(seq)` iterates lazily without copying.
<!-- rule:5233 -->
- Use `max()` instead of manual conditionals or stateful flags to compute maxima — fewer branches, no stale-flag bugs — Manual comparison chains and flag variables drift out of sync and recompute only the current source, silently dropping the running maximum accumulated in earlier iterations or branches. `max(acc, *new_values)` makes the accumulator explicit so the result is correct by construction. Guarding empty iterables with a truthiness check prevents `ValueError` from `max()` on an empty sequence, and passing generators directly avoids the cost of unpacking them into a tuple when no initial accumulator is needed.
  - Thread accumulators through `max(acc, *new_values)` instead of recomputing from the current source — keeps the running max correct across iterations and branches
  - Guard `max()` with a truthiness check on the iterable, and pass generators directly rather than `*`-unpacking when you have no accumulator — avoids `ValueError` on empty input and avoids materializing sequences
<!-- rule:3413 -->
- Define classes at module level, not nested inside functions or methods — hot paths avoid re-creating the class object on every call, and tests can patch it by qualname. — Module-level class definitions avoid per-call class-creation overhead in frequently invoked code and keep classes reachable for tooling like `monkeypatch`. Using closures for enclosing-scope parameters keeps that behavior without the cost of a factory function recreating the class.
  - When a nested class needs parameters from an enclosing scope, use a closure that defines the class inside the function rather than a factory function that rebuilds the class each call.
<!-- rule:4922 -->
- Use explicit ASCII classes in regexes (`[a-zA-Z0-9_-]` not `\w`, `[0-9]` not `\d`) — Python 3's `\w`/`\d` are Unicode-aware and will match non-ASCII tokens in URL routes, version numbers, and validators like `django/core/validators.py`. — Python 3's `\w`/`\d` match Unicode letters and digits, so protocol-defined ASCII tokens (URL path params, version numbers, validator inputs in `django/core/validators.py`) would incorrectly accept non-ASCII characters. Also skip `\d` when the class already contains `\w`, since it's redundant.
<!-- rule:6174 -->
- Prefer `re.findall`/`re.finditer`/`re.search` over manual per-character or repeated-match loops — the C-backed builtins are faster, avoid off-by-one and overlap bugs, and signal intent. Use `re.finditer` for non-overlapping iteration, and add a brief comment above non-obvious regex loops. — Hand-rolled scanning loops regress on performance and commonly mishandle edge cases (overlapping matches, bounds); documenting non-obvious patterns keeps the intent reviewable.
<!-- rule:384 -->
- Keep `em` for cascade-relative sizing (icons beside text, nested `font-size`) and `rem` for root-relative dimensions — swapping them breaks elements that rely on inherited `font-size`. Convert px to exact fractional `rem` (e.g. `0.875rem`), never round — rounding compounds drift across `base.css` and template inline CSS. — In `django/contrib/admin/static/admin/css/base.css` and inline CSS like `default_urlconf.html`, `em` and `rem` are not interchangeable: `em` resolves against the inherited `font-size` while `rem` resolves against the root. Using the wrong one silently breaks icons or nested `font-size` declarations whose computed size depends on the cascade, and rounding px→rem introduces small offsets that accumulate across stylesheets.
<!-- rule:5489 -->
- Use unitless `0` and drop redundant trailing zeros in CSS — `padding: 0`, `margin: 0`, `1rem`, `0.5rem` (never `0px`, `none`, `1.0rem`). — Keeps numeric values consistent across global stylesheets, including Django admin CSS, and avoids linter/prettier churn on every edit.
<!-- rule:1389 -->
- Use `<th scope="col">`/`<th scope="row">` only for real headers, `<td>` for data (e.g. line numbers) — screen readers can't associate headers without `scope`, and putting `scope` on `<tr>`/`<td>` is invalid. — Enables assistive tech to link each data cell to its header; misuse breaks that association and fails HTML validation.
<!-- rule:1444 -->
- Use `<p>` only for paragraph text; use `<div>` for layout wrappers and place block elements like `<pre>` as siblings — invalid block-in-inline nesting triggers HTML parser error recovery, which reorders the DOM and breaks rendering across `django/contrib/admin/templates/admin/` and `django/views/templates/`. — Invalid block-in-inline nesting triggers parser error recovery, which can reorder or drop elements in the DOM.
<!-- rule:3519 -->
- Use exact hyphenated ARIA/HTML attribute names (`aria-current`, `aria-describedby`, `role`) with a single space between attributes — typos like `aira-*` or run-together names (`role="button"href=`) silently break accessibility and parsing — Misspelled or concatenated attributes are ignored by browsers and screen readers, so the intended accessibility semantics disappear without any error — the element renders fine but is broken for assistive tech.
<!-- rule:386 -->
- Prefer `for...of` (values) and `array.entries()` destructuring (index + value) over C-style `for` loops; reserve index loops for cases needing explicit counter control or mutation-during-iteration — avoids off-by-one bugs and index bookkeeping. — Manual `i < len` bookkeeping is the usual source of off-by-one and stale-index bugs; `for...of`/`entries()` express intent directly and stay correct when collections change shape. Fall back to a C-style `for` only when you genuinely need the counter, indexed access, or in-loop mutation semantics (e.g. skipping elements while iterating).
<!-- rule:6399 -->
- Omit trailing delimiters (`;`, MySQL `//`, Oracle `/`) in embedded SQL — only needed when concatenating multiple statements for a client to run together — Single statements passed to `cursor.execute()` or defined in provider `features.py` don't need terminators; stray delimiters are ignored or break execution, and are only meaningful as separators between concatenated statements.
<!-- rule:5321 -->
- `INFO` for user-facing operations (`creating table`, `altering column`); `DEBUG` for internal bookkeeping, state-change details, raw SQL, and parameters — users see the operation without DEBUG noise. — These levels are the public contract for anyone configuring logging in `django/db/backends/` and `django/db/migrations/`. Emitting internal bookkeeping or raw SQL at `INFO` floods normal output; dropping the alias prefix makes queries unattributable when several databases are configured; and mirroring a caller's `INFO` message at the same level produces duplicated lines that can't be silenced separately.
  - Prefix raw SQL at `DEBUG` with the database alias in brackets (e.g. `[default] SELECT ...`) — multi-database setups need to attribute queries to a connection.
  - If a higher-level caller already logs the event at `INFO`, log the lower-level duplicate at `DEBUG` — avoids duplicate INFO lines while keeping the detail independently filterable.
<!-- rule:2006 -->
- Audit regexes touching user input for ReDoS before merge; prefer string ops/parsers over tweaking a flagged pattern — Without this, a regex applied to user input can catastrophically backtrack on crafted input and stall the worker, while tweaking a flagged pattern often just moves the blowup instead of eliminating it, so non-regex string operations or parsers are the reliable fix.
<!-- rule:5173 -->
- Route all Django relation resolution through `resolve_model_field_relations()` / `resolve_relation` (django/db/migrations/, django/db/models/sql/, django/db/models/fields/) — don't open-code remote/through model-key resolution, proxy-to-concrete mapping, or `_relations` mutation at `add_field`/`remove_field`/`alter_field` call sites. — These call sites must stay in lockstep: when resolution drifts between migration-state and compiler paths, fields silently point at the wrong model. The helper owns its own `real_apps`/`concretes` setup and takes the related model as an arg, so callers should pass the model in and consume the return value — leaving it unused skips the resolved relation entirely.
<!-- rule:1156 -->
- Define one module-level named logger beneath imports; log through it, not `logging.error()`. — Root-logger calls bypass Django's documented logger hierarchy (e.g. `django.db.backends`, `django.management`, `django.contrib.sessions`), so `LOGGING` config filters and handlers silently miss the records.
<!-- rule:1773 -->
- Prefer `asyncio.TaskGroup` over manual task-list tracking plus exception suppression — structured concurrency guarantees all tasks are awaited or cancelled, avoiding orphaned tasks and swallowed exceptions. — These are behavioral conventions for async concurrency helpers used across modules like `django/core/handlers/asgi.py` and `django/dispatch/dispatcher.py`, so they apply globally rather than to any single file.
  - Don't keep a separate collection of created `Task` objects; collect results via wrapper coroutines or `task.result()` — prevents reference cycles through exception tracebacks.
  - To stop one task inside a `TaskGroup`, call `task.cancel()` directly instead of raising a sentinel exception — sentinels tear down the entire group when only one task needs to end.
<!-- rule:728 -->
- Cache post-init values once; build module globals eagerly at import, instance caches in `__init__` or via `@cached_property` — lazy `if X is None` globals add a concurrency race window with no memory or startup benefit, since the value lives in non-collectable module memory anyway. Precompute fallbacks (`self._context`) so per-call methods like `get_context()`, `value_from_datadict()`, and `to_python()` don't rebuild them; if several accessors repeat the same fallback resolution, extract a shared private helper instead of duplicating it. — Django fields, widgets, and storage backends recreate the same fallback objects on every access — Recomputing values that are fixed after startup or initialization in Django form/model fields wastes work and can produce inconsistent behavior: lazily initializing a module-level global behind an `if X is None` guard leaves a race window under concurrent access, while rebuilding instance values in per-call methods duplicates fallback resolution that can drift. Eager module-level caching and instance precomputation in `__init__` or `@cached_property` prevent duplicate initialization, keep fallback results consistent, and avoid repeated work on every access or request.
<!-- rule:2329 -->
- Trade readability, consistency, or a single uniform code path for a perf optimization only with benchmark/disassembly evidence; keep the more verbose form when simplification measurably regresses perf and record why (e.g., `loops over fields twice`) — prevents unmeasured complexity and silently re-introduces regressions. — Bidirectional guard: on one side, unmeasured micro-optimizations add complexity for imaginary wins; on the other, documenting the reason for a verbose form (with a concrete note like `loops over fields twice`) stops future maintainers from 'simplifying' away a measured performance gain.
<!-- rule:3733 -->
- Provide a visible keyboard focus state for interactive controls — Keyboard-only users can't see which control is focused if you strip `outline` without a replacement `:focus` style. <!-- rule:1392 -->
- In CSS, use one space around combinators (`>`, `+`, `~`), one space after property colons (`vertical-align: middle;`), and one space before `{` (`selector {`). — Consistent minimal whitespace across the Django admin and docs theme CSS keeps diffs clean and avoids style churn when these files are edited side by side.
<!-- rule:5502 -->
- Brace all `if`/`else`/loop bodies, even single statements; write `} else {` on the same line — required by ESLint's `curly` and keeps JS control flow unambiguous — Matches ESLint `curly`, which forces braces anyway, so unbraced single-statement bodies in any JavaScript file would fail lint; the `} else {` placement keeps diffs minimal when bodies grow to multiple statements.
<!-- rule:814 -->
- Declare list-valued options in `django/contrib/admin` (`list_display`, `filter_vertical`, `filter_horizontal`, admin `fields`) and `Index.fields` as list literals — `["field"]`, no trailing comma on one-liners — never tuples: the surrounding APIs and tests (`tests/modeladmin/`, `tests/admin_inlines/`, `tests/invalid_models_tests/`) validate list-only contracts, so a tuple silently fails them. — Django's admin and index options are semantically lists: they get appended to, validated as lists, and asserted as lists throughout `tests/modeladmin/`, `tests/admin_inlines/`, and `tests/invalid_models_tests/`. Writing a tuple instead breaks list-only contracts and produces confusing failures, while single-line trailing commas are noise. Conversely, `ModelAdmin.inlines` and `actions` are fixed collection defaults whose identity as tuples matters for consistency; mixing tuple/list forms across the two analogous class attributes makes the defaults look accidentally different.

<!-- rule:1041 -->
- Prefer event delegation (`$(document).on(event, selector, handler)`) or a single shared named handler over per-element anonymous callbacks and `.each()` attach loops — keeps dynamically inserted elements wired up and stops handler count from scaling with row count. — Per-element anonymous callbacks and `.each()` loops duplicate handlers for every row and silently break for elements inserted after load, so delegation/shared handlers are the durable pattern. <!-- rule:1536 -->
- Wrap lazy init in a named helper/property that returns and caches the value — set the backing attribute on every path, including fallbacks, so repeat calls don't recompute or re-raise. — Scattered `if not initialized` guards duplicate the work and risk leaving the cache unset on some paths, forcing expensive recomputation or re-triggering the same failure on every call.
<!-- rule:2472 -->
- Scope DB queries to the branch that needs them; hoist or batch repeated query-backed calls out of loops — Keeps lazy `QuerySet` evaluation from firing redundant DB hits and scopes them to the code path that needs the data. Confirm the call actually executes a query before hoisting — if it returns a cached value, moving it out changes semantics without any benefit.
<!-- rule:3227 -->
- Isolate debug instrumentation from production paths — puts debug metadata in sidecar/wrapper objects (`django/views/debug.py`, `django/template/base.py`) instead of mutating core framework objects, and keeps built-in debug logs (e.g. `django/tasks/signal_handlers.py`) to essential fields only — prevents overhead and debug-driven refactors in non-debug execution paths
<!-- rule:1422 -->
- Keep reusable templates/widgets context-agnostic — use semantic HTML internally, no presentational wrappers or styling classes — Wrapper markup baked into a template collides with surrounding layouts when the widget is embedded elsewhere; moving page- or context-specific containers to the parent include/composition site keeps the partial reusable across call sites.
<!-- rule:5138 -->
- Query with plain `document.querySelector()` and explicit fallbacks/comments for structural variants — compound selectors and manual element loops obscure which DOM shape is expected — Keeping DOM access to direct native APIs makes selector intent readable and prevents regressions from over-broad selectors or tag comparisons that miss because of case handling.

<!-- rule:1393 -->
- In `.css` files: put `}` on its own line at the selector's indentation, start top-level selectors at column 0 (indent only inside `@media`-style nesting), and end every declaration with `;` — including the last — since a missing final `;` silently swallows the next property when one is appended, and unaligned braces make edits diff noisily across Django's admin/doc stylesheets. — A missing semicolon on the last declaration isn't a syntax error — the following line gets absorbed into the previous value, so the bug shows up far from the edit. Keeping top-level selectors at column 0 and braces aligned with selectors makes nested vs. top-level rules visually distinguishable and keeps diffs confined to the lines actually changed.
<!-- rule:1394 -->
- Use exactly one blank line between CSS rule sets; group with comments, not whitespace — prevents noisy diffs across CSS files — Ad-hoc extra blank lines and whitespace-based grouping make CSS diffs noisy and produce merge conflicts unrelated to real style changes, so requiring exactly one blank line and comments for grouping keeps changes minimal and reviewable across files.
<!-- rule:2429 -->
- Write RFC 5322-valid raw email strings: `Name: value` with no space before `:`, headers at column 0, and a truly empty line before the body — `django/core/mail/message.py` and `tests/mail/tests.py` fixtures treat malformed header lines as body text, silently corrupting serialized messages; build triple-quoted messages with `textwrap.dedent()`. — Stray spaces or tabs around header separators, or a whitespace-only line where the header/body boundary belongs, cause the parser to fold those lines into the body, so fixtures and constructed messages no longer match what real mail servers expect. <!-- rule:4757 -->
- Write attribute access with no space around the dot: `obj.attribute`, never `obj. attribute` — Whitespace after the dot is a `SyntaxError` in Python, and in `docs` code examples it teaches users broken syntax; keep it consistent with patterns like `self._meta_ordering`.
<!-- rule:4822 -->
- Format YAML with two-space indentation for nested mappings and sequences, and match blank-line and trailing-whitespace style to the surrounding file — Prettier-compatible output keeps automated tooling and manual edits from churning diffs. — Prettier-compatible formatting keeps automated tooling output matching manual edits, preventing noise diffs.
<!-- rule:4835 -->
- End every compound statement header (`def`, `class`, `if`, `for`, `while`, `with`, `try`, `except`, `else`) with `:` — Omitting the colon is a hard `SyntaxError` in Python, not a style nit — the file fails to import at all, so nothing downstream (tests, migrations, imports) can run.
<!-- rule:6022 -->
- Terminate JavaScript statements with semicolons, including `return` statements — ASI silently changes semantics (a `return` on its own line yields `undefined`) and the repo's JS under `django/contrib/admin/static/admin/js/` uses explicit semicolons throughout
<!-- rule:5940 -->
- Scope form-specific DOM queries to the form element and gate them behind the same existence check — avoids null-reference errors and cross-form leakage when the form is absent or duplicated — Scoping form-specific queries to the form and gating them behind the same existence check prevents null-reference errors and broken initialization when the form is absent, cross-form leakage when forms are duplicated, and unnecessary or unsafe resolution of descendant input/select/textarea attributes that are never consumed.

<!-- rule:4612 -->
- Prefer `data-*` attributes on existing semantic elements for JS hooks; use `json_script`/`JSON` `<script>` with a stable id when no element fits — avoids pointless `<div>` wrappers that bloat markup and break semantic structure — Server data and behavior hooks belong on elements that already exist in the semantic structure. When there is genuinely no suitable element (e.g. a JSON blob for JS to read), a `json_script` / `<script type="application/json">` tag with a stable id is the idiomatic mechanism and avoids wrap-and-hide patterns. Apply this across Django templates (e.g. admin `change_form.html`, `filter.html`, `change_list.html`) and front-end JS examples like `docs/howto/javascript.txt`.
<!-- rule:5343 -->
- In Django/Jinja2 template loops, match the control structure to the data: reference the loop variable (e.g. `frame.lineno`) for per-item values, use `{% for %}` only when emitting one row per item, use `{% if <collection> %}` when the body emits the collection itself, and guard single trailing content with `forloop.last` — Emitting aggregated collections inside `{% for %}` duplicates rows, and unguarded trailing content repeats per iteration — matching the block type to the data keeps template output aligned with the structure it renders.
<!-- rule:5348 -->
- Apply conditional CSS classes concisely: in DOM code use `classList.toggle(className, force)`; in templates emit one `class` attribute with conditionally included whole class names. — Duplicated `class` attributes or concatenated name fragments drift out of sync and make grepping for a class name unreliable; keeping full names intact in a single attribute keeps templates and JS toggleable and searchable.
<!-- rule:1882 -->
- Verify old CSS selectors still exist in templates before removing them — e.g. check `ul.inline`/`p.datetime` emitters in `django/views/templates/technical_500.html` before editing `django/contrib/admin/static/admin/css/widgets.css` or `forms.css`. When changing a selector (e.g. `ul.inline` → `div.inline`), keep the old selector in the comma-separated list until markup migration lands — dropping it early breaks styling on un-migrated templates. Preserve existing declarations (`font-size`, `font-weight`, etc.) instead of silently dropping them — unintended overrides cascade across shared admin stylesheets. — CSS selectors in shared admin stylesheets are coupled to the templates that emit the corresponding markup; removing a selector that is still emitted, or altering declarations without need, silently breaks styling for templates that have not migrated yet.
<!-- rule:6069 -->
- Use `cls.__mro__`/`cls.__bases__` with `getattr` for MRO traversal, not `inspect.getmro` — avoids import overhead and is the native Python idiom — In the Django codebase, resolving class attributes and inherited members is a hot path in modules like `django/template/utils.py`, `django/db/models/manager.py`, and `django/db/models/fields/related.py`; `inspect.getmro` adds unnecessary complexity over the native dunder attributes that Python already exposes on every class.
<!-- rule:1252 -->
- Interpret GitHub review suggestions literally — An empty suggestion block means delete the highlighted code, a `#optional` prefix marks non-blocking feedback, and anything the reviewer leaves to the author's discretion must not be enforced — misreading these silently changes code the reviewer never asked to change.
<!-- rule:3436 -->
- Format multi-line SQL by splitting only at token boundaries outside string literals, and align each `FROM`/`JOIN` with its `SELECT` (including in subqueries) — Keeps embedded SQL syntactically valid — a newline inside a quoted literal silently changes the query — and readable for reviewers of Oracle/Django backends and tests.
<!-- rule:1048 -->
- Transform the raw rule into a concise, actionable guideline for an AGENTS.md file. — This rule covers frontend visibility handling across `django/contrib/admin/static/admin/js` and `.../css`. Rephrased into bullets so AI assistants keep CSS as the source of truth for visibility, avoid JS/CSS drift, and don't pollute production styles for test convenience.
<!-- rule:5031 -->
- Use `dict.keys()` for set operations (`|`, `&`, `-`) instead of merging dictionaries with `|` — set-like views avoid the copy; for in-place `|=` on keys, wrap as `set(d.keys())` when Python 2 must be supported, since `keys()` returns a plain list there and in-place set ops would fail. — In Python 3, `dict.keys()` returns a set-like view that supports `|`, `&`, `-`, and `|=` directly without materializing a new dictionary. Merging dicts with `|` is a different operation (combining values) and creates a copy. On Python 2, `keys()` returns a list, so `|=` is not a set union — `set(d.keys())` is required to get correct set semantics.
<!-- rule:3255 -->
- Use `concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor` instead of raw `threading.Thread`, and default workers to a multiple of `os.cpu_count()` (e.g. `2 *` or `5 *`), not a hard-coded `10` — pools handle thread lifecycle and shutdown, and deriving the limit lets concurrency scale with the host rather than bottlenecking on large machines or oversubscribing small ones — Pools handle thread lifecycle and shutdown; deriving the limit from `os.cpu_count()` scales with the host instead of bottlenecking large machines or oversubscribing small ones.
<!-- rule:4815 -->
- Unpack DB-API cursor rows directly in the `for` loop (`for key, value, expires in cursor.fetchall():`) instead of indexing an intermediate `rows` variable — keeps the `SELECT` column order and the unpack in sync so adding or reordering columns can't silently shift indices; omit any column the method doesn't consume from both the `SELECT` list and the unpack so the query stays minimal. — Prevents silent index drift between the `SELECT` list and row access, and keeps queries minimal.
<!-- rule:5333 -->
- Pass `widgets` to `MultiWidget` as a list (`[TextInput(), ...]`), not a tuple — keep it mutable — `MultiWidget` and its subclasses treat `self.widgets` as a mutable sequence (indexing, replacement, appending); a tuple silently survives construction but breaks with `AttributeError`/`TypeError` the moment a subclass or fixture mutates it — often only surfaced in tests.
<!-- rule:634 -->
- Build binary payloads with `b"".join([...])`, one element per boundary, header, body, and terminator — implicit adjacent string-literal concatenation silently merges intended items and drops the `\r\n` delimiters that separate them (see `django/utils/text.py`). — Keeps each logical field visible as its own list element so delimiters are never lost; Python's implicit string-literal concatenation hides the missing `\r\n` until a downstream parser fails. Short fixed test payloads under `tests/requests/` are the exception — a single literal with explicit `\r\n` escapes reads more clearly than a join.
<!-- rule:4255 -->
- Unescape regex-escaped literals (`\.` → `.`, `\/` → `/`) in display/simplification route representations (`django/contrib/admindocs`, `django/urls/resolvers.py`, `django/core/management/commands/listurls.py`) — shown paths must match real URLs and prefix filtering must work — These modules present or filter routes for humans and for prefix matching, so any regex-only syntax (escaped literals, anchors, named-group markers) leaks into displayed URLs and breaks string-based prefix matching. Normalizing representations keeps `listurls`/admindocs output faithful to real URLs and makes non-regex `path()` routes safe to consume.
  - Keep anchors and group fragments (`^`, `$`, `(?P<`) out of route strings that are not regex patterns; warn when they appear in `path()` routes — leaks regex syntax into user-facing output and breaks non-regex consumers
<!-- rule:1788 -->
- Keep async/ASGI paths non-blocking — never `time.sleep()` or wait on `asyncio.wait_for(..., timeout=N)` for rate limits, delayed failures, or rare-condition checks — Blocking calls stall the event loop and serialize unrelated requests; surface these conditions via a dedicated exception type or `Queue.get_nowait()` so the happy path returns immediately.
<!-- rule:706 -->
- Use `\s+` (not `\s`) when splitting on whitespace, and `[ \t]` (not `\s`) for intra-line whitespace in multiline regexes — `\s` swallows newlines, corrupting line-oriented matches — Used in Django's regex handling (e.g. `django/contrib/staticfiles/storage.py`, `django/utils/text.py`); mirroring the split/line-trailing conventions there keeps whitespace handling consistent and avoids newline consumption and trailing-whitespace pollution in captured values.
  - Capture with `.*?` and place trailing `[ \t]*$` outside the capture group — keeps trailing whitespace out of captured values
<!-- rule:5393 -->
- **URL regex quantifiers — bound for production, permissive for tests:** — Production regexes run against untrusted input, so unbounded quantifiers are a DoS vector; test regexes only need to route fixtures, so length bounds there just duplicate the token generator and break silently when it changes.
  - In production validators (`django/core/validators.py`), bound components that match user-influenced input with explicit quantifiers (e.g. port as `\d{2,5}`) instead of unbounded `\d+` — unbounded `\d+` on long adversarial input triggers catastrophic backtracking (ReDoS).
