<!-- rule:3835 -->
- Apply the same token pattern to sibling capture groups and update every backreference when renaming — inconsistent groups or stale `\1`/`(?P<name>)`/`(?P=name)` refs silently match the wrong text — Regexes with mismatched group patterns or dangling backreferences still compile and run, so the bug only surfaces as incorrect matches at runtime.
<!-- rule:479 -->
- Use raw strings or escape backslashes in Windows path literals; preserve `.\(` prefixes in `makemessages` paths — Prevents `unicodeescape` syntax errors and gettext path breakage
<!-- rule:3377 -->
- Put a space after comment delimiters (`// Comment`, `/* Comment */`, `# Comment`) — keeps comments readable and diffs clean. — AI-generated comments often run the delimiter into the text (`//Comment`), which reads as noise and inconsistent with the surrounding codebase. The convention applies across all languages and files (Django Python and JS both follow it), so it's surfaced globally.
<!-- rule:722 -->
- Include both the cutoff and omitted size in truncation notices, e.g. `trimmed to {cutoff} bytes, {omitted} bytes not shown` — readers need to know how much was dropped, not just that it was trimmed. — A notice that only reports the cutoff hides the severity of what was dropped, making debug output misleading when the omitted portion is large.
<!-- rule:5089 -->
- Extract duplicated content-type init (parsing `CONTENT_TYPE` into `content_type`/`content_params`, `cgi.parse_header` charset validation) from ASGI/WSGI request classes into a shared protected helper `_parse_content_type_params(meta)` on `HttpRequest` — one implementation keeps ASGI/WSGI behavior in sync — ASGI and WSGI reimplementing the same parsing drifts apart; a single helper on `HttpRequest` in `django/http/request.py` is the source of truth.
<!-- rule:2963 -->
- Fold syntax variants into one regex (extend the pattern or add an optional `(?:...)` non-capturing group) instead of a near-duplicate regex tuple or parallel match branch — duplicated patterns drift out of sync silently; keep a separate pattern only when the original non-match behavior must be preserved — Consolidating variants avoids drift between near-duplicate regexes and repeated match branches, which can diverge over time and produce inconsistent parsing. Preserving the original behavior on non-match is the one case where an independent pattern is safer.
<!-- rule:202 -->
- Preserve intentionally separated defensive and type-conversion guards in Django internals — merging them with `and` silently changes edge-case behavior even when the merge looks equivalent. — 
  - Keep separate `None` guards and type-conversion branches in DB backend field converters (`django/db/backends/*/operations.py`, `django/db/backends/*/base.py`) — the split handles distinct cases (missing value vs. unexpected type) that a combined condition drops.
  - When registering a dummy/no-op override to skip a conversion, document the avoided chain (e.g. `decode` → `dumps` → `loads`) and the condition under which the override matters — otherwise it reads as dead code and gets deleted.
  - Before removing a guard in `django/forms/widgets.py` (e.g. the `try`/`getattr` workaround in `is_initial()`, type checks feeding `AdminFileWidget` output), confirm existing tests still pass — these guards cover historical regressions.
  - Annotate Python 2 compatibility code with its removal condition so cleanup is deliberate instead of accidental.
<!-- rule:4155 -->
- Lead success/confirmation templates with the congratulatory phrase and bold the main text — keeps confirmations prominent and visually consistent — Users should see confirmation of success immediately and consistently across all templates, so the primary message stays the focal point rather than getting buried among other layout text.
<!-- rule:6226 -->
- Indent `static/**/css/` and `static/**/js/` assets with 4 spaces and commit them uncompiled — commit the source, not generated/minified output — Committed minified bundles are unreadable in diffs, conflict on every rebuild, and hide the actual source of truth. Keeping these files in their authored form under `static/**/css/` and `static/**/js/` means reviewers see real changes and the repo stays consistent across app-local static dirs (e.g. `django/contrib/gis/static/`) as well as root `static/`.
<!-- rule:6095 -->
- Call date/time helpers with the context value explicitly; omit args restating the default format constant (`date_format(value)`, not `date_format(value, DATE_FORMAT)`). Mirror this in tests (`tests/i18n/tests.py`). — The default format constant in `django/utils/formats.py` and `django/utils/http.py` is the contract — passing it back in restates the signature and hides when a call site actually overrides formatting. Tests that spell out the default stop catching regressions the moment that constant's value changes, so they should exercise the same default-based call pattern as production code. The timestamp/value itself stays explicit because it is call-specific context the helper can't infer.
<!-- rule:4396 -->
- Guard a template section's heading (or container) with the same display-limit/visibility conditional that controls its content — prevents orphaned or empty headings from rendering. — When a heading's visibility diverges from its content's (e.g., a display-limit or truncation guard), users see a title with nothing under it. The Django admin delete-confirmation template is the concrete motivation: the 'Objects' heading must be tied to its limit, related objects without a delete action should list in full, and both limit cases need coverage so regressions surface.
  - In `admin/delete_confirmation.html`, render the 'Objects' heading only when the limit > 0, show related-object lists in full when they include no destructive action (e.g., no delete button), and test both zero and non-zero limits — mismatch between heading visibility and content visibility leaves users with a title and no body.
<!-- rule:5835 -->
- Use `<div>` wrappers, not `<ul>`/`<li>`, for per-option and option-group markup in multi-input choice widget templates (`django/forms/**/widgets/multiple_input.html`) and their rendering tests (`tests/forms_tests/widget_tests/test_radioselect.py`, sibling `test_*.py`) — template output and test assertions must match exactly, so changing one alone breaks the test suite. — - Wrap each option group's label and options together in a single group container `<div>` — when the `<ul>`/`<li>` structure that encoded the group boundary is removed, the container is the only thing preserving which options belong to which label, and the rendering tests assert on that nesting.
<!-- rule:2568 -->
- Comma-separate every identifier in JS `/*global ... */` comments — linters treat the list as comma-delimited — Linters parse the `/*global ... */` directive as a comma-delimited list; missing or dropped commas cause identifiers to be misread (e.g. `a b` becomes one global), producing false `no-undef` errors and unwarranted warnings when appending or editing the list.
<!-- rule:3031 -->
- Avoid `static` class fields (`static foo = ...`) in ES6-targeted browser JS; use `static` methods or assign props after the class declaration instead — static fields are ES2022-only and throw syntax errors in ES6 engines (see `django/contrib/gis/static/gis/js/OLMapWidget.js`). — ES6 target browsers and tooling do not support `static` class field syntax (standardized only in ES2022), so declaring `static foo = "bar"` in shipped client-side JavaScript (e.g. `django/contrib/gis/static/gis/js/OLMapWidget.js`) causes parse errors before any code runs; static methods and post-declaration assignments achieve the same result on all ES6 engines.
<!-- rule:5733 -->
- Use `parseInt(value, 10)` instead of bitwise coercions like `~~value` — explicit parsing states intent and avoids 32-bit truncation bugs — `~~value` silently truncates to 32 bits, so large or out-of-range numbers produce wrong results, and readers can't tell whether the coercion was intentional. `parseInt(value, 10)` makes the conversion and radix explicit.
<!-- rule:4437 -->
- Use `background-position-x`/`background-position-y` when adjusting one axis — the shorthand `background-position` resets the other axis and breaks RTL overrides — In RTL-aware CSS, base stylesheets set the direction-independent axis (e.g., `background-position-y`) and RTL overrides target only the direction-dependent one (e.g., `background-position-x`). Using the shorthand in either place clobbers the other axis, silently undoing the RTL adjustment.
<!-- rule:3981 -->
- Use `Match.end()` directly as the next search start when chaining regex calls (e.g., to find a closing delimiter) — spans are start-inclusive/end-exclusive, so adding `1` skips a character — `re.Match` spans are end-exclusive, so `Match.end()` already points just past the match; offsetting by `+1` silently skips a character and breaks chained delimiter/scan logic.
<!-- rule:3355 -->
- Delete stale GEOS/GIS artifacts instead of leaving references — drop unsupported platforms (e.g. Solaris) from GIS docs and unreferenced prototypes like `create_cs` in `django/contrib/gis/geos/prototypes/coordseq.py`; outdated entries mislead contributors and obscure the real supported API surface. — Stale platform notes and unused prototypes (e.g. `create_cs`) accumulate in `django/contrib/gis/geos/prototypes/` and the GIS docs, making it hard to tell which GEOS APIs and platforms are actually supported.
<!-- rule:6323 -->
- Deriving `Context(context)`: set only new or overridden keys — re-adding inherited ones (`opts`, `preserved_filters`, `original`, `is_popup`) duplicates state that silently drifts from the source context. If a conditional guard (e.g. `is not None`) looks necessary, document why the inherited value would differ. — Copying inherited attributes like `opts`, `preserved_filters`, `original`, and `is_popup` into a derived `Context` creates a second, independent copy of state that will silently diverge from the source context the moment either is mutated, so bugs surface later as stale options, wrong filters, or incorrect popup/original detection rather than as an immediate error.
<!-- rule:6261 -->
- Use `cannot be applied`/`not_applicable`, not `inapplicable`, for excluded items — the latter is ambiguous with "irrelevant" or "inappropriate". — Readers of comments, identifiers, and messages can misread `inapplicable` as a judgment about relevance rather than a statement that the item doesn't apply, so explicit inability wording keeps the exclusion reason unambiguous.
<!-- rule:1266 -->
- Use `100vh` (not `100%`) for full-viewport layouts — Percentage heights resolve against the parent's height, which collapses to `auto` when no ancestor sets an explicit height — so full-height admin/dashboard shells silently shrink to content height. Viewport units are independent of ancestor sizing and always match the visible viewport.
<!-- rule:1267 -->
- Avoid `width: 100%` on elements with margins/padding/borders unless `box-sizing: border-box` — prefer `width: auto` — With the default `content-box` model, percentage widths add to padding/border, so the element overflows its container and breaks layouts in any stylesheet.
<!-- rule:3939 -->
- Apply hover styles to the hovered container (`th`), not just nested links (`a`) — partial styling leaves text unreadable in the container's padding/border regions. — When only the inner descendant gets the hover color, the container's padding and border areas keep the default foreground, producing unreadable text across part of the hover target.
<!-- rule:2734 -->
- Admin UI choice/group section headers must be visually distinguished from surrounding body content with a strong bottom border and a distinct background colour; retain this treatment when adjusting header styling. — Without a strong bottom border and distinct background, section headers blend into adjacent body content and users lose the visual grouping of choice/group options. Preserving this treatment through styling changes keeps the admin UI's section hierarchy legible instead of silently regressing to flat, undifferentiated blocks.
<!-- rule:6059 -->
- Hide sidebars/filters with `visibility: hidden` on the container instead of toggling `tabIndex` per descendant — CSS already removes them from tab order, so tabIndex helpers are redundant. — `visibility: hidden` on a container implicitly removes it and all descendants from the tab order, so maintaining separate `tabIndex` enable/disable helpers duplicates logic and risks drift (e.g., a descendant left focusable after hide, or unfocusable after show).
<!-- rule:6240 -->
- Indent `case`/`default` one level inside `switch` and enforce with ESLint `indent` + `SwitchCase: 1` — keeps switch bodies visually nested under the condition, matching editor/Prettier defaults. — Without `SwitchCase: 1`, `case` labels sit flush with `switch`, making it harder to see which statements belong to which branch and causing churn when editors or Prettier reformat the same file differently.
<!-- rule:5694 -->
- Never derive localized abbreviations by slicing translated strings (`getFullMonthName().substring(0, 3)`) — consume dedicated abbreviation data like `CalendarNamespace.monthOfYearAbbrev`, since abbreviations are not the first N characters of the full word in most languages/locales. — Truncating a translated full name yields the correct abbreviation only in a handful of locales; in most, the abbreviated form is a distinct string, so slicing produces visibly wrong UI text for non-English users.
<!-- rule:2079 -->
- Format GitHub Actions expressions as `${{ value }}` with a space inside both braces, not `${{value}}` — matches GitHub's own docs and keeps workflow diffs consistent — GitHub parses both forms identically, so this is purely about consistency: matching the spacing GitHub uses in its documentation and `actions/runner` examples means contributors copy-paste correctly, and uniform spacing prevents noisy diffs in `.github/workflows/*.yml` and composite `action.yml` files when expressions are added or edited. This only affects GitHub Actions expressions, so place it in `.github/AGENTS.md` rather than the root file — a repo-wide rule would wrongly imply the convention applies to code outside the `.github/` tree, where `${{ }}` syntax never appears.
<!-- rule:310 -->
- Preserve the existing `msgid` and placeholder style (`%s` vs `%(name)s`) when editing `gettext`/`ngettext` strings — changing either invalidates the `.po` translations and forces translators to redo them. — In gettext, the `msgid` (including its placeholder style, since `%s` and `%(name)s` yield different keys) is the exact lookup key into the catalogs under `locale/`, so any edit to it or to the placeholder style makes the existing entry unfindable and the string silently reverts to English, discarding translators' completed work.

## API Design

<!-- rule:822 -->
- Don't rely on `bulk_create()` for PKs or `save()` signals — it emits neither; use `create()`/`save()` or re-query when you need them. — `bulk_create()` skips per-row `save()` and (on most backends) doesn't return populated primary keys. Code that assumes otherwise breaks downstream FK assignments, signal-based side effects, and test fixtures that reference `.pk` right after insertion — a bug that only appears at runtime.
  Document the PK/signal caveat wherever you wrap `bulk_create()`, and never assume PKs are populated in tests — these limitations silently break callbacks, FK assignments, and test fixtures.
<!-- rule:2470 -->
- When authoring custom Django model fields, subclass the closest built-in (e.g., `IntegerField`, `BigIntegerField`, `DecimalField`, `FileField`) rather than `Field`, and override only what genuinely differs (e.g., don't make `SerialField` handle custom defaults — point users to a general-purpose field) — inheriting from the nearest built-in preserves type coercion, validation, and DB mapping for free, so users get predictable behavior instead of reimplemented surprises. — Subclassing the closest built-in field inherits its type conversion, validation, and DB mapping so custom fields behave like the rest of Django. Extending `Field` directly means reimplementing that logic and users hit subtle inconsistencies. Keeping `SerialField` scoped to its core purpose (and redirecting flexible cases to a general-purpose field) prevents it from accumulating one-off behavior.
<!-- rule:4998 -->
- Acquire cursors via `with connection.cursor() as cursor:` (or `with <query>.get_compiler(using).execute_sql(CURSOR) as cursor:`) instead of assigning `conn.cursor()` to a bare variable — cursors then close deterministically even on exceptions, and only non-cursor-side code stays outside the `with`; keep query execution, `rowcount` reads, related assignments, and the `return` inside the block. This is the cross-cutting DB-API convention used across `django/core/management`, `django/contrib/gis`, `django/contrib/postgres`, `django/core/cache`, `django/db/models`, and `tests/backends`. — Cursors left as bare variable assignments leak connections when an exception fires mid-block since nothing closes them; the `with` form guarantees cleanup and keeps cursor-dependent work (queries, `rowcount`, returns) scoped inside the block so nothing reads a already-closed cursor.
<!-- rule:1747 -->
- `Model.save()` overrides: match Django's current recommended signature and delegate to `super().save(...)`, never `self.save(...)` — `self.save` recurses infinitely — Enforces the `Model.save()` override conventions Django applies across docs and framework code so overrides don't recurse, break callers relying on default kwargs, clobber concurrent writes, or mislead users copying stale documentation examples.
  - Preserve default kwarg values like `update_fields=None` in `save()` overrides — dropping them silently changes behavior for existing callers
  - Use `*args/**kwargs` only in the position the current API recommends, dropping `*args` once deprecated — keeps overrides compatible with upstream subclasses
  - After mutating known fields, pass `save(update_fields=[...])` with concrete field names — avoids clobbering concurrent writes and unnecessary `UPDATE`s
  - In `save()` overrides that augment `update_fields`, treat it as an arbitrary iterable, merge via `{field}.union(update_fields)`, assign the merged set to `kwargs['update_fields']`, and forward to `super().save(*args, **kwargs, update_fields=update_fields)` — callers may pass lists, sets, or generators
  - When a `Model.save()` signature changes, update the affected docs and prose together (e.g. `docs/topics/db/models.txt`, `docs/ref/signals.txt`) — stale examples teach users to copy a broken pattern
<!-- rule:420 -->
- Use Django timezone utilities (`timezone.now`, `timezone.make_aware`, `timezone.get_default_timezone`) instead of naive stdlib calls like `datetime.datetime.now()`/`utcnow()`, `os.environ['TZ']`, pytz `localize()`, `LocalTimezone`, or hard-coded UTC — keeps `settings.USE_TZ` semantics intact across `db/backends/base`, `db/models/fields`, `contrib/admin`, `core/cache`, and `db/migrations`. Preserve `USE_TZ` behavior: naive local time when `USE_TZ` is false, naive UTC when true. Omit the tz argument to `timezone.make_aware` when the configured default is intended, and use it to assign timezones to naive date/time values. Never add a `TypeError` requiring a tz that previously accepted naive datetimes or date-only values; treat date-only with time specifiers as midnight UTC. Pass dynamic defaults as callables (`default=timezone.now`, `default=datetime.date.today`), not invoked values (`default=timezone.now()`), so values are per-row and Django's fixed-default system check warning is avoided. — Naive datetime calls and manual TZ handling bypass `settings.USE_TZ`, producing mixing-aware/naive bugs across the DB layer, admin, cache, and migrations; invoking a default callable freezes one timestamp for every row and triggers Django's fixed-default check warning.
<!-- rule:4805 -->
- Normalize keys once at the call boundary, then reuse — re-normalizing risks silent lookup misses. — - Mixed raw/normalized keys across `db`, `utils`, and `test` paths produce lookups that pass in one place and fail in another; tying normalization to one site (call boundary or point of use) makes behavior predictable.
- Normalizing stored keys would break iteration order/reprs for users who inserted mixed-case identifiers; normalizing at lookup keeps both a tolerant API and faithful iteration.
- Decorator-wrapped or throwaway normalization hides where keys become canonical, making it hard to trace why a dict miss occurred.
  - Case-insensitive containers: normalize only for lookup/`in`; keep stored keys' original casing for iteration and `str()` — users expect to see what they inserted. Document this in the container's docstring.
  - Don't hide trivial normalization behind decorators or stash normalized values in unused variables — it obscures the single canonical normalization point.
<!-- rule:4502 -->
- Treat formset-factory options as public API: propagate them through `modelformset_factory`, `inlineformset_factory`, and `formset_factory` into the generated formset class, document each factory-call layer, and test every public entry point (including the exact broken factory path for bug fixes) — formset factories in `django/forms/models.py` and `django/contrib/contenttypes/forms.py` are user-facing, so inconsistent signatures and untested paths break user code. — Formset factories in `django/forms/models.py` and `django/contrib/contenttypes/forms.py` are public API, so inconsistent signatures and untested factory paths break user code.
<!-- rule:3509 -->
- In Django middleware and request header handling, document and configure `request.META` keys by their actual names (e.g. `REMOTE_USER`, `HTTP_REMOTE_USER`), not vague placeholders like "configured key" — placeholders hide the real contract callers and docs must match. — Concrete key names let users and downstream code reference the exact `request.META` entry Django reads, avoiding misconfigurations that silently fail auth.
<!-- rule:567 -->
- Preserve all state in `__getstate__`/`__setstate__`/`__reduce__` — copy-and-filter `__dict__` rather than whitelisting attributes; whitelists silently drop state on pickle and break copies. — Pickle/copy hooks that hardcode a minimal attribute set or skip derived/constructor state silently reconstruct broken instances — the copy behaves differently from the original. Filtering `__dict__` and storing all `_constructor_args` (positional + `**extra`) keeps reconstructed objects faithful.
  - Restore descriptor-derived attributes in `__setstate__` — descriptor logic is bypassed during unpickling, so derived values would be missing.
  - Populate `_constructor_args` with every argument needed for faithful reconstruction — all positional args (e.g., `output_field`) plus `**extra` kwargs — subsets produce objects that diverge from originals.
<!-- rule:4885 -->
- Expose `select_for_update(of=...)` as a single set of relation paths, not boolean flags like `of_self` — extensible API, consistent with Django's set-based query args — Placement: repository-root AGENTS.md — the rule spans `django/db/models/query.py` (API), `django/db/models/sql/compiler.py` (SQL `FOR UPDATE OF` generation), and `tests/queries/tests.py`, so it applies across directories and belongs at the broadest scope.

Design intent: A single `of` set mirrors Django's other relation-path query arguments and avoids a combinatorial explosion of flags (e.g. `of_self`, `of_related`) as new lock targets are added. The `'self'` sentinel keeps the common case (lock the queryset's own table) explicit without inventing new parameters, and conditional locking on `'self'` avoids emitting a `FOR UPDATE OF` clause for a table whose columns aren't actually selected — a real correctness issue when the base model is only used for joining.
  - Use the `'self'` sentinel for the queryset's own model table; resolve each relation path to its joined table name when emitting SQL `FOR UPDATE OF`
  - Lock `'self'` only when a selected column comes from the base model's table, otherwise omit; include every model whose rows are fetched and locked in `FOR UPDATE OF` — prevents locking unfetched rows or leaving fetched rows unlocked
<!-- rule:3305 -->
- ``` — The rule targets a duplicated-source-of-truth bug class: a parameter like `app_label` or `model_name` passed next to an object that already knows its own model (e.g. `self.object_list.model`) can silently disagree with that object and requires touching every call site whenever the signature evolves — evidence spans `django/contrib/admin`, `django/forms`, and `django/db/migrations`, so it applies wherever new function arguments or request/query params are added, not to one module.
  - Don't pass parameters derivable from an existing reference (`self`, parent, container) — resolve them inside
  ```
  **Rationale:** Duplicated inputs like `app_label`/`model_name` alongside a view or model instance (`self.object_list.model`) drift out of sync with their source of truth and force every caller to change when the signature does. Read the value from the object already in scope instead.
<!-- rule:5366 -->
- Raise on unexpected keys left in `options`/`**kwargs` bags — silently ignored typos become no-ops that are near-impossible to trace — Flexible option bags (an `options` mapping, `**kwargs`, or a base method's `**options` meant for subclasses like `search.py`, `query.py`, and `base/operations.py`) accept anything by default. Validating keys against the allowed set and erroring once supported options are consumed turns a mistyped or stale key from a silent no-op into a loud failure. Without this, callers pass misspelled options, get default behavior, and debug for hours before finding the typo.
<!-- rule:2499 -->
- Copy mutable mapping args in widget constructors (`self.x = {} if x is None else x.copy()`) and `pop()` consumed keys like `type` from the copy, never the caller's dict — aliasing mutates shared `attrs`/`option_attrs` and can duplicate HTML attributes across renders. — The rule describes a general API-design convention for widget constructors that accept mutable mapping arguments, and the evidence spans multiple modules/directories (`django/forms/widgets.py`, `django/contrib/admin/widgets.py`) as well as tests. It is not limited to a single module or file, so a repository-root `AGENTS.md` gives it the broadest practical visibility and ensures assistants apply it across all widget implementations.
<!-- rule:3131 -->
- Parenthesize SQL expressions in `as_sql` only when required: wrap non-column expressions, leave bare columns and SQL already starting with `(` (tuple/row constructors) unwrapped, and skip position-sensitive operands like `ANY`/`ARRAY` — implement as one uniform rule, not `isinstance` checks on `Col` or backend branches, so compiled SQL stays portable across backends — isinstance checks against concrete expression classes and backend-gated branches produce non-portable compiled SQL; a single uniform parenthesization rule in `as_sql` keeps the ORM backend-agnostic.
<!-- rule:674 -->
- In Django ORM code, do not explicitly wrap a QuerySet in `Subquery` for ordinary subquery use; pass the QuerySet directly, even with `OuterRef`. Reserve `Subquery` for specialized templating or advanced configuration. When a `Subquery` wraps a `values()` expression that already determines the type, omit `output_field` and let the ORM infer it; auto-inference should handle aggregated subqueries rather than relying on a single select column. — Wrapping a plain QuerySet in `Subquery` obscures intent and can defeat the ORM's SQL generation and type-inference paths. Explicit `output_field` on a `values()`-typed `Subquery` overrides auto-inference and breaks aggregated subqueries, which cannot be described by a single select column. Keeping these expression-layer rules consistent across `django/db/models/expressions.py`, `django/db/models/sql/datastructures.py`, and the expression/composite-field test suites prevents divergent behavior between ORM internals and user-facing APIs.
<!-- rule:4219 -->
- In data migrations and `use_in_migrations = True` managers, resolve historical models via `state.get_model(app_label, model_name)` (preferred) or `self.model` — not `state.apps.get_model(...)`. Never instantiate the global model class or use `Model.objects`; if a class-level helper is missing on the historical model, call `apps.get_model` only for that helper, still returning `self.model`. — Historical models bind to the schema and managers as they existed at that migration, so reusing the current class or its `objects` manager breaks reproducibility and can fail on older or newer DB states.
<!-- rule:2705 -->
- Bind `transaction.atomic` to the right alias (`using=router.db_for_write(...)`, `self.connection.alias`, or `using=using` in `Model.save_base`) — wrong alias silently skips the intended transaction. — These rules matter because binding `atomic` to the wrong database alias silently commits or rolls back on the wrong connection, placing `try`/`except` inside the block prevents exceptions from triggering rollback, over-broad scopes cause unrelated writes to roll back together, and choosing the wrong `save_base` transaction form adds unnecessary savepoints or misses rollback marking—so following them preserves correct atomicity, database routing, and error propagation.
  - Put `try`/`except` *outside* the `atomic` block — exceptions must propagate through it to trigger rollback.
  - Scope transactions to the smallest correct unit (per-entity in loops; wrap delete-then-save) — prevents unrelated writes from rolling back together.
  - In `Model.save_base`-style paths, pick by query count: `transaction.mark_for_rollback_on_error(using=using)` for single-query, `transaction.atomic(using=using, savepoint=False)` when grouping related queries (e.g. `meta.parents` branches) — avoids unnecessary savepoints and matches Django's write semantics.
<!-- rule:3610 -->
- Size bulk ORM batches with `DatabaseOperations.bulk_batch_size()`, deriving limits from `features.max_query_params` — hardcoded or hand-counted field limits break across backends. — Backends diverge in how many placeholders they tolerate per statement, so deriving batch sizes from `features.max_query_params` keeps `bulk_create`/`bulk_update` portable. Counting only logical fields undercounts SQL that a `CompositePrimaryKey` expands into multiple placeholders, or that repeats a field across `CASE`/`WHEN` and `WHERE`, producing queries that exceed the limit at runtime. Backend hard limits like `SQLITE_MAX_COMPOUND_SELECT` only bind when the emitted SQL actually uses that construct; applying them to `VALUES`-based inserts or parameter-less queries over-shrinks batches for no reason. Recorded practical caps (SQLite's 999, set by `SQLITE_MAX_EXPR_DEPTH`) document why the number differs from the raw constant. Accepting a conservative limit and deferring relaxation prevents ad-hoc workarounds from becoming permanent and obscuring the underlying bound.
  - Count every placeholder the emitted SQL actually contains: each `CompositePrimaryKey` constituent (unless the backend supports tuple-comparison lookups) and each repeated field in `CASE`/`WHEN` and `WHERE`.
  - Apply backend hard limits only to SQL the ORM emits: don't enforce `SQLITE_MAX_COMPOUND_SELECT` on `VALUES`-based bulk inserts, and don't batch parameter-less queries.
  - Keep documented practical caps (e.g. SQLite's effective 999 from `SQLITE_MAX_EXPR_DEPTH`) with the reason recorded.
  - If a conservative limit causes test failures, accept it and file a separate ticket to relax — temporary workarounds mask the real cap.
<!-- rule:3510 -->
- Distinguish grouped (`optgroup`) from flat `choices` via `isinstance()` or the field's `_choices_is_value()` helper — don't hardcode `isinstance(label, (list, tuple))`, since flat choices can use any accepted sequence type. — Each bullet explains why the constraint exists: hardcoding tuple/list shapes breaks on the full range of accepted choice sequence types and misclassifies flat choices; iterating scalar values or consuming iterators in an upfront guard causes type errors and silently bypasses validation; permitting hybrids forces ad-hoc handling in downstream code; treating nested callables uniformly prevents lazy callables from being re-wrapped incorrectly; and matching on the `ChoiceIterator` base class is necessary to avoid circular imports with `ModelChoiceIterator` while keeping normalization deferred until iteration.
  - Treat `str`, `bytes`, and lazy `Promise` values as atomic `choices` — return them early instead of iterating/unpacking, and validate per-item during iteration; an upfront `any()` guard consumes iterators and lets scalar values slip through.
  - In `normalize_field_choices`, accept only canonical container shapes (mapping, sequence of 2-tuples, nested groups); reject hybrids like iterables of mappings that would need special-case handling.
  - Handle `Callable` at every depth: wrap top-level callables (`depth == 0`) in `CallableChoiceIterator` for lazy evaluation, but eagerly evaluate nested callables so they materialize as plain choice lists.
  - Detect lazy iterators via the shared `ChoiceIterator` base class, not concrete subclasses like `ModelChoiceIterator` — avoids circular imports and preserves lazy behavior until iteration.
<!-- rule:5154 -->
- In `Field.has_changed()` overrides, normalize `initial`/`data` via `to_python()`, return `False` early when both are in `self.empty_values` or when the field is disabled, and keep distinct values (e.g. JSON `null` vs `""`) distinct; document the disabled-field behavior in the docstring. — Ad-hoc conversions like `bool()` and collapsing semantically distinct values into one placeholder produce false-positive change detection, and treating disabled fields (which are bound to their initial value) as changed breaks the form contract. Applies in `django/forms/fields.py` and subclasses such as `django/contrib/postgres/forms/array.py` and `jsonb.py`.
<!-- rule:4603 -->
- Centralize value normalization in `to_python()` — duplicate parsing across model and form fields drifts apart — Django model and form fields both implement normalization; keeping conversion in `to_python()` avoids two diverging codepaths that disagree on edge cases and silently break form/model symmetry.
<!-- rule:2849 -->
- Normalize optional/default values exactly once in one authoritative layer; consume the normalized form everywhere else. — Scattered fallbacks like `arg or []` or duplicated setter guards drift out of sync, so callers see inconsistent defaults and bugs hide in whichever path skips normalization.
<!-- rule:5280 -->
- Keep `%W`/`%V` Monday-first and `%U` Sunday-first in strftime week branches — mixed semantics skew week arithmetic by a day (see `week_choices = {'%W': '1', '%U': '0', '%V': '1'}` in `datetime.py`) — Django's strftime `%U` is Sunday-first while `%W`/`%V` are Monday-first; without a fixed convention the week-based transforms and truncation silently drift by a day and disagree with the ISO 8601 behavior users expect from `kind="week"`. Introducing `IsoWeekDay` alongside `week_day` avoids breaking the existing Sunday-based API.
  - Add a separate `IsoWeekDay` function/transform instead of redefining `week_day` — preserves the existing Sunday-based transform for callers
  - Document that `kind="week"` truncation returns Mondays and that ISO week 53 isn't every year, using the wording `ISO-8601 week days` — users assume week 53 is recurring
  - Apply the same Monday-first semantics across `django/views/generic/dates.py` and `docs/releases/*.txt` — keeps code and release notes from contradicting each other
<!-- rule:166 -->
- Use singular `metavar`/option names for repeatable CLI options (`--database DATABASE`), but plural `dest` (e.g., `dest='tags'`) — the option name reflects one occurrence, the destination the accumulated list. — Naming the option after a single occurrence matches how users invoke it, while a plural `dest` keeps the collected values iterable. In help text, say "Can be used multiple times.", refer to it as "this option", and state cardinality in prose (e.g., "one or more database aliases") so users know it accumulates across flags. Applied across `django/core/management/commands/` (`test.py`, `loaddata.py`, `templates.py`) and `docs/ref/` (`django-admin.txt`, `howto-release-django.txt`).
<!-- rule:525 -->
- Keep Django admin delete flows independent of change permission — expose the built-in delete action only in the changelist, redirect post-delete to `index`, catch `ProtectedError` in `ModelAdmin.delete_view` and redirect to the protected-objects page, and skip deletion-confirmation settings on read-only inlines — delete-only users otherwise land on change-permission pages and hit broken flows. — Separating deletion concerns from add/change permission and read-only inline config keeps admin usable for users who only hold delete permission; routing back to the changelist or protected-objects page avoids intermediate pages that require change access.
<!-- rule:348 -->
- Open auxiliary SQLite connections via `self.connection.Database.connect(...)`, not `sqlite3.connect(...)` — the wrapper applies test DB settings and tracks connections, so direct calls can target the wrong file or leak — Guidelines for SQLite backend code in `django/db/backends/sqlite3/creation.py` and SQLite-specific tests (`tests/backends/sqlite`, `tests/test_sqlite.py`), placed at the root AGENTS.md so they apply across backend and test utilities.
  - Derive SQLite DB filenames from configured test DB names/settings, never hard-code `.sqlite3` — parallel test runners rename/append suffixes, so literal paths go stale
  - Close copied and in-memory SQLite connections with `_close()` during cleanup — `_close()` unregisters the connection; plain `.close()` leaves it tracked and can hang teardown
  - Remove obsolete temporary workarounds/guards in `django/db/backends/sqlite3/` and SQLite tests — dead guards mask regressions once the underlying issue is fixed
<!-- rule:119 -->
- Resolve two-digit years per RFC 7231 / `datetime.strptime` semantics (`00`-`68` → 2000s, `69`-`99` → 1900s), not a hardcoded pivot like `50` — matches Python so client-side mirrors stay consistent. — Parsing two-digit years via a fixed pivot or ad-hoc cutoff diverges from RFC 7231 and Python's `datetime.strptime`, so server and client code resolving the same input can return different dates. Naming two-digit inputs distinctly (`short_year`) also prevents accidental comparisons against resolved four-digit values, which bypass century resolution and can reorder events.
  - Mirror `strptime` century rules in JS reimplementations (e.g. `django/contrib/admin/static/admin/js/core.js`) — a divergence yields different dates than the server-side `django/utils/http.py` parser.
  - Name two-digit year inputs `short_year` (or similar) and never compare them against four-digit years — mixing them silently produces wrong orderings.
<!-- rule:624 -->
- Build correlated ORM subqueries with `Subquery`/`OuterRef`, not `RawSQL` — keeps queries portable across DB backends and composable with the ORM. — All four points keep correlated subqueries correct across backends. `RawSQL` bypasses the ORM and breaks on backend-specific quoting/composition; a pre-filtered queryset bakes in filters that change which outer row gets correlated; and `__in=OuterRef(...)` produces a redundant `IN (SELECT ...)` instead of a direct `=`.
  - Derive the `Subquery` from the model's base manager (e.g. `MyModel.objects`), not an already-filtered queryset — inherited filters silently corrupt the correlation.
  - Filter the inner queryset by the outer row (`filter(related_field=OuterRef("pk"))`).
  - Prefer direct relation equality (`related_field=OuterRef(...)`) over `related_field__in=OuterRef(...)` — `__in` adds an unnecessary `IN` wrapper instead of a plain equality.
<!-- rule:421 -->
- Use `astimezone()` to convert an aware datetime; use `replace(tzinfo=...)` only to relabel wall-clock time — mixing them silently changes the instant. — The rule prevents silent off-by-an-hour bugs and DST-related `NonExistentTimeError`/`AmbiguousTimeError` failures by keeping instant-preserving conversions distinct from wall-clock relabeling, avoiding pointless DST resolution for UTC, making DST assumptions explicit instead of exception-driven, and keeping datetime construction unambiguous by naming `tzinfo`.
  For UTC, prefer `datetime(..., tzinfo=timezone.utc)` or `.replace(tzinfo=timezone.utc)` over `make_aware` — UTC has no DST so DST resolution is pointless and can raise.
  For naive→aware with an explicit DST assumption, pass `is_dst` to `timezone.make_aware(value, tzinfo, is_dst=...)` directly rather than catching `NonExistentTimeError`/`AmbiguousTimeError` and falling back to `tzinfo.localize(..., is_dst=...)`.
  Pass `tzinfo` to datetime constructors as a keyword argument.
<!-- rule:1286 -->
- In async I/O paths, use `aiofiles` or block only via `sync_to_async` when the op can truly block (e.g. a `SpooledTemporaryFile._rolled` to disk), and keep in-memory fast-path writes synchronous — unconditional `sync_to_async` adds thread-hop overhead on every call while skipping it stalls the event loop. — Async codepaths in Django (session backends, ORM query internals, ASGI handler) must stay non-blocking without paying per-call thread-switch costs on in-memory data. Shipping an async API that's slower than its sync counterpart misleads callers and hides the regression.
  - Don't ship an async API that's slower than its sync backends; omit/disable it or add chunked buffering gated by a configurable `chunk_size` — an inefficient async path silently regresses throughput for callers who switched to `await` expecting parity.
<!-- rule:5844 -->
- Read `data-*` attributes via `element.dataset` (or `.data()` in jQuery code), not `getAttribute`/`.attr()` — native `dataset` and jQuery `.data()` handle attribute-name conversion, value coercion, and caching, so raw getters silently return strings and miss updates. In code that doesn't already require jQuery, prefer native `dataset`. — Convention spans Django contrib/admin and GIS static JS, so it applies to any JavaScript/DOM code.
<!-- rule:2145 -->
- Generate `aria-*` referenced IDs with suffixes matching the attribute value and add the target element in the same change — dangling references leave elements unlabeled for screen readers. — The rule governs ARIA relationship attributes and HTML ID generation across multiple Django subsystems (admin helpers, admin templatetags, forms utilities), so it is not confined to one module or file. It is a general behavioral accessibility rule that any AI assistant editing HTML-producing code should apply repo-wide, making AGENTS.md at the repository root the most findable and appropriately broad location.
  - Skip `aria-labelledby` on elements that already have `aria-labelledby` or `aria-label` — duplicate names make screen readers announce conflicting text.
<!-- rule:1978 -->
- Build querysets internally with `self.model._base_manager` (or `_base_manager.db_manager(using=..., hints={'instance': ...})` for routing) instead of `objects`/`_default_manager` + `router.db_for_read` — `_base_manager` is unfiltered, so internal ORM queries don't silently drop rows due to a custom manager's `get_queryset()` filters. — `_base_manager` skips custom `get_queryset()` filtering, so internal querysets (in `django/db/models/query.py`, `django/db/models/fields/related_descriptors.py`) see the full table — a custom manager's filters would otherwise cause ORM internals to miss rows. The doc note in `docs/topics/db/managers.txt` prevents users from expecting `default_manager_name` filters to apply on intermediate joins.
  - Document that custom manager `get_queryset()` filters don't apply to related-field joins, and `base_manager_name` only affects related-object access — avoids users relying on `default_manager_name` to filter across relations.
<!-- rule:3476 -->
- Check cross-database relations via the router's `allow_relation()`; treat `None` as disallowed so only an explicit `True` permits the relation — Routers are the single source of truth for relation compatibility. Comparing `_state.db` values bypasses router overrides and silently allows relations the router intended to block, causing integrity errors when the related row lives in a database the router fenced off. Documenting the tri-state (`True`/`False`/`None`) keeps custom routers consistent with Django's contract — implementers otherwise assume `None` means "no opinion, allow".
<!-- rule:1164 -->
- Reuse existing ORM abstractions (`ForeignObject`, `MultiColSource`, `SQLQuery.build_filter`, `Tuple`/`CompositeField` `output_field`) when adding composite/tuple lookups instead of writing a parallel implementation — a second code path drifts from the original's semantics and bugs get fixed in only one place. Only add a dedicated composite expression when the existing abstraction genuinely doesn't fit (e.g., don't reuse relational `MultiColSource` for the composite primary-key column source in `cached_col`, whose semantics are column-source rather than relation traversal). — Reusing existing ORM abstractions for composite lookups prevents a parallel implementation from drifting from the original semantics, where bugs and feature changes would be fixed in only one path, while the `cached_col` exception ensures genuine column-source semantics are not forced into relation traversal.
<!-- rule:1161 -->
- Introspect attributes with `callable()` (not `try/except TypeError`); fall back to `attr.__call__` for callable instances and `attr.fget` for `property` objects (guard with `isinstance(attr, property) and hasattr(attr, 'fget')`) — properties and callable instances lack `__code__`, so naive `unwrap`/`__code__` access crashes. Cover both fallbacks with regression tests. — The fallbacks exist because introspection code across Django subpackages (`django/contrib/admin/utils.py`, `django/contrib/syndication/views.py`, `django/contrib/admin/templatetags/admin_list.py`) assumes attributes are plain functions; `property` objects and callable instances violate that assumption and would otherwise raise `AttributeError` on `__code__`.
<!-- rule:1584 -->
- Use Django batch APIs for bulk ops: chunk `pk__in` filters to stay under the backend's parameter limit (extract shared chunking logic when reused, mirroring `QuerySet.in_bulk()`), use `QuerySet.in_bulk()` for pk-keyed dicts, stream large querysets with `.iterator()`, and prefer a single `.update()` with F expressions in data migrations. Per-row queries blow past backend parameter limits and unbounded materialization exhausts memory; keep documentation examples on the same batched patterns. — Per-row queries exceed the database backend's parameter limit and unbounded queryset materialization loads entire tables into memory; documentation examples shape what future code looks like.
<!-- rule:1774 -->
- Use `asyncio.create_task()` (not `asyncio.ensure_future()`) and pass the coroutine object (`tg.create_task(push())`), never the function reference — matches the pattern in `django/core/handlers/asgi.py`; passing a function reference silently schedules the wrong callable — `ensure_future` is a lower-level API that also accepts futures, and passing a coroutine function instead of a coroutine object is a silent bug. Explicit `create_task` calls keep heterogeneous task ownership obvious across Django's async code.
  - For a small fixed set of heterogeneous tasks, create them with explicit `create_task` calls and reuse the existing task collection — avoids `zip`-over-tuples and one-off task variables that obscure task ownership (see `django/db/utils.py`, `tests/asgi/urls.py`)
<!-- rule:2660 -->
- Use `http.HTTPStatus` members and Django's `status_code` param/attr for HTTP statuses, not numeric literals or undocumented `http.client.*` constants — status-specific response subclasses, code-to-class mappings, and a settable `status` alias each re-encode the same code and drift out of sync with `django/http/response.py` — `HTTPStatus` is the documented, stable enum interface for status codes, and `status_code` is the canonical Django response field and constructor parameter; using numeric literals or undocumented constants, status-code-specific response subclasses, status-code-to-class mappings, or duplicate `status` attributes lets those representations drift and forces callers and maintainers to reconcile multiple sources of truth. A one-class-per-status-code design causes subclass proliferation, while a flag or direct `status_code` keeps response construction consistent and avoids unnecessary maintenance.
<!-- rule:4251 -->
- Put WSGI environ classes in `django/http/request.py`, not `django/utils/datastructures.py` — WSGI is an HTTP-layer concern and generic utilities are shared with ASGI — This matters because WSGI-specific concerns leaking into shared utilities or being assumed to apply to ASGI cause subtle interface mismatches, malformed environ input, divergent hacks, and regressions that only dual-interface tests and docs will catch.
  - Convert headers/keys explicitly per target interface; don't assume CGI/WSGI `HTTP_` semantics carry over — prevents subtle WSGI/ASGI mismatches
  - Validate and sanitize WSGI environ values where the environment is created — catches malformed input before it reaches handlers
  - Work around non-standard WSGI environ/header quirks only if Django core handlers already do — avoids divergent one-off hacks
  - Cover both WSGI and ASGI in tests and docs — dual-interface support hides regressions when only one path is exercised
<!-- rule:2473 -->
- Use semantic introspection metadata (`identity` vs. `sequence`, sequence ownership, PK membership) instead of default expressions to classify schema constructs — low-level signals misclassify fields when backends express the same construct differently — Default-expression sniffing and mechanism-named flags break across backends that express the same construct differently, silently misclassifying columns and gating features on the wrong signal — introspection metadata and behavior-named flags keep classification correct as implementations change.
  - Name capability flags after the general DB behavior they gate, not the mechanism behind it (e.g., not `has_sequence`) — the behavior outlives the implementation, so flags stay correct across backends
<!-- rule:3371 -->
- Is clear and direct (imperative mood). Includes WHY (backward compat, prevents bugs, consistency). Is NOT deontological. Fits in a bullet point (ideally under 100 chars for the rule, rationale can be longer). Preserves project-specific references — class names, file paths, directory conventions, config patterns. — the bug, incompatibility, or inconsistency it prevents. Written for a reader who only ever sees the final text. Uses backticks for all code identifiers, file paths, class names, function names, parameter names, etc.
<!-- rule:5075 -->
- In key-rotation APIs, `signing`/`dumping` functions must use only the current/primary key — never accept verification-only params like old/retired keys. Exposing them lets a signer emit tokens no `unsign` path can verify. — Keeps signing one-directional so tokens are always verifiable against an evolving rotation set.
<!-- rule:1284 -->
- Only `await` awaitables: if a callable like `cache_key()` becomes a coroutine, `await` it at every call site — a missed `await` passes a coroutine object instead of the value, and awaiting a sync function raises `TypeError`, both breaking callers silently. — Async/await mistakes fail at runtime, not import time: an un-awaited coroutine leaks a coroutine object into downstream code (e.g. as a cache key), while awaiting a plain sync function raises `TypeError`. Propagating `await` at every call site when a function flips to async keeps return types honest across the codebase.
<!-- rule:4105 -->
- Avoid non-atomic set-and-restore of process-global state (`umask`, shared FS) in worker/multithreaded code — races corrupt concurrent tasks; use locks, read-only `/proc/self/status` `Umask`, or avoid the dependency (see `collectstatic`, `django/core/files/storage.py`) — Concurrent workers cannot observe consistent global state across a non-atomic set-and-restore sequence; interleaved mutations leak into other tasks (e.g., `collectstatic`'s mode changes or `storage.py` file writes), causing hard-to-reproduce permission and filesystem bugs.
<!-- rule:3019 -->
- For update-specific SQL compilation, extend existing transform classes (e.g. `IndexTransform`) instead of making `Field` compilable or hand-writing raw SQL in the compiler — expose the SQL via a helper next to `get_placeholder()` (e.g. `get_transforms_for_update()`, or `as_update_sql(rhs)` returning `(sql, params)`) and call it from the update path; default impls must `raise NotImplementedError` so unsupported transforms fail explicitly rather than emit incorrect SQL. — Reusing transform classes keeps update SQL consistent with the rest of the compiler and avoids divergent SQL paths. Explicit `NotImplementedError` defaults turn silent wrong-SQL bugs into loud failures, and only transforms that actually support update need overrides.
<!-- rule:4940 -->
- Update m2m/reverse relations via `.add()`/`.set()`/`.remove()` — direct assignment relies on a deprecated implicit save — Django deprecated implicit saves on direct m2m/reverse assignment; `.add()`/`.set()`/`.remove()` are the supported API and will keep working. Passing PKs to `.add(*ids)` lets the manager bulk-insert the through-table rows in one query instead of one per instance, avoiding N+1 behavior in `tests/auth_tests/models/with_many_to_many.py`-style models and the query patterns documented in `docs/topics/db/queries.txt`.
  - Pass PKs as unpacked args to `.add(*ids)` instead of fetching instances or looping — avoids redundant queries and N+1 loops
  Use relation-manager methods for many-to-many and reverse relation updates. Direct relation assignment (e.g. `obj.tags = [...]`) triggers an implicit save that Django has deprecated, so it will break on upgrade. When attaching multiple related objects, pass primary-key values directly as unpacked positional arguments (`.add(*ids)`); fetching each instance first or looping `.add()` per object issues one query per row and reintroduces the N+1 pattern these managers exist to avoid.
<!-- rule:3722 -->
- Use backend sequence helpers (`pg_get_serial_sequence`, `_get_sequence_name`, Oracle `currval` execution form) instead of assuming `{table}_{column}_seq` — default sequence names vary by backend/version and branch, so hardcoded names and generic `SELECT` templates produce wrong or missing results in `django/db/backends` and `django/contrib/postgres` code and tests. — Sequence naming is backend- and version-specific; relying on a default `{table}_{column}_seq` pattern or a helper that only exists on another branch causes tests and production code to query the wrong sequence or fail outright on Oracle/PostgreSQL.
<!-- rule:1595 -->
- Test `query.combinator` truthiness (not a hardcoded combinator list) — new combinators are caught without list updates — Hardcoded value lists silently miss new combinators, and checking `where` for non-emptiness reports a restricted state even when the user added no filters — both produce wrong admin/API behavior in `Query`/`QuerySet` state detection.
  - Detect restricted querysets via `root_qs.query.where != qs.query.where`, not non-empty `qs.query.where` — the latter flags internal/default where clauses as user filtering
  Apply in `django/db/models/query.py` (`Query`/`QuerySet` state detection) and any caller like `django/contrib/admin/views/main.py`.
<!-- rule:3438 -->
- Match `execute_sql` result kind to its flags (`ROW_COUNT`, `CURSOR`) and guard `None` before consuming — `CURSOR` may return `None` for DML that runs no query — The `execute_sql` API is a two-sided contract: callers declare intent via `ROW_COUNT`/`CURSOR` flags, and implementations must return a matching type. `execute_sql(CURSOR)` is not guaranteed to return a cursor — it returns `None` for DML with no query — so callers that assume a cursor will crash (guard like `with ... as cursor: return cursor.rowcount if cursor else 0`). Symmetrically, implementations that override `CURSOR` to a different type or raise `ValueError` for internal-only kinds silently break callers in `django/db/models/query.py` and `django/db/models/sql/subqueries.py`. Changing one side without the other breaks the invariant.
  - Keep `execute_sql` implementations honest: never override `CURSOR` to return another type or raise `ValueError` for internal cursor kinds — the same contract is relied on by call sites in `django/db/models/query.py` and `django/db/models/sql/subqueries.py`, so compiler and callers must change together
  (Scope: `django/db/models/sql/compiler.py` and `execute_sql` call sites such as `django/db/models/query.py`.)
<!-- rule:5782 -->
- Bulk mutation methods (`bulk_create`, `bulk_update`) return the database-reported affected/matched row count — not `len(objs)` — and their docs in `docs/ref/models/querysets.txt` and release notes must state the count may differ from the input, listing the causes: duplicate PKs (across or within a batch), batching, and race conditions. Returning `len(objs)` was rejected in #599301998 because callers rely on the DB's actual write outcome, not the caller's assumed input size. — Prevents callers from trusting a count that ignores duplicate PKs, batching, and races — the DB's reported count is the only source of truth.
<!-- rule:2934 -->
- Centralize connection creation in an overridable `_get_new_connection` helper — a single override point keeps driver/version branching (e.g. `postgresql/base.py`, `core/mail/__init__.py`) out of public methods — Duplicating provider/version checks and fallback `get_connection()` calls across public methods drifts out of sync and forces subclasses to patch every call site; one helper lets callers pass an existing `connection` (only create one `if not connection:`) and lets subclasses override connection setup once.
<!-- rule:4071 -->
- Preserve existing TTL in `incr`/`decr` — only `set`/`touch` change expiry, so counters don't silently extend key lifetimes across backends (`django/core/cache/backends/db.py`, `filebased.py`) — These four points together keep TTL semantics consistent across the DB and file-based cache backends. Letting `incr`/`decr` reset expiry would make counters behave differently from other backends and surprise users relying on sliding-window patterns; describing timeout changes as 'updated' rather than 'reset' avoids implying that `touch` restores an old deadline when it actually starts a new one from now. Testing both the value and the preserved expiry in the shared cache suite prevents per-backend regressions from slipping through.
  - `touch` re-applies the timeout from the current call time, not the original insertion — matches backend contract
  - Document TTL changes as 'updated', never 'reset' — 'reset' implies restoration and misleads readers of `docs/topics/cache.txt`
  - In `tests/cache/tests.py`, assert both the `incr`/`decr` result and that the key still expires at the original timeout — catches TTL drift between backends
<!-- rule:5661 -->
- Treat the top level of JSON as the full JSON type space — when parsing or validating JSON, handle objects, arrays, strings, numbers, booleans, and `null` instead of assuming a `dict`. When producing JSON, default to a top-level object (e.g., a Python `dict` via Django `JsonResponse`) and emit a top-level array only for protocols that explicitly require one. — Preserves the project-specific `django/db/models/fields/json.py` and `django/http/response.py` references and explains why each half matters: parsing must round-trip every JSON type per spec, and defaulting responses to objects keeps APIs extensible and avoids top-level-array pitfalls.
  Rationale: the JSON spec permits any type at the top level, so parsers like `django/db/models/fields/json.py` must round-trip arrays, scalars, and `null` without corrupting them; on the response side, defaulting to an object keeps `django/http/response.py` outputs consistent and extensible (new fields can be added without breaking clients, and JSON hijacking from top-level arrays is avoided).
<!-- rule:2307 -->
- `Field` subclasses without a `name` param must set `self.name = None` in `__init__` instead of relying on `django/db/migrations/autodetector.py` or classification helpers to assign `field.name` afterward. Helpers should read existing field attributes, never mutate them as a side effect. — Fields constructed outside the migration autodetector (e.g. `ModelState` reconstruction, `deconstruct()`-based cloning) never pass through those helpers, so leaving `name` unset causes `AttributeError` when code later reads `field.name`; keeping `name` owned by `Field.__init__` makes field instances self-consistent regardless of construction path and prevents autodetector side effects from silently changing field identity across migration runs.
<!-- rule:273 -->
- In Django CBVs, set view config as class attributes and pass init params (`next_page`, `extra_context`) to `as_view(**kwargs)` — don't override `as_view()` or repeat kwargs at the call site (only pass `request`), keeping config declarative and single-sourced so views stay reusable and call sites can't drift. — Splitting config between `as_view()` and the call site duplicates state and lets callers silently override view behavior; class attributes keep configuration declarative and greppable wherever the view is mounted (tests/ and django/ alike).
<!-- rule:5885 -->
- Put pickling exclusions in the owning class (`HttpResponse.non_pickleable_attrs`), not in downstream consumers like `django/utils/log.py` — one canonical exclusion list keeps serialization consistent for every caller — Scattering exclusions across consumers like logging handlers means the class can't be pickled reliably regardless of which component touches it; test-client attrs (`resolver_match`, `asgi_request`) are invisible to the class unless explicitly excluded, so sync/async tests fail intermittently.
  Include all test-client-injected attrs (`resolver_match`, `asgi_request`) for both sync and async clients, grouped under an origin comment — missing entries break pickling in tests
  Comment why each attr is excluded, distinguishing test-harness-only entries from core instance state — reviewers can't infer origin otherwise
<!-- rule:2386 -->
- Serialize `CompositePrimaryKey` values as JSON arrays — store them in `CharField`/`TextField` as JSON, never flatten to a plain string; constituent fields must be JSON-serializable so the key round-trips intact across admin, models, and tests. — A composite key preserves its constituent fields' types only when serialized as JSON; flattening to a string loses type information and breaks deserialization. Documenting this constraint in `tests/composite_pk/models` and docs keeps admin and model code consistent.
<!-- rule:4772 -->
- Treat class/model defaults (e.g., `Meta.ordering`) as fallbacks, not overrides — explicit caller input (e.g., an `order_by()` call) must always win, so never skip processing just because a default exists. Track override state with a boolean flag (initialized `False`, set `True` when the overriding option is applied) instead of storing a copy of the original value. — Explains the WHY: value-copy comparisons drift when defaults mutate, and short-circuiting drops caller intent. Preserves the Django file paths and the boolean-flag mechanism as the concrete fix.
  Rationale: Storing the original value and comparing against it drifts if the default is reassigned or mutated, and short-circuiting on "default present" silently drops caller intent — the boolean flag keeps override detection explicit and cheap across `django/db/models/sql/compiler.py` and `django/db/models/options.py`.
<!-- rule:1060 -->
- Exclude model fields at the forms layer (`editable=False`, explicit `Meta.fields`, or `fields_for_model()`), never by making `formfield()` return `None` — that bypasses the standard contract and breaks callers relying on it. When `Meta.fields = '__all__'`, strip fields tied to uninstalled optional apps in `__init__`. Use framework-native terminology ('exclude') in docs. — Silently returning `None` from `formfield()` violates the API callers expect and breaks field-removal logic across contrib apps (e.g. `django/contrib/redirects`, `contenttypes`, `postgres`). Explicit `Meta.fields`/`editable=False` keeps forms consistent with uninstalled optional dependencies.
<!-- rule:1783 -->
- Prefer semantic dimension predicates (`is_3d`, `is_measured`, `widget.supports_3d`) over numeric `dim`/`coord_dim` comparisons — keeps dimension logic readable and avoids hardcoded magic values drifting across `django/contrib/gis`. — Django GIS code crosses `admin`, `postgis`, `gdal`, and `tests/gis_tests/inspectapp`; relying on raw `dim`/`coord_dim` numbers or implicit geography-vs-geometry handling causes mismatches between field introspection, migrations, widget capabilities, and PostGIS `ST_Force*` results. Explicit predicates, `has_m` flags, and `::geometry` casts keep all layers aligned.
  - Add explicit `has_m` flags (e.g. `GeometryField.has_m=False`) when a field must distinguish M from Z — numeric `dim` alone conflates them, breaking introspection and migrations.
  - Cast geography to `geometry` inside dimension-changing SQL (e.g. `ST_Force3D("geom"::geometry)::geometry(POINTZ,4326)`) — `ST_Force*` operates on geometry, so the cast makes the result type explicit.
  - Declare model/test fields with matching flags (e.g. `PointField(geography=True, dim=3)`) — keeps field introspection, migration state, and DB-level casts in agreement.
<!-- rule:820 -->
- Batch ContentType lookups with `ContentType.objects.get_for_models(*models)` — one query instead of N from looping `get()` — These patterns come from `django/contrib/admin` optimizations: `get_for_models()` batches into a single query, inlining avoids temporary assignments for throwaway lookups, and `for_concrete_model=False` ensures proxy models (e.g. custom `User` subclasses) resolve to the correct ContentType when checking permissions.
  - Inline `ContentType.objects.get_for_model(obj).id` for single-use lookups in list comprehensions — avoids a redundant query
  - Pass `for_concrete_model=False` to `ContentType.objects.get_for_model()` in permission codename/fetch code — proxy models otherwise resolve to their concrete type and permissions break
<!-- rule:4781 -->
- Mark JSON embedded in `<script>` with `type="application/json"` — keeps markup inert so the browser never executes it as JavaScript. — These three steps form one behavioral contract for `<script>`-embedded JSON: correct MIME type prevents execution, quote escaping prevents XSS breakouts, and `textContent` ensures the data (not the node) reaches `JSON.parse`. Referenced in Django by `json_script` in `django/template/defaultfilters.py`, `_json_tag_escapes`, and `django/utils/html.py`; also documented in `docs/ref/templates/builtins.txt`.
  *   Escape single quotes in the embedded-JSON escape table (e.g. Django's `_json_tag_escapes`) — an unescaped `'` breaks out of the JSON string context and enables XSS.
  *   Parse embedded JSON with `JSON.parse(element.textContent)`, never the element itself — passing the DOM node stringifies to `"[object HTMLScriptElement]"` instead of the payload.
<!-- rule:719 -->
- Mirror serialization encoding changes on both encode and decode paths — asymmetric codecs silently corrupt round-trips — The rule sets a contract that encoders and decoders must agree on the encoding. A one-sided change means a payload written by an encoder is misread by a decoder, producing mojibake or decoding errors rather than a clean failure. Symmetry also makes it easier to reason about which encoding is in play, while documenting the contract (e.g., UTF-8) and any fallback prevents implicit assumptions from drifting out of sync, and skipping no-op changes avoids churn and false confidence that a meaningful encoding decision was made.
    Skip no-ops (if output is provably ASCII-only, drop the change) and document the assumed encoding contract (e.g., UTF-8) plus any fallback, so callers know what a decoder will accept.
<!-- rule:2834 -->
- Write `JSONBAgg` (not `ArrayAgg`) for JSON fields, emulate what the minimum supported version lacks (`array_agg` for `JSON_ARRAYAGG` on PostgreSQL < 16), and return the same empty-input value on every backend (`None`, not `[]`, on SQLite) — divergent aggregate behavior breaks portable ORM code and cross-backend tests. — Aggregate output must be identical and type-correct across `django.db.backends.*` so ORM code written against one backend keeps working on others.
<!-- rule:2803 -->
- Merge fixed defaults and optional caller entries into one mapping (`{**defaults, **optional}`) before iterating — branching on existence and concatenating separate lists lets one optional source silently drop or overwrite the other's entries; on true conflicts, raise a clear exception or comment the deliberate precedence (see admin `choices()`, `CompositeField.from_select`). — — branching on existence and concatenating separate lists lets one source silently drop the other's entries; raise on true conflicts or comment the deliberate precedence.
<!-- rule:4443 -->
- Extract class-level validation into a `classmethod` that inspects `cls`, and call it from `__init__` and factory methods instead of instantiating or duplicating the check. — Keeps one source of truth: factory paths and direct construction stay consistent, and validation runs without constructing a throwaway instance.
<!-- rule:2217 -->
- Keep request-state logic at its conceptual layer: `token rotation`, `session flush`, and `CSRF` stay in middleware, views only set state/flags — thin views are easier to test and reuse — Placing concerns in the wrong layer spreads responsibility and makes behavior hard to locate; keeping middleware concerns in middleware and view concerns in views means each piece can be tested and changed at its own level without dragging the rest along.
<!-- rule:6224 -->
- Route redirect targets through `resolve_url()`, never hardcode URLs or view names — settings like `LOGIN_REDIRECT_URL` may hold a view name or named URL pattern, so `resolve_url()` resolves both; for self-redirects derive the view name and kwargs/args from `request.resolver_match` instead of assuming names like `'password_reset_confirm'`, which breaks under custom or namespaced URLconfs. — Hardcoded redirect targets silently 404 or misroute when projects override URLconf paths or namespaces; `resolve_url()` and `request.resolver_match` keep redirects correct regardless of how URLs are wired.
<!-- rule:5893 -->
- Support both per-app targets and a global "latest of all apps" request in migration target resolution, and gate the incomplete-replacement fallback in `MigrationExecutor.migration_plan()` on a specific `migration_name` — never apply it globally. — The global path has no `migration_name` to key the fallback on, so applying it unconditionally would mis-resolve targets. `django/core/management/commands/migrate.py` must pass per-app targets for the executor in `django/db/migrations/executor.py` to trigger the special case correctly.
<!-- rule:838 -->
- Use `LEFT OUTER JOIN` for optional reverse/multi-valued relations in `Coalesce(related_field, fallback)` queries — an inner join drops parents with no related row, so the column is never `NULL` and the fallback never fires. Add a test with a parent that has no related row and assert the fallback value. — Inner joins silently eliminate rows with no matching relation, which means `Coalesce` never gets a `NULL` to replace and the fallback is dead code — the regression test pins this behavior.
<!-- rule:2655 -->
- In Django multi-value model fields and related `__in` lookups (`django/forms/models.py`, `related_lookups.py`): — Keeps pk validation consistent with how pks are actually consumed (per-value in `_check_values`, via the model field's conversion in lookups), avoids a redundant DB round-trip per pk, and prevents an optimization that would slow down the dominant model-instance path in `{field}__in` queries.
