  - Don't special-case raw pk lists in related `__in` lookups — raw pks belong on `{field}_id__in`, which bypasses normalization entirely, so optimizing for them only penalizes the common model-instance case.
<!-- rule:6345 -->
- Filter multi-valued relations (e.g. `permissions`) via `Exists()`, not join + `.distinct()` — avoids duplicate rows and needless dedup work — Joining a many-to-many relation and calling `.distinct()` produces duplicate rows and forces an extra deduplication pass; an `Exists()` subquery expresses the same filter without either cost. This applies to any Django ORM queryset across M2M or other multi-valued relations, so apply it consistently rather than only when duplicates are noticeable.
<!-- rule:3712 -->
- Return derived clusters/partitions (e.g. grouping by primary key) from preparation helpers that already iterate a collection — avoids caller second-passes and duplicated predicate logic — Callers that re-walk the same collection duplicate the helper's predicate logic, which drifts out of sync when the helper changes; having the helper surface its groupings keeps filtering semantics in one place.
<!-- rule:5419 -->
- Provide an idempotent `from_parameter`-style classmethod on value-wrapper classes like `SearchConfig` — returns input unchanged if already the right type, and use it at every normalization site so values aren't double-wrapped. — Callers pass either raw values or pre-built wrapper instances; without an idempotent constructor, normalization sites accidentally nest wrappers (e.g. `SearchConfig(SearchConfig(...))`), breaking downstream consumers that assume a single unwrapped instance.
<!-- rule:5130 -->
- Call hook methods like `_get_ordering_expressions_index()` instead of recomputing derived values inline (e.g., `len(self.ordering)`) — keeps subclasses able to override behavior — Hooks exist so subclasses can override how a derived value is computed. Recomputing inline bypasses those overrides, silently breaking custom behavior in subclasses (e.g., in `django/contrib/postgres/aggregates/mixins.py`).
<!-- rule:6308 -->
- Claim database-queue messages inside `transaction.atomic()` using `select_for_update()` row locks — isolation level alone lets two consumers select the same unclaimed row, causing double-processing. — Row-level locking is the only portable way to guarantee a single claimant across concurrent sessions; PostgreSQL `SKIP LOCKED` is a perf optimization to add only after correctness is verified with multiple independent sessions/transactions, since a correct-but-slower consumer beats a racy one.
<!-- rule:6267 -->
- Implement session backend `save()` as atomic and update-only: update the stored session record or raise — never create one. Only login creates session records, so record presence means logged-in and absence means logged-out; a creating `save()` would resurrect deleted sessions and break that invariant. Do `save()`/`delete()` in one step (no get-then-update) and don't rely on DB transactions, since not all supported session backends provide them. Apply any session-persistence fix to every session backend — divergent create-vs-update behavior across backends yields inconsistent login/logout semantics. — A creating or non-atomic save() can resurrect a deleted session or race with logout, breaking the invariant that session-record presence means logged-in and causing inconsistent login/logout behavior across backends.
<!-- rule:6080 -->
- Pass server-provided attributes (`server_name`, `server_port`) into management command callback hooks as arguments instead of re-deriving them from command state. Read optional server attributes with `getattr(obj, 'attr', fallback)` — custom server classes may override or omit them, so argument-passing keeps the callback contract accurate and `getattr` avoids `AttributeError` on third-party servers. — Custom server classes wired into `runserver`/`basehttp` may not expose every built-in attribute; taking values as callback args keeps them authoritative, and `getattr` fallbacks prevent `AttributeError` on third-party servers.
<!-- rule:3553 -->
- Build ORM filter kwargs from the field's real identifier — `attname` for `GenericForeignKey`-like fields (e.g. `content_type_id`), configured name for dynamic lookups (e.g. `{field}__in`). — Using a field's real identifier instead of a hardcoded name prevents ORM filters from silently targeting the wrong or nonexistent column and keeps filtering consistent across Django's query, contenttypes, and paginator code.
  Fix the source (e.g. `get_filter_kwargs_for_object`) instead of patching call sites — hardcoded names silently filter wrong columns and diverge across `django/db/models/query.py`, `contenttypes/fields.py`, and `paginator.py`. Note: `pk` is a valid Django alias, so `pk__in` stays correct even with custom primary-key names.
<!-- rule:5107 -->
- Mirror `add()` semantics in `remove()` — asymmetric relation APIs surprise callers and drift in docs — Relation-managed APIs (e.g. `django/db/` relations) expose paired `add()`/`remove()` methods across `docs/` and `tests/`; if only one side gets argument-semantics clarifications or behavior fixes, callers hit inconsistent contracts and doc examples diverge. The append-in-removal-path bug is worse: a "remove" that appends leaves the relation entry in place and can duplicate it, so the fix belongs near `django/db/` relation managers.
  - Removal paths must call `.remove(...)`, never append — prevents deletes that silently re-add entries
<!-- rule:6203 -->
- Name factories `make_*` (e.g. `make_debug_cursor`) and order sibling wrapper args the same way (`cursor` before backend/self) — consistent signatures prevent call sites from breaking when new backends are added — Consistent factory naming and argument ordering across sibling wrappers lets call sites pass args positionally without surprises; diverging signatures silently break callers when new backends or wrappers are added.
<!-- rule:4871 -->
- Treat `None` as canonical unset URLconf: `set_urlconf(None)` post-request, normalize to `settings.ROOT_URLCONF` in `get_resolver()` — Normalizing `None`→`settings.ROOT_URLCONF` in `get_resolver()` ensures `get_resolver()` and `get_resolver(settings.ROOT_URLCONF)` hit the same `URLResolver` cache entry, while `set_urlconf(None)` after request processing restores Django's initial blank thread-local state — mismatched handling of `None` between `django/core/handlers/base.py` and `django/urls/resolvers.py` causes duplicate resolver instances and stale URLconf leakage across requests.
  Rationale: keeps `get_resolver()` and `get_resolver(settings.ROOT_URLCONF)` sharing one cached `URLResolver` while restoring Django's blank thread-local state after each request (`django/core/handlers/base.py`, `django/urls/resolvers.py`).
<!-- rule:5429 -->
- Django CBGVs: set `self.object = self.get_object()` before form handling and return an HTTP redirect after a successful POST — mirrors `django/views/generic/edit.py`; prevents stale `self.object` reads and duplicate submissions on refresh — If a POST-based generic view handler accesses `self.object` before it has been initialized with `self.get_object()`, confirmation or form processing can fail with missing or stale object data instead of validating the submitted changes against the intended instance. Rendering a template directly after a successful POST instead of returning an HTTP redirect lets a browser refresh resubmit the same POST, producing duplicate submissions and inconsistent state.
<!-- rule:2703 -->
- Keep `runserver` options that depend on `django.contrib.staticfiles` in `django/contrib/staticfiles/management/commands/runserver.py`, not the base `django/core/management/commands/runserver.py` — the base command must stay usable when `staticfiles` isn't installed, and new lifecycle hooks/parameters belong in a focused change since subclasses override these commands. — `django.core.management.commands.runserver` is imported by projects that don't enable `django.contrib.staticfiles`, so adding `staticfiles`-dependent options there breaks those users. Placing staticfiles-specific behavior in the `staticfiles` command override — which does `super().handle()` into the base — keeps the dependency boundary intact. Adding new lifecycle hooks or parameters to `runserver` as a side effect of unrelated changes forces third-party subclasses to adapt and risks breaking them without the maintainers' intent.
<!-- rule:6029 -->
- Apply behavior to both the immediate path and the `run_on_commit.append` deferred path — including the deferred runner (`run_and_clear_commit_hooks`) — and capture execution-time context at registration time, not at callback invocation. — It prevents divergence between immediate execution and deferred registration and avoids stale/incorrect context when callbacks fire after the transaction commits.

<!-- rule:3336 -->
- Pass a unique, descriptive `key_salt` (e.g. `'staticfiles_json'`) to every `salted_hmac` call — placeholder salts like `'dummy'` let derived values collide or be reused across contexts. — `salted_hmac` derives values that are only safe as long as their salt uniquely namespaces their purpose; reusing a placeholder salt across call sites means values derived for one context can be accidentally accepted in another.
<!-- rule:819 -->
- Keep single-object APIs as thin wrappers over their new bulk counterparts — preserves backward compatibility for existing callers — Keeping the single-object API as a thin delegate to the bulk API and sharing the legacy conversions prevents existing callers from seeing changed defaults or value handling and keeps the two paths from silently diverging.
  Have the bulk implementation reuse the wrapper's conversions/representations and accept caller-supplied params as-is — prevents silent behavior changes and divergent value handling between the two paths
<!-- rule:5886 -->
- Apply signature changes to every sibling factory (`modelformset_factory`, `inlineformset_factory`) with the same default, and update each affected API reference page — callers switching between factories in `django/forms/formsets.py` and `django/forms/models.py` expect identical arguments, so any drift only surfaces as a runtime `TypeError`. — If a new keyword argument is added to one factory but not its siblings, callers who switch between `modelformset_factory`, `inlineformset_factory`, and `formset_factory` hit an unexpected `TypeError` at runtime instead of a documented, consistent API.
<!-- rule:5718 -->
- Preserve doubled-`%` escaping workarounds in every function taking `params`, even when `params` is empty — inconsistent handling silently breaks existing SQL strings — Callers already pass pre-escaped SQL like `LIKE '%%foo%%'` to work around parameter substitution. If some functions unescape or re-interpret `%` when `params` is `None`/empty and others don't, the same query string behaves differently depending on which function it flows through, producing hard-to-trace SQL errors. Applying the identical compatibility path across all `params`-accepting functions keeps old call sites working after substitution changes.
<!-- rule:2612 -->
- Configure decorators via the decorator's own call arguments, not by adding matching params to the decorated function — new params change the wrapped function's signature and break every other call site. — Decorator-wide settings are reusable across call sites; pushing them into the function signature makes each call site pass config it shouldn't own and silently breaks existing callers.
<!-- rule:5632 -->
- Thread an optional variant param through existing parameter-building methods rather than wrapping them — each method derives its own value to keep derivation logic in one place. — Single source of truth for derived values; wrappers and aliases duplicate derivation logic and drift when the original method changes.

  Avoid wrapper methods that override a returned dict or alias another parameter's value — they duplicate derivation logic and silently drift when the original changes.
<!-- rule:2284 -->
- In `django/db/models/sql/query.py` + `compiler.py`, resolve related fields with the same `select_mask` as regular fields: pass `select_mask` into `select_related_descend`, key related masks by the field object from `opts.get_field(...)` (not the remote field attr), and set `related_select_mask = select_mask.get(related_object) or {}` only after inclusion succeeds. — These two files must stay in sync: `query.py` builds the masks `compiler.py` consumes in `get_related_klass_infos`. Keying related masks by the remote field attribute instead of the local field object from `opts.get_field(...)` produces lookups that miss, and updating only one of `only`/`defer` makes the two paths disagree about which related fields to load.

<!-- rule:1554 -->
- Expose per-query behavior via chainable `QuerySet` methods/hooks (mirroring `QuerySet.using()`) — global defaults and context managers break `Manager.get_queryset()`, `ListView.queryset`, forms, and serializers that build querysets declaratively and can't reach runtime context. — `Manager.get_queryset()`, `ListView.queryset`, forms, and serializers construct querysets outside any ambient context; only chainable `QuerySet` methods like `QuerySet.using()` travel with the queryset. Add a module-level default only as an additive convenience after strong consensus — never as the primary API.
<!-- rule:2315 -->
- `pk` orders every composite primary key field ascending, `-pk` every field descending — they must be exact reverses. Applying direction to only some fields breaks the inverse users expect when flipping the shorthand. — Users treat `-pk` as a mirror of `pk`; if sort direction only applies to some member fields, the two orderings aren't reverses of each other and query results silently diverge from intent.
<!-- rule:4937 -->
- Match descriptor classes to a field's declared cardinality: `one_to_many=True` fields (including `GenericRelation` and `empty_join.StartsWithRelation`) must use `ReverseOneToManyDescriptor`/`OneToManyDescriptor`, never `ReverseManyToOneDescriptor`/`ForwardManyToOneDescriptor` — many-to-one descriptors route through reverse-manager APIs like `create_reverse_many_to_one_manager`, which are incompatible with one-to-many relations and fail at runtime. — Preserves the ORM invariant that descriptor class tracks cardinality; mismatches invoke reverse-manager machinery on the wrong relation type.
<!-- rule:932 -->
- In reversible migration operations, resolve models from the direction-appropriate state: `to_state` in `database_forwards`, `from_state` in `database_backwards` — the wrong state resolves an outdated model schema when a migration is reversed. — Each direction must operate against the schema as it exists at that point in history. Using `from_state` during `database_forwards` (or vice versa) looks up the model with the wrong field set, so reverse migrations silently mutate or drop the wrong columns/constraints.
<!-- rule:4351 -->
- Use `django.http.FileResponse` to serve files in views — it streams from disk instead of buffering the whole file in memory. — Reading a file into `bytes` and passing it to `HttpResponse` loads the entire file into memory per request, which is wasteful or fatal for large files; `FileResponse` streams it in chunks and sets headers like `Content-Length` and `Content-Disposition` for you.
<!-- rule:5430 -->
- Don't subclass `TemplateView` when you need rendering but not GET — compose `ContextMixin` + `TemplateResponseMixin` + `View`, or remove `'get'` from `http_method_names`; otherwise GET requests silently succeed where the view was meant to be non-GET (POST-only, etc.). — Mixing in `TemplateView` pulls in `get()`, which no-ops the `http_method_names` restriction and causes side-effect endpoints (POST-only actions) to also respond to GET requests.

<!-- rule:5566 -->
- Read response headers in tests with `response.get('Header-Name')`, not `response.headers['Header-Name']` — the documented `HttpResponse` API and its case-insensitive lookup — Both patterns use Django's public `HttpResponse` API instead of bypassing it: `response.headers[...]` raises `KeyError` on a missing header (masking the actual assertion failure) and skips Django's case-insensitive handling, while `assertIsNone(response.get(..., None))` conflates "header absent" with "header present but set to `None`".
  - Assert header absence with `assertFalse(response.has_header('Header-Name'))`, not `assertIsNone(response.get('Header-Name', None))` — `has_header` is the documented check and states the intent directly
<!-- rule:5020 -->
- Preserve rendered DOM structure and element nesting in `django/forms/templates/django/forms/*.html` (e.g. `ul.html`, `table.html`) — never adjust `tests/forms_tests/tests/test_forms.py` to accept a moved/nested element — The exact HTML that Django forms render is a public contract downstream projects rely on; adding conditionals/loops to a template to keep the output byte-identical is fine, but changing the nesting breaks users' CSS/JS selectors and silently weakens the test suite's coverage.
<!-- rule:6189 -->
- Adapt building & debugging of widgets in the Django form widgets and follow strictly the principles of the reference tests. — This is needed because the rules of ...
<!-- rule:5777 -->
- Override `__eq__` structurally on parser/DOM node classes — comparing via `__str__` or serialized output is brittle and couples equality to formatting — Structural equality on parser/DOM nodes is more reliable than string/serialized comparison, which breaks on formatting changes. Pushing input canonicalization to parse time means `__eq__` only needs to compare normalized attributes and children, and the `isinstance` + `NotImplemented` pattern keeps subclass comparisons honest while letting Python resolve reflected binary operations correctly.
  - Canonicalize equivalent input forms at parse time (e.g., valueless attributes to name/value pairs) — keeps representation differences out of `__eq__` so it only compares normalized attributes and children
  - Guard `__eq__` with `isinstance(other, self.__class__)` and return `NotImplemented` for incompatible types — lets Python fall back to reflected comparison instead of raising or silently mismatching subclasses
  - Do not paper over representation differences inside equality methods — that hides parse-time normalization bugs and splits the canonicalization logic
<!-- rule:5913 -->
- Return a sorted list from `__dir__()`, e.g. `sorted(set(super().__dir__()).union(dir(target)))` — the data model requires a list/sequence, and returning a `set` breaks callers like `dir()` and `help()` that expect ordering and list semantics. — Returning a set from `__dir__` violates the Python data model contract, which specifies a list/sequence — consumers such as `dir()`, `help()`, and REPL tab-completion may reorder, deduplicate, or fail to display entries correctly. Sorting also gives deterministic output across calls.
<!-- rule:5645 -->
- Define callables on Django model/expression classes as module-level functions or `@staticmethod`s, never lambdas or closures — Django routinely pickles expressions, querysets, and rows, so unpicklable callables (e.g. a locally-scoped converter returned by `cached_property`) fail at runtime with `PicklingError`. — Django pickles ORM expressions, querysets, and rows during caching, async boundaries, and multiprocessing; lambdas and local closures cannot be pickled, so they break serialization at runtime.
<!-- rule:5574 -->
- Accept plain `dict` in public constructor params, wrap internally — callers shouldn't need internal types like `ResponseHeaders` — Requiring the internal wrapper (e.g. `ResponseHeaders`) leaks an implementation detail into the public API, forcing callers to import it and breaking them if the wrapper changes. Taking a `dict` and wrapping inside keeps the constructor ergonomic and the internal mapping type swappable.
<!-- rule:1793 -->
- Name `@property` getters with noun phrases (`page_range`, `num_pages`); expose work as verb-named methods (`render()`, `get_html()`) — properties imply cheap attribute access, so hiding computation or side effects behind them misleads callers. — In Python, `@property` reads like attribute access, so callers expect it to be cheap and side-effect-free; a noun-phrase name makes that contract obvious, while a verb-named method signals that work (rendering, HTML generation) is being done.
<!-- rule:5930 -->
- String-parsing helpers return decomposed components (`(name, sign, offset)`), not a recombined string plus index — callers in the SQLite, PostgreSQL, MySQL, and Oracle backends would otherwise re-slice and re-parse the original value themselves. — Recombining parsed parts forces every caller — the four DB backends plus `django/utils/dateparse.py` consumers — to duplicate slicing/parsing logic, which drifts and breaks when the composite format changes. <!-- rule:4576 -->
- For mutable result objects like `TaskResult`, hash/compare on stable `id` plus mutable state fields (`last_attempted_at`, and `status` only if it can't change independently). — Prevents stale results with the same id but different state from comparing equal, which would break caching, sets, and deduplication in `django/tasks`.
<!-- rule:4605 -->
- Test priority-queue ordering on the queued `TaskResult`/`TasksResults` objects and give them rich comparison (`__lt__`, etc.) over their ordering fields — the callable `Task` definition lacks those fields, so sorting it makes ordering tests pass without exercising real queue behavior. — Priority-queue semantics live on the queued job object (`TaskResult`/`TasksResults`), which carries the ordering fields and implements rich comparison against them. Ordering tests and implementations that operate on the callable `Task` definition instead will sort by the wrong thing and can pass while the actual queue runs in the wrong order. This applies across `tests/tasks/test_tasks.py` and the task execution module, so it's placed at the repository root where assistants working anywhere in the repo will see it.
<!-- rule:3577 -->
- Expose JSON path compilation/formatting as overridable hooks on backend classes (`django/db/backends/base`, `django/db/models/fields/json.py`) — alternate backends can supply their own syntax without patching core — Third-party and alternate backends need to emit backend-specific JSON path syntax; overridable hooks with a generic base implementation let them do so without forking Django's ORM internals or diverging from the `JSONField` API.
<!-- rule:6056 -->
- Return backend-specific failure messages from `check_*`/`ops` capability hooks (`None`/`False` on success) rather than raising — keeps messages in the return contract so callers build `checks.Error` without `try`/`except` or `exception.args[0]`. — Callers then convert hook results directly into `checks.Error`, avoiding brittle exception-inspection and keeping custom backend messages part of the documented API.
<!-- rule:1519 -->
- Expose `can_handle(content_type)` (or `matches`) on each parser/handler and call it from dispatch — avoids hardcoded `content_type == parser.media_type` comparisons that block handler-specific matching — Handlers can then bring arbitrary matching logic (wildcards, prefixes, multi-type support) without requiring changes to the dispatch code.
<!-- rule:2151 -->
- Expose template-tag discovery as `get_template_tag_modules()` on `django/template/backends/django.py`; reuse it from `django/core/checks/templates.py` and `get_installed_libraries()` instead of re-iterating `candidates`/`import_module`/`get_package_libraries`. — The rule consolidates template-tag module discovery behind a single hook on the Django template backend, so system checks and `get_installed_libraries()` see the same set of tag modules. Duplicating the `candidates`/`import_module`/`get_package_libraries` loop lets the two paths drift, causing checks to miss or misreport tag libraries that the backend actually loads.
    - Why: a single discovery hook keeps system checks and installed-library listing in sync — duplicated iteration silently diverges when tag discovery changes.
<!-- rule:6194 -->
- Isolate temporary models for schema/migration work in a local `AppCache` (or an unregistered clone with `auto_register = False`) — never mutate or swap the global `AppCache`, since global mutation leaks model state across apps and tests. — Temporary models (`django/db/models/`, `django/db/backends/sqlite3/`) must not leave residue in the global `AppCache`: mutating or swapping it leaks model definitions across apps and test runs, and the save/restore and deep-copy workarounds mask that leak rather than contain it.

  - Only introduce dynamic cache wrappers in `django/db/models/` when code genuinely needs to register, replace, or expose an alternate model set through the global cache.
<!-- rule:3208 -->
- Defer enqueue-on-commit with `transaction.on_commit(partial(tasks.enqueue, ...), alias)` instead of an `ENQUEUE_ON_COMMIT` bool in `django/tasks/backends`; accept `using`/`on_commit_using` (or `TASKS_ENQUEUE_DATABASE`) defaulting to the default DB — a plain `bool` binds only the default connection, silently dropping enqueues on other `DATABASES` aliases. — Django's `transaction.on_commit` already accepts an `alias`, so reusing it keeps the enqueue tied to the transaction that triggered it. A task-level boolean can only capture the default connection, so multi-database projects lose enqueues (or fire them against the wrong DB) whenever the write happens on a non-default alias.
<!-- rule:5981 -->
- Route all file creation through the shared `self.written_files` tracker — not per-branch formatting — so no write path silently skips post-processing like formatting. — Management commands (e.g. `django/core/management/commands/makemigrations.py`) have multiple branches that emit files; adding formatting or post-processing inside each branch means new branches silently bypass it. A single tracking mechanism (`self.written_files`) guarantees every generated file is processed uniformly.
<!-- rule:5731 -->
- Subclass the archive class (e.g. `zipfile.ZipFile`) and override `write` to `writestr` a single member named from the output filename for disk-bound compressed output — preserves the standard `write(path)` API; reuse or uniquify arcnames on repeat writes to avoid `Duplicate name` warnings and unintended archive contents — Subclassing keeps the familiar `write(path)` call working for disk-bound (non-streamed) compressed output; emitting one `writestr` member derived from the output filename matches user expectations, while reuse/uniquify logic on repeated writes prevents `Duplicate name` `UserWarning`s and corrupt/unexpected archive contents.
<!-- rule:3963 -->
- Pair each visibility-toggle button with `aria-controls="${controlledElement.id}"`, and on close focus the trigger via `[aria-controls="${controlledElement.id}"]` — the ARIA attribute is the single source of truth, so focus restoration stays correct without manually tracking the previously focused element (which easily goes stale). — The `aria-controls` attribute already encodes the trigger→target relationship, so reusing it to locate the trigger on close keeps the association in one place; storing a separate "last focused" reference drifts out of sync when the controlled element or its trigger changes, dropping focus onto the wrong node and breaking keyboard users.
<!-- rule:764 -->
- Don't pass `distinct=True` to aggregates over already-unique columns like `Count('id')` — the `DISTINCT` is redundant, harms query clarity, and misleads readers into thinking the underlying data isn't unique. — ### Why this matters
Passing `distinct=True` to an aggregate over a column that is already unique (e.g., a primary key) produces a redundant `DISTINCT` clause. It silently obscures the fact that the column is unique and can suggest to future maintainers that the query needs deduplication when it does not.

### What to do instead
- Omit `distinct=True` when aggregating a column you know is unique — e.g., `Count('id')`, `Sum('pk')`. The uniqueness of the column already guarantees one row per value.
- Reserve `distinct=True` for aggregates over columns that can legitimately repeat values, so the flag signals real deduplication intent.

### Scope
Applies to all ORM aggregate calls in the codebase, not just `tests/expressions/tests.py` where the pattern was first observed.
<!-- rule:2110 -->
- When preserving a renamed public function under its old name, alias with `old_name = new_name` — a self-assignment (`old_name = old_name`) is a silent no-op that leaves existing callers of the old name broken at import/call time. — A self-assignment evaluates the RHS from the same name, so the old symbol either stays undefined (raising `NameError` at import) or keeps pointing at the removed function, breaking downstream code that still imports the old name. Pointing `old_name` at `new_name` keeps the deprecated entry point live while the new canonical name is adopted.
<!-- rule:617 -->
- Restrict aggregate `filter` to `Q` objects, boolean expressions, or lookups — `When(self.filter, ...)` needs a compilable condition, and provider-specific options like GIS `tolerance` must be validated and excluded separately rather than relying on source-field extraction. — reason kept intern within rule text
<!-- rule:1592 -->
- Detect next-page existence with `len(page)` or a `per_page + 1` fetch, not `count()`/`exists()` — exact counts on large tables are O(n), and pagination/autocomplete UIs only need to know if another page exists, so avoid the extra latency. — The `count()`/`exists()` avoid is only a performance concern: exact counting on large tables is O(n) and adds latency that next-page detection doesn't need. Using the already-evaluated page length or over-fetching `per_page + 1` rows answers the same question cheaply and keeps pagination/autocomplete responsive on large datasets.
<!-- rule:6427 -->
- Represent content-type-less permissions as `None` in the `ctypes` set and `(None, perm)` in `searched_perms` — `ctypes.add()` takes a single element, so passing a permission tuple breaks the set contract. — Preserves the project-specific contract for null-content-type permissions in `django/contrib/auth` — `ctypes` is a `set`, so `ctypes.add((None, perm))` would insert a tuple element instead of `None`, producing inconsistent stringification. The test requirement pins the `None.{code}` format so all three permission-lookup backends stay aligned.
  - Cover null-`content_type` perms in tests through `get_all_permissions`/`get_group_permissions`/`get_user_permissions`, asserting they stringify as `None.{code}` — keeps all auth backends handling the same edge case.
<!-- rule:6428 -->
- Override `as_sqlite` for SpatiaLite math functions (`LOG`, `ATan2`) in `django/db/models/functions/math.py` — reverse `(x, y)` args and cast non-float inputs to `FloatField` to match SpatiaLite's semantics. — SpatiaLite swaps argument order and rejects non-float inputs, so the PostgreSQL/MySQL formula produces wrong SQL; tests in `tests/db_functions/math/` must assert SpatiaLite-specific results or they'll pass against the wrong reference.
<!-- rule:5754 -->
- Short-circuit `str`/`bytes` and lazy-proxy types before recursing in type-dispatching normalization helpers — without this, a string is treated as an iterable and normalized character-by-character, silently corrupting values that flow through the helper. — Strings are iterable, so a recursive dispatcher that only checks `isinstance(x, Iterable)` will descend into them and rebuild values char-by-char; lazy proxies (e.g. `Promise`-style or Django's lazy translation objects) likewise masquerade as other types and get mis-dispatched. Ordering these checks first keeps normalization idempotent and correct for any caller passing user-supplied strings or deferred values.
<!-- rule:5753 -->
- Push `isinstance(..., Mapping)` guards into shared normalization/iteration helpers, not call sites — but keep them at call sites for generators, where the branch would force an extra `yield from` pass over mappings. — Helper invoked from multiple sites: hoisting removes duplicated type-dispatch branches. Generators are the exception — wrapping the guard inside means every mapping takes a redundant yield-from hop.
<!-- rule:6089 -->
- Consume the normalizing accessor (e.g., `streaming_content`, which applies `make_bytes`) instead of the raw `_iterator` — re-normalizing in fallback/conversion code double-encodes values. — Accessors like `streaming_content` already wrap `_iterator` with the needed normalization, so touching the raw iterator and normalizing again corrupts bytes/choices and diverges from what callers of the public API receive (seen in `django/utils/choices.py`, `django/http/response.py`, `django/forms/models.py`).

## Error Handling

<!-- rule:2334 -->
- Name the variable/identifier that actually triggered an exception in its message — never reconstruct names by parsing source lines or walking the call stack (e.g. `inspect`, `traceback` introspection) — reconstructed names go stale under refactors, differ across Python versions, and mislead anyone debugging `db`, `utils`, `core`, `test`, or `urls` errors; use the real loop variable, format specifier, or input token instead. — Derived identifiers are guesses derived from unstable artifacts (source text, call frames) and silently become wrong when code is refactored, optimized, or run on another interpreter — producing exception messages that point at the wrong name. Referencing the actual variable, loop variable, format specifier, or input token always yields an accurate, immediately actionable message across all of Django's modules.
<!-- rule:3244 -->
- Allowlist `dict`/`list`/`tuple` before `json.dumps`; let `TypeError` propagate instead of swallowing it — silently stringifying non-serializable values (as in `django/test/client.py` and `django/forms/utils.py` JSON helpers) hides bugs and makes failures undebuggable — Explicit type checks mean callers get a real `TypeError` for unsupported values rather than a `str()`-coerced payload, so bad input fails at the source.
<!-- rule:4129 -->
- Use `multiprocessing.Pool` as a context manager or a `finally` cleanup path so the pool is terminated on every exit path before `pool.join()` — leaked pools hang workers and exhaust file descriptors on error. — Ensures deterministic cleanup when exceptions propagate (e.g. in `django/test/runner.py`, `tests/db_utils/tests.py`); also drop redundant `pool.close()` when using `with`, since the context manager already closes it.
<!-- rule:1332 -->
- In `__init__`, raise `ValueError` for invalid or incompatible constructor arguments — Surfaces misconfigurations at construction time rather than deep inside rendering/execution/accessor methods, so failures are clear and local. Applies to constrained option sets (`index_type`), mutually exclusive options (`include`+`spgist`, `deduplicate_items`+`include`), required companion args (`conditions` without `name`), and pluggable settings (a custom `AutoField` subclass). Don't defer the check or fall back to `assert`/`TypeError`; add tests asserting the `ValueError` for each invalid combination.
<!-- rule:684 -->
- Raise `CommandError` at the management-command layer for user-facing failures instead of `sys.exit()` or writing to stderr — Django renders `CommandError` as a clean message, while raw exits bypass that handling. — Django management commands scattered across `django/core/management/commands`, `django/contrib/auth/management/commands`, and `django/contrib/staticfiles` share the same error-handling contract: the command boundary owns user-facing error reporting. `CommandError` produces a clean, consistent message and a stable exit status; `sys.exit()` mid-command and raw stderr writes do not. Library code raising `CommandError` would couple it to the CLI. Letting `subprocess.CalledProcessError` escape shows users an unhelpful traceback and loses the external tool's stderr; hardcoding the exit status breaks callers and CI that key off the tool's real code. Tests asserting `CommandError` exercise the real error path, while patching `sys.exit` verifies only the mock.
  - Keep reusable backend/library code raising its own domain exceptions and let the command boundary convert them to `CommandError` — library code stays usable outside CLI contexts.
  - Catch `subprocess.CalledProcessError` in commands that run external tools and re-raise as `CommandError` including the failed command and exit status, preserving the tool's stderr — keeps raw tracebacks away from users.
  - Pass the subprocess `returncode` to `sys.exit` rather than a hardcoded status — preserves the external tool's exit code for callers and CI.
  - Assert `CommandError` in management-command error-path tests instead of monkeypatching `sys.exit` — tests the actual failure path rather than a mock.
<!-- rule:1789 -->
- Run request cleanup on every exit path via `try`/`finally` or on the request object in `django/core/handlers/asgi.py` and `django/core/servers/basehttp.py` — otherwise early return, exception, client disconnect, or cancellation skips cleanup and leaks the connection. — The category `error_handling` and the rule's emphasis on safe connection reuse on abnormal exits place this guidance in the repository-root `AGENTS.md`, where every Django request-handling path (`django/core/handlers/asgi.py`, `django/core/servers/basehttp.py`, `LimitedStream` handling) can find it.
  - Drain the remaining request body before `body_file.close()`: loop `read()` until empty, or use an `exhaust()`/`discard()` helper that bypasses `LimitedStream`'s limit and catches `IOError` — a bounded single read leaves the stream unread, and later reads hit a closed body file, breaking safe connection reuse.
<!-- rule:3008 -->
- Errors/hints about invalid options must list valid choices in a `"Choices are: ..."` clause, sourced from the canonical list — stale duplicates of an option set drift and mislead users about what the code accepts. — Hardcoded choice lists silently diverge from what the code honors (including options inherited via `super()`), so users see misleading errors. Derive the list at the point of error — via a shared helper like `django.utils.text.get_text_list` or a deterministic join (e.g. `', '.join(str(e) for e in ...)` for containers like `ErrorList`, or `sorted(...)` for names) — and order by commonality (most common first) so the likely fix appears first. Avoid dead fallbacks such as `"All"` that never trigger and hide real choices.
<!-- rule:3432 -->
- Initialize variables before `try` and wrap only statements that can fail — variables assigned inside `try` are unbound in `except`/`finally` if an early statement raises (`UnboundLocalError`), and `locals()`/`globals()` guards hide the bug instead of fixing it. — When the first statement in a `try` raises, any variable the `finally`/`except` handler reads may never have been assigned, so cleanup code crashes with `UnboundLocalError` — masking the original error. Keep assertions and non-raising validation outside the `try` (or in `else` when an `except` exists) so they aren't mistakenly shielded by the handler.
<!-- rule:2346 -->
- In lookup errors, name the failing component and include the offending operand — e.g. LHS/RHS values, and the required key/index structure for transforms. Pick the exception type by which name in the chain failed: field-resolution error with valid choices for a bad leading field, transform-specific error for a bad trailing transform. — Multi-component lookups (`field__transform`) fail ambiguously otherwise — users can't tell whether the field, transform, or operand is wrong. Including values and structure (`composite.py`, `tuple_lookups.py`, `json.py`, `sql/query.py`) makes the failure self-diagnosing without a traceback dive.
<!-- rule:2449 -->
- Enrich exceptions via `BaseException.add_note()` guarded by `PY311`, never by rewriting the message or wrapping — preserves the original exception type and traceback for callers — Rewriting messages or wrapping exceptions breaks type-based handling in downstream code and hides the original traceback; `add_note()` keeps the exception identity while surfacing diagnostics, and gating on `PY311` keeps the code importable on older interpreters. Asserting on `__notes__` verifies the note landed without making tests brittle to wording changes.
  - Bind the exception to a local, call `add_note()` with a subtype-appropriate note, then `raise` it bare — re-raising without args keeps the traceback intact
  - In tests, capture with `assertRaisesMessage(...) as cm:` and assert on `cm.exception.__notes__` under `PY311` — checks the diagnostic without coupling to message text
<!-- rule:459 -->
- Name the specific missing/unsupported dependency and its triggering condition in validation errors — a vague message sends users down the wrong fix path — Ambiguous dependency/configuration error messages and optional-dependency fallback notices obscure whether a validation-helper requirement is unmet because of invalid configuration, a missing fallback module, or an unavailable driver, leaving users and maintainers to debug the wrong dependency. Preserving exception context, distinguishing failure causes, and using the most specific phrasing in the concrete Selenium example and distinct messages keeps each failure tied to the exact missing, unsupported, or optional-bypassed dependency and prevents silent degradation from masking unsupported combinations.
<!-- rule:3940 -->
- Validate reserved separators at the construction boundary: reject `LOOKUP_SEP` in `name`, `__` in ORM annotation aliases, invalid `Variable` syntax in `Variable.__init__` — parser/tokenizer checks miss direct construction (see `django/template/base.py`, `django/db/models/fields/composite.py`, `django/db/models/sql/query.py`), so downstream split sites silently misbehave. — Callers can construct objects without going through the parser, so relying on tokenizer checks alone lets illegal names reach split sites and corrupt downstream behavior.
<!-- rule:1958 -->
- Treat primary keys as implicitly unique in `_alter_field` (`django/db/backends/*/schema.py`): exclude PK fields from removed-unique-constraint detection, keep the existing PK when the altered field is the same field, and drop/rework the old implicit PK when a field becomes PK — otherwise rebuilt tables get duplicate primary keys or redundant unique constraints. Assert with `len(self.get_constraints_for_column(Author, 'uuid')) == 1` in `SchemaTests` (e.g. `tests/schema/tests.py`, `tests/migrations/test_operations.py`). — SQL backends treat primary keys as implicitly unique, so `_alter_field` must mirror that invariant when detecting removed unique constraints, preserving an existing PK, or promoting a field to PK. Missing any of these cases produces rebuilt tables with duplicate primary keys or a redundant unique constraint, which breaks schema correctness silently. The `get_constraints_for_column` regression assertions catch exactly this drift in `SchemaTests`.
<!-- rule:2850 -->
- Guard `None` before iterating optional iterables (`if values:` or `for item in values or []`). — A comprehension still iterates `None` first, so a trailing `or None` cannot prevent a `TypeError` — as seen across `django/test/runner.py`, `django/db/models/fields/__init__.py`, and `tests/serializers/tests.py`.
<!-- rule:3971 -->
- Catch `KeyError` on named lookups and re-raise the module's standard lookup/syntax exception — bare `KeyError` leaks internals and breaks the module's error contract. — If a named lookup lets a bare `KeyError` escape, callers that catch the module's documented lookup/syntax exception (such as template rendering or model option resolution) silently miss it and see an internal dict failure instead, breaking the module's error contract and leaking implementation detail.
<!-- rule:2511 -->
- Let exceptions propagate in commands/scripts — never `try`/`except` that only logs and continues — Swallowed errors exit 0, so CI passes on broken runs (`django/core/management/commands/shell.py`, `scripts/check_migrations.py`).
<!-- rule:3134 -->
- Use `traceback.print_exc(file=<stream>)` over manual `sys.exc_info()` formatting, and make `<stream>` injectable rather than hardcoding `sys.stderr` — testability and consistent diagnostics — Testable/consistent traceback output across dirs like `django/core/management`, `django/tasks`, `django/views`; version-compatible signatures prevent `TypeError` on older Pythons.
    - Pick `traceback` signatures (e.g., `format_exception(exc)` vs `format_exception(etype, value, tb)`) that work across every Python version in the test matrix — mismatches raise `TypeError` on older runtimes
<!-- rule:5095 -->
- Send diagnostics and test results to `sys.stderr`, not `sys.stdout` — keeps stdout clean for piping/redirection, so consumers aren't polluted with error noise. — In Python CLI scripts and test runners, stdout is often redirected or piped to other tools, so mixing diagnostics into it corrupts the data stream. Use direct, idiomatic calls (`print(..., file=sys.stderr)` or `sys.stderr.write(...)`) rather than introducing a custom printer/logging abstraction for simple diagnostics. For test-result output, emit each result to `sys.stderr` and use `os.linesep` when platform-consistent line endings are needed.
<!-- rule:899 -->
- Accumulate recoverable errors in validation/check functions and management commands, then report them together in one pass — users fix every issue in one run instead of re-running per failure. — These functions exist to tell users everything wrong at once; aborting on the first error forces N runs to find N problems. Per-entry messages preserve the identifier/field/item that failed, which string concatenation destroys. Management commands that print as they go (or bail early) bury later errors behind the first one. Internal consistency checks have no user-facing budget, so fail-fast is fine there.
  - Add each invalid entry as its own message entry (e.g. in `core/checks`) rather than concatenating into one string — keeps per-item context and lets output be filtered/grouped.
  - In management commands, collect errors during the processing loop and report them after it, continuing execution — an early `return`/`abort` hides later failures.
  - Document only the user-visible outcome for these commands (errors are reported, execution continues), not implementation details or `versionchanged` notes for minor tweaks.
  - Internal consistency checks that only need the first violation MAY short-circuit — these are not user-facing and a single abort is cheaper.
<!-- rule:3994 -->
- Make dict/mapping lookup failures explicit — raise `KeyError(key)` or a domain-specific exception, never return `None` sentinels — Returning `None` masks bugs and forces callers to guess; a documented exception surfaces the failure at the source. Don't blanket-catch `KeyError`/`pass` — that hides real errors. Validate or catch `None`/non-mapping inputs (`TypeError`/`AttributeError`) too, since lookups assume dicts and non-mapping callers crash unpredictably. Keep generic lookup utilities domain-agnostic and test non-mapping inputs to catch regressions in shared helpers (e.g., migration/template/translation lookups).
<!-- rule:5105 -->
- Thread calling context (e.g. an `operation_name` param) into shared/extracted helpers so their error messages name the operation that failed — a generic deep-helper message is unusable at the call site — Error messages from deeply shared helpers lose all context, so callers can't tell what operation failed; pushing feature-specific recovery into shared infra couples it to callers it shouldn't know about.
  - Keep feature-specific recovery/guidance out of reusable infrastructure — shared helpers can't know caller policy, and doing so couples unrelated features
  - When a helper is too broadly shared to thread context (e.g. `names_to_path()`), keep its message generic and add contextual hints only in the feature-specific code path that owns the exception
  - In tests, assert the generic message for shared-helper exceptions and a contextual message only for feature-owned ones — keeps the generic/contextual boundary enforced
<!-- rule:1171 -->
- Use `zip(..., strict=True)` for parallel sequences expected to match lengths; skip it after an explicit length check — Python 3.10+ otherwise truncates silently, hiding length-mismatch bugs — Without `strict=True`, `zip` silently truncates to the shorter sequence, so mismatched parallel data produces wrong results instead of a clear error, while the explicit-length-check exception avoids redundant failures once equality is guaranteed.
<!-- rule:4292 -->
- In `django/db/migrations/**` and commands like `showmigrations.py`, read optional settings/attrs (`MIGRATION_MODULES`, `applied`, `options`) with `getattr`/`.get` + sentinel defaults, or explicit membership checks — distinguishes missing entries from explicitly `None`. — This prevents `TypeError` or `AttributeError` when migration code assumes a value is present, and avoids treating an explicitly disabled migration (`None`) as if it were missing.
  - Never pass `None` into iteration or rely on truthiness for `None`-able migration values; comment non-obvious conditions so disabled-vs-absent state stays clear.
<!-- rule:5452 -->
- Check `.fetchone()` for `None` before indexing: `row = cursor.execute(...).fetchone(); if not row: return ...` — A missing row returns `None`, so `row[0]` raises `TypeError` — and `try/except TypeError` around it masks unrelated bugs (e.g. a bad column type) as "no row found". Truthiness is the intended existence check, matching the rest of Django's DB code.
<!-- rule:3794 -->
- Explicitly `aclose()` resource-owning async iterators/generators — GC never guarantees cleanup, leaking file handles and connections — Resource-owning async iterators (DB cursors, HTTP bodies, ASGI streams) hold file descriptors and network connections that GC won't promptly release; missing `aclose()` leaks them and breaks the version compatibility matrix across `django/db/models/query.py`, `django/core/handlers/asgi.py`, and `django/http/response.py`.
  - Encapsulate cleanup in the owner: `__aenter__` returns `aiter(self)` (store it), `__aexit__` `await`s `aclose()` on the stored iterator
  - Without an owner, wrap the iterator via `contextlib.aclosing(...)` in the same `async with`: `async with it as v, contextlib.aclosing(v):`
  - Once the owner adopts its async context manager, delete the now-redundant `aclosing()` wrapper and its explanatory comment at call sites
  - On Python <3.10, use the local `aclosing` backport in `django/utils/asyncio.py` — `contextlib.aclosing` doesn't exist there
<!-- rule:5064 -->
- Access optional keys via `.get(key)` / `.get(key, default)` in form code, but index mandatory fields and catch `KeyError` to raise a descriptive error naming the missing `<field>` and `<Form>` (e.g., `'<field> cannot be found in <Form>.Meta.fields'`) — a bare `KeyError` hides which form and field failed. — Applies to Django forms, formsets, and their tests. Optional keys like `form.cleaned_data` and `widget.attrs` may legitimately be absent, so `.get` avoids spurious failures; required fields must exist, and a wrapped `KeyError` with the field and form name turns an opaque crash into an actionable message. Consistent handling across `django/contrib/auth`, `tests/auth_tests`, and docs keeps form errors debuggable everywhere.
<!-- rule:3897 -->
- Route all response/exception logging through the shared response-logging utility in `django/utils/log.py` — it picks the level from the status class (`error` for 4xx/5xx, `info` for success/redirect) and dedupes at the response/event level, so direct `logger` calls in `django/core/handlers/exception.py` or `django/core/servers/basehttp.py` would log the same response twice or at the wrong level. — The shared utility owns level selection and deduplication; bypassing it in `django/core/handlers/exception.py`, `django/core/servers/basehttp.py`, or similar call sites reintroduces duplicate log lines and incorrect severity for 4xx/5xx responses.
<!-- rule:2990 -->
- Phrase at-most-one/mutually-exclusive constraints precisely in Django user-facing error/warning/hint and `checks` messages: use 'can be specified' (not 'must be specified') for at-most-one, 'cannot be used with/together' for mutually exclusive options, prefer 'either X or Y' and 'cannot have <noun> with both X and Y', and lead with the fix ('use X instead of Y') — sloppy phrasing makes misconfiguration hard to diagnose. — Django's check/validation strings are user-facing diagnostics, so wording carries the fix. Saying 'must be specified' for an at-most-one constraint implies a required setting and sends users the wrong way; inconsistent or indirect phrasing across the surfaces makes debugging misconfigured projects slower. Leading with the recommended action tells the reader what to change, not just what is wrong.
<!-- rule:2783 -->
- ```markdown — Validating raw input—or placing checks before deprecated positional args are assigned—without applying defaults and explicit overrides lets invalid or colliding effective values, including `None`-short-circuited defaults, slip through and surface as runtime bugs or inconsistent behavior across shared Django modules.
  - Validate the resolved/effective value (after defaults, explicit overrides, and deprecated positional args), not the raw input — skipping `None` short-circuits lets invalid or colliding defaults slip through
  - In constructors that assign deprecated positional args to attributes, place validation after the deprecated-arg handling block — checks must run against the final effective values, not pre-assignment state
  ```
<!-- rule:2569 -->
- Coerce only external values to `int()`, inside `try/except (ValueError, TypeError)` with a safe default — Values from request metadata (`CONTENT_LENGTH`), size/count/interval options, or numeric names aren't guaranteed integers — `int()` raises `ValueError` for malformed strings but `TypeError` for wrong types, so catching only `ValueError` still crashes and a bare `except` hides unrelated failures. Where the wrapped API already documents the parameter as an integer (e.g. `pickle` protocol), skip the coercion entirely — redundant string-to-int conversion adds complexity and masks misconfigured callers instead of surfacing them.
<!-- rule:3433 -->
- Revert the following change to comply with the AGENTS.md guideline. — The current `finally` block in `Connection.close()` had a narrowly-scoped, well-understood cleanup path: `_close()` is only called when `self.closed_in_transaction` is false and `self.connection is not None` — a usability guard that ensures cleanup never runs against an already-unusable handle. The proposed retry loop wraps `self._close()` in a broad `except BaseException`, then a nested `try`/`except` that swallows `OperationalError`/`InterfaceError` without `raise` — silently discarding connection/resource errors that callers depend on. It also re-raises the original exception only in the `except Exception` branch, meaning a should-never-happen `BaseException` (e.g. `KeyboardInterrupt`) is caught, retried, and then dropped. Worst of all, the original exception that triggered cleanup is never re-raised if the first `_close()` succeeds on retry, so a failing `close()` can now silence a caller-visible failure. This violates the AGENTS.md rule: cleanup in a `finally` block must not raise a new exception replacing the original and must not silently swallow connection/resource errors; when catching a should-never-happen error only to clean up, always re-raise it after cleanup. Restore the original guard-and-return pattern so cleanup remains silent on already-closed connections and propagates anything unexpected.
  Add preflight checks and retry logic to `Connection.close()` for transient network failures.
  --- Change 1
  File: django/db/backends/base/base.py
  @@ -446,12 +446,22 @@ class BaseDatabaseWrapper:
                   self._close()
               finally:
  -                # self.connection is unusable, no need to call
  -                # self.set_connection(None).
  -                if self.closed_in_transaction:
  -                    return
  -
  -                # Use a scalar to avoid a race condition.
  -                self.closed = self.closed_in_transaction or self.connection is None
  -
  -                self._close()
  +                # Guard: check the connection is still usable before touching it.
  +                if not self.closed_in_transaction and self.connection is not None:
  +                    try:
  +                        self._close()
  +                    except BaseException as exc:
  +                        # Retry once on transient failures.
  +                        try:
  +                            self._close()
  +                        except (OperationalError, InterfaceError):
  +                            # Swallow to guarantee cleanup completes.
  +                            pass
  +                        except Exception:
  +                            raise
  +                        # Note: on the fact that we proceed without re-raising
  +                        # the original exception here.
  +
  +                self.closed = self.closed_in_transaction or self.connection is None
<!-- rule:3709 -->
- Choose log levels by the severity and semantics of the condition, not by convenience: use DEBUG (and INFO) for expected, informational, non-actionable diagnostic events, and reserve WARNING/ERROR for actionable problems such as client misuse, misconfiguration, and potential performance pitfalls (since DEBUG/INFO are suppressed in most default logging configurations). When changing or documenting logging, update logging documentation to explain when each level is emitted for the subsystem, not just list messages by level. — This is a global logging-level policy that applies across the codebase, including Django core and documentation. It is a behavioral convention ('choose log levels by severity/semantics') rather than a rule about one file, entity, module, or a narrowly scoped sync relationship. Root AGENTS.md is the place AI coding assistants will look for repository-wide logging and error-handling standards. Although the rule mentions updating logging docs, that is an instruction within a broader global convention.
<!-- rule:717 -->
- Use inclusive boundary checks for numeric guards: `<= 0` not `== 0`, and `index >= count` not `== count` (last valid index is `count - 1`) — exact matches let negative and out-of-range values slip through — Covers both numeric degenerate-case guards and zero-based index validation; equality checks miss negative counts, oversized indices, and other out-of-range inputs that the same branch should handle.
<!-- rule:3754 -->
- Validate paths by segment, not substrings like `'/./'` — substring checks miss `/..` escapes and mis-flag valid filenames — Why: substring checks on paths are both too loose (a filename like `.ssh` or `foo./bar` can contain the matched substring without being a traversal) and too strict (they can be evaded with trailing `/.` or `/..` sequences). Short-circuit fast paths or boolean conditions that skip normalization quietly reintroduce the vulnerability, so every validity check must funnel through segment-aware normalization and reject on the first failure with `or`.
  - Check trailing `/.` and `/..` explicitly, and apply the same guard to closely-related path variants
  - Reject with `or` across validity checks and fast-path conditions — any shortcut that skips normalization reopens the path-traversal bug
<!-- rule:3267 -->
- Raise `TemplateDoesNotExist`/`ImproperlyConfigured` from `get_template` and generic-view resolution for invalid/missing names — never let incidental `AttributeError`/`TypeError` escape to callers who depend on documented exceptions. — These three behaviors form one contract: template-related failures across `django/template/base.py`, `django/template/backends/django.py`, and `django/views/generic/list.py` must surface as the documented exception types with the template name intact. Callers catch those types and read the name to debug missing templates, so leaking `AttributeError`/`TypeError` or losing the name during suffix parsing turns a clear `TemplateDoesNotExist` into an opaque crash or misleading message.
  Include the offending template name in the error message, falling back to `"the template"` for string-rendered templates — users need to know which template actually failed.
  Preserve the caller-supplied identifier when parsing a partial suffix (e.g. `#...`) — overwriting it with the parsed component hides the template name from later exception messages.
  Don't silently swallow errors from bad user-supplied template names — masks misconfigurations as empty output.
<!-- rule:4747 -->
- Use 'conflicts with ...' or 'collides with ...' (not 'overrides') in alias, annotation, and attribute conflict errors, naming the concrete conflicting identifier. — Signals a real collision to users — 'overrides' implies an intentional replace, obscuring the bug.
<!-- rule:1337 -->
- Raise `NotSupportedError` when a backend can't perform a requested op instead of silently skipping or returning `None` — silent no-ops hide unsupported-feature bugs from callers. — This prevents backend capability gaps from being masked as successful no-ops, ensures `NotSupportedError` is raised only when an unsupported feature is actually requested rather than for unset optional arguments, and lets callers probe `has_xxx` properties without exception handling.
  - Evaluate unset-optional-arg no-op guards before capability checks, so `NotSupportedError` fires only when the feature is actually requested.
  - Return sensible defaults (`False`) from feature-detection properties like `has_xxx` rather than raising — callers reading them shouldn't have to guard every access.
<!-- rule:3334 -->
- Pass arrays to PostgreSQL via driver-native adaptation — a list with `ArrayField(TextField())` or a psycopg2 array adapter — not string-formatted SQL or tuples; the driver handles escaping and typing, while literals break on quotes and tuples adapt as composite row types. — String literals risk quoting/injection bugs and tuples are adapted by psycopg2 as composite types, silently producing the wrong query.
<!-- rule:3541 -->
- Raise `NotImplementedError` naming the value in default branches of operator/lookup-type dispatchers — silent `None` returns or catch-all `else` clauses route unsupported operators into unrelated branches, producing wrong results instead of a clear failure. — Enumerating supported values explicitly and failing loudly on unknown ones surfaces unsupported operators at the call site rather than silently misrouting them into a subset branch.
<!-- rule:4988 -->
- Consume both `min_value` and `max_value` in range fast-paths; return `EmptyResultSet` for any RHS outside either bound — one-sided checks let underflow/overflow lookups hit the DB and match wrong rows. — Backend-provided integer ranges (e.g. `IntegerField` limits, Postgres `ArrayField` bounds) are used to short-circuit lookups before emitting SQL. If the fast-path only checks one bound, a lookup outside the valid range still runs a query and can match rows that should be excluded; asserting `assertNumQueries(0)` at both `min_value - 1` and `max_value + 1` guarantees the shortcut fires on both edges and no DB round-trip leaks through.
  - Test range fast-paths at both bounds (`value__lt=min_value - 1`, `value__gt=max_value + 1`) with `assertNumQueries(0)` each — only-one-side coverage hides gaps where a bound is silently ignored.
<!-- rule:3942 -->
- **Duplicate destinations:** In `collectstatic`, resolve `finders.list` conflicts as duplicate *destinations*, not duplicate sources — check the collected-files mapping before any file handler runs, so each destination is processed once (first-found-wins). Keep overwrite warnings and test descriptions phrased as duplicate destinations to match the implementation. — When two finders resolve to the same destination path, treating them as duplicate sources lets the file handler run twice on one destination, producing double-copy work and misleading overwrite warnings. Derived warnings and tests that describe "duplicate source files" drift from what the collector actually does.
<!-- rule:4553 -->
- In `FileSystemStorage._save()`, `makedirs` the destination, stage the upload in a temp file there, and unlink it if any pre-`file_move_safe()` step fails; mirror the delete in `InMemoryStorage._save()` — orphan temp files leak disk and break move-not-copy semantics. — The temp file must live in the destination directory so `file_move_safe()` can atomically rename it within the same filesystem once it takes over; any earlier failure leaves the temp file behind unless explicitly unlinked, leaking disk. `InMemoryStorage._save()` must delete its temporary upload too, so both storage backends behave like a move (not a copy) and a failed upload leaves no side effects.
<!-- rule:1013 -->
- Don't silently swallow `IntegrityError` from stale/duplicate rows in Django migration/management operations — delete the stale row and retry, or surface a diagnostic (e.g. `RuntimeWarning` pointing to `remove_stale_contenttypes`). Silent passes leave the DB and user in an unrecoverable state. — Django management/migration operations that modify existing rows (e.g. `rename_contenttypes_without_conflict`, auth permission renames) can hit `IntegrityError` when a stale or duplicate target row exists. Swallowing it silently corrupts state and gives users no fix path; the corrected pattern either deletes the stale row and retries, or surfaces a diagnostic (`RuntimeWarning` → `remove_stale_contenttypes`). Non-interactive runs (`--noinput`) must fail loudly rather than only warn, since there's no user to notice. Restoring the original `app_label`/`model` after handling keeps the in-memory object matching the resulting DB row.
  - Pick the conflict resolution mode by execution context in operations exposing `interactive`: prompt when interactive, raise an explicit error under `--noinput`. Warning-only handling hides failures that only manifest later.
  - After catching a row conflict, restore the original `app_label`/`model` on the in-memory object — re-assigning values that were already set leaves it out of sync with the post-conflict DB state.
<!-- rule:1375 -->
- Enforce stream size limits before buffering — reject truncated reads even if they decode/validate — A truncated prefix of encoded stream data can pass decode/validation while silently corrupting downstream state, so overflow must be caught during buffering, not after.
<!-- rule:4794 -->
- ## Combining optional values — Aggregating optional values (e.g., helper results that may be `None`) without filtering causes `TypeError` on join or produces strings like `"None"` in user-facing output; always pairing the filter with an empty-case fallback keeps both cases correct. Applying the same filter where `None` is impossible is noise that masks genuine bugs and misleads readers about the data's shape.
  - Filter `None`/falsy entries before joining, indexing, or concatenating optional values, and give the empty result an explicit fallback — unfiltered joins raise `TypeError` or leak literal `"None"` into output.
  - Join inputs directly when falsy elements aren't possible — defensive filtering hides real bugs and implies optionality the data doesn't have.
<!-- rule:5225 -->
- Validate URL schemes by parsing the value and checking allowlist membership — not hardcoded `http://`/`https://` prefixes — A configured scheme allowlist is authoritative; when `schemes == '__all__'`, fall back to the generic scheme syntax regex instead. Scheme-relative URLs like `//host/path` declare no scheme and resolve using surrounding context, so treat them as insecure. Hard-coding prefixes or ignoring the allowlist either rejects valid schemes or silently accepts unsafe ones.
<!-- rule:3400 -->
- Duck-type operands in operator/combine/dispatch helpers with `getattr()`/`hasattr()` and raise `TypeError` for unsupported combos — These helpers receive heterogeneous operands (expression/lookup subclasses, `Q`-like objects, arbitrary params) without a uniform interface, so assuming `.value`/`.deconstruct` or numeric type lets unexpected shapes surface as confusing `AttributeError`s instead of explicit `TypeError`s.
<!-- rule:5692 -->
- ## Error Handling (global) — Later serializers can't recover `__qualname__` after the fact, so the enrichment must happen where the callable is in hand. Dropping to `__name__` produces ambiguous diagnostics (e.g. `<lambda>` with no enclosing class/module), which blocks debugging of migration/serialization failures.
  - Serialize callables in errors via `__qualname__`/`__module__`, enriching at the layer that holds the callable — bare `__name__` loses scope for lambdas and closures
<!-- rule:493 -->
- Define each CSRF rejection as its own `REASON_*` constant in `django/middleware/csrf.py` and import these into `django/views/csrf.py` instead of re-literaling or overloading another reason's format — prevents the view from drifting out of sync with the middleware's rejection reasons. When a formatted constant needs a shorter prefix, add a shared prefix constant/helper rather than duplicating a substring. — Literal strings or overloaded format strings in the view drift from the middleware's canonical reasons; shared prefix constants keep truncated messages synchronized.
<!-- rule:4442 -->
- Decorate sensitive view entry points (`login`, password-change) with `@sensitive_post_parameters` — keeps POST secrets out of error reports; use the no-arg form when the form class can be overridden, audit sibling views, and document that this opt-in is separate from Django's built-in `settings`/`HttpRequest.META` cleansing. — Django's `SafeExceptionReporterFilter` does not clean POST bodies by default, so undecorated views leak passwords and other sensitive fields into tracebacks and error emails. The no-argument form is required whenever the form class can be overridden so the decorator still targets the actual form fields.
<!-- rule:5078 -->
- In token-auth views and `dispatch` methods, validate the token and any session value it depends on before mutating session state or redirecting — invalid requests must fail safely (sanitize/drop, treat link as invalid, or raise an explicit security exception) rather than create sessions, emit redirects, or crash. — Validating the token and any session value it depends on before mutating session state or redirecting prevents malformed or missing credentials from creating sessions, issuing redirects, or raising unhandled errors in token-authenticated views and dispatch methods. In Django password-reset flows such as `PasswordResetConfirmView`, an explicit `token is not None` check before `token_generator.check_token(user, token)` is redundant because `check_token` already returns False for None, and clearing `INTERNAL_RESET_SESSION_TOKEN` on paths that are unreachable—since the token is stored only after successful validation—adds dead defensive code that can obscure the generator contract and later clear valid reset state if control flow changes.
  - In Django password-reset flows (`PasswordResetConfirmView` and similar), don't add defensive checks the token generator's contract already covers: skip the explicit `token is not None` guard before `token_generator.check_token(user, token)` (it returns `False` for `None`), and don't clear `INTERNAL_RESET_SESSION_TOKEN` on unreachable paths — the token is only stored after successful validation.
<!-- rule:6035 -->
- Don't use `asyncio.sleep(0)` to order tasks or assume one completed/one pending under `asyncio.wait(return_when=FIRST_COMPLETED)` — `sleep(0)` doesn't guarantee scheduling order and `FIRST_COMPLETED` can return multiple done tasks; await order-critical tasks before starting competitors and inspect all tasks' done/pending state before cancel/cleanup. — Prevents race conditions where assumed ordering or completion counts silently break under different event loops or task scheduling.
<!-- rule:4319 -->
- Guard queries against possibly-missing tables in migrations and `django/db/backends` — Check table existence via connection introspection or catch the DB error and no-op — migrations and backend constraint introspection run before other apps' tables exist (e.g. `contenttypes`), so an unguarded read breaks `migrate`.
<!-- rule:5699 -->
- Make token validators, hashing, and sanitization helpers null-safe: validators like `check_token` return `False` for `None`, hash/token calc normalizes nullable attributes (`email`) to `''`, and sanitizers return a fallback token — keeps downstream code free of redundant `None` guards and prevents `None.split()` crashes — A non-null return contract on these helpers lets callers compare/validate without `None` checks; leaking `None` into string ops like `.split()` causes runtime crashes. Always add a regression test covering the null case.
<!-- rule:4516 -->
- Guard success/progress output when a validation loop reports an invalid item — use `continue` or chain the message as an `elif` so the iteration can't emit both a failure and a success for the same item. Keep per-item `try`/`except` (or equivalent) inside the loop so one malformed item doesn't abort checks for later items. — Prevents contradictory output (a warning followed by an "OK"/progress line for the same item), which misleads users and hides real validation failures in `django/core/...` commands. Per-item error handling keeps the loop's coverage complete — later items must still be validated.
<!-- rule:3114 -->
- **Short-circuit empty collections in subset predicates and `all()`/`any()` branches** — return `True` for empty-left ⊆ any-right, `False` for non-empty-left ⊆ empty-right, and explicitly cover (or exclude) empty branches in `all()`/`any()` with regression tests. Vacuous truth (`all([])` is `True`, `any([])` is `False`) silently inverts logic when the empty case is left implicit. — Prevents vacuous-truth bugs where containment checks and `all()`/`any()` aggregate logic silently return the wrong answer on empty collections (an empty left is always a subset; `all([])` is `True`; `any([])` is `False`). Explicit branches plus regression coverage in `django/db/models/sql/` make the intended semantics clear and lock them in.
<!-- rule:4073 -->
- Set worker-affecting state (e.g. result buffering flags) before starting the pool with `pool.imap_unordered(...)`, and signal failures via exceptions or a dedicated channel — not shared sentinel values — Workers capture state at pool start, so late writes are missed; sentinel values written into shared state can be read by concurrently running workers mid-flight, causing hangs or spurious failures.
<!-- rule:5144 -->
- Guard one-time init with a monotonic flag + `threading.Lock` (e.g. app registry `populate()`) — never restore the flag in `finally`, so failed init can't re-enter or race. — Clearing the flag on failure would let another caller re-enter the one-time initialization while the first attempt is only partially complete, causing duplicate side effects, corrupted shared state, or a race that the lock alone cannot prevent.
<!-- rule:2873 -->
- Gate resource cleanup and early returns on the underlying resource's actual usability property (`connection.connection`, `_reader`, etc.), not on secondary state like `connection.in_atomic_block` or proxy attributes — those flags can stay set after the resource is already unusable, so cleanup/guard logic silently no-ops or misfires. — Secondary state (logical flags, unrelated proxy attrs) can remain set while the real resource is gone — checking it makes cleanup and early-return logic skip work that's actually needed. Condition on the property the failing call depends on.
<!-- rule:4690 -->
- Acquire the guarding lock before the initial read in read-modify-write/check-then-act sequences — keeps the read and mutation atomic so concurrent writes can't interleave. — Acquiring the guarding lock before the first read keeps read-modify-write and check-then-act sequences atomic, preventing concurrent writes from interleaving and causing lost updates or mutations based on a stale snapshot, while the inline-comment requirement makes lock-exclusivity, reentrancy, and race-avoidance assumptions explicit so future maintainers can verify that the safety reasoning still holds.
  - Inline-comment race workarounds and any lock exclusivity/reentrancy assumptions — records which race is avoided or why no race exists.
<!-- rule:2185 -->
- Treat error-handling callbacks/hooks as a public API contract: pass exception context consistently, enforce advertised robustness at invocation time (not just registration), and document the delivered error set with a deprecation plan. — Callbacks like `django/conf/urls/__init__.py` handlers or DB backend error hooks are consumed across packages; inconsistent exception context or silently weaker guarantees on invocation cause bugs that surface far from the call site, and undocumented error sets make safe removals impossible.
<!-- rule:3968 -->
- Preserve a method's exception contract when adding preprocessing or optional handling — Callers catch domain-specific exceptions; leaking incidental `str`/`list` errors or raising new preconditions silently breaks them — handle optional configuration conditionally instead.
<!-- rule:3972 -->
- Handle empty, delimiter-only, and missing-delimiter inputs explicitly when parsing delimited strings — never rely on `split()`/`partition()` side effects — `split()`/`partition()` silently return surprising shapes (e.g. `''` → `['']`, `'a'` with no delimiter → `['a']`) that propagate bad values like `django/core/validators.py` and `django/template/backends/django.py` handling. Explicit branches let you raise a clear validation error and ensure tests cover those cases.
<!-- rule:4342 -->
- Use `safe_join`/`validate_file_name` for untrusted paths; avoid ad hoc `os.path.basename` or heading-stripping — Letting `SuspiciousFileOperation` surface keeps the path-traversal security boundary explicit and avoids silently normalizing absolute paths, which would duplicate (and mask) the storage layer's validation.
<!-- rule:3595 -->
- Update `tests/` and `docs/` together when changing `Model.DoesNotExist`/`ValueError` behaviour in Django temporal-ordering helpers (`get_previous_by_*`, `get_next_by_*`, `_get_next_or_previous_by_FIELD`) — split docs and tests drift apart and mislead users about which call sites actually raise. — These helpers raise `Model.DoesNotExist` from multiple locations (`QuerySet.get()`, the helper itself, `OneToOneField` handling), and `ValueError` for both unsaved and deleted instances. Covering both in tests and reference docs — using a shared error-message variable — keeps the documented contract in sync with runtime behaviour, so callers don't write wrong `try`/`except` handlers.
<!-- rule:4748 -->
