- In Django serialization error paths (e.g. `django/db/migrations/serializer.py`, `django/db/models/base.py`), keep the specific message for known failure modes like missing `__module__`, but derive exception names from the exception object (`obj.__name__`) rather than hardcoding them — hardcoded names go stale and send readers chasing the wrong exception. — Hardcoding exception names in serialization error paths drifts out of sync when Django's exceptions are renamed or subclassed, so the error message points readers at an exception that isn't actually being raised.
<!-- rule:2883 -->
- Restore autocommit before any transaction-state-based early returns in `django/db/backends/base/base.py` — keeps connection invariants intact for callers that inspect state afterward — Early returns keyed on transaction state can skip restoration, leaving autocommit in the wrong mode for subsequent operations.
<!-- rule:4151 -->
- Cap validation retries to 1 when `stdin` isn't a TTY in Django management commands (e.g. `changepassword`) — non-interactive input can never satisfy prompts, so retry loops only repeat diagnostics — Commands that validate and re-prompt assume a human on the other end; when `stdin` is piped (CI, scripts, `subprocess`), the input is fixed and every retry re-emits the same error, flooding logs and stalling callers. Bypassing retries or capping them at 1 keeps such commands failing fast and readable.
<!-- rule:2579 -->
- Suffix only positive integers in `humanize` `ordinal` filter/helper — return `0` and negatives unchanged to prevent bogus `"0th"`/`"-1th"` output; assert in `tests/humanize_tests/`. — These rules ensure consistent and predictable behavior of the `humanize` `ordinal` filter/helper across code, tests, and documentation. By only applying ordinal suffixes to positive integers, we avoid confusing output for zero and negative numbers. Asserting this behavior in `tests/humanize_tests/` guards against regressions. Keeping the documentation in `docs/ref/contrib/humanize.txt` aligned with the implementation in `django/contrib/humanize/templatetags/` for non-integer and negative inputs prevents documentation drift, where the docs claim one behavior but the code does another, leading to user confusion and false expectations.
  - Keep `ordinal` docs in `docs/ref/contrib/humanize.txt` synced with `django/contrib/humanize/templatetags/` for non-integer/negative inputs — either implement the documented return-unchanged contract or document actual behavior (floats truncate/format, strings unchanged); prevents implementation, tests, and docs from drifting.
<!-- rule:5607 -->
- Throttle failed logins with non-blocking rate limits keyed on `username_hash:remote_addr`, never a `sleep()` keyed on username alone — blocking sleeps tie up server workers and let unauthenticated attackers DoS a victim username. — Non-blocking, IP-scoped throttling avoids worker starvation and per-victim DoS that blocking sleeps enable; centralizing the 429 mapping in `django/core/handlers/exception.py` keeps security policy out of forms/views; a disable value (`0`) lets deployments turn the feature off cleanly.
  - Raise a dedicated `TooManyRequests` exception on rate-limited auth and map it to HTTP 429 in `django/core/handlers/exception.py` — keeps blocking logic out of `django/contrib/auth/forms.py` and views.
  - Make login throttling configurable with a disable value (`DELAY_AFTER_FAILED_LOGIN = 0`) that skips all checks and cache ops — gives operators an opt-out and lets the setting tune protection scope (`user` vs `user+ip`, default `user+ip` to resist both targeted and distributed attacks).
<!-- rule:2586 -->
- Wrap `urlparse()` calls on untrusted request input in `try`/`except ValueError` and reject the input — malformed URLs like `http://[` (bad IPv6) otherwise raise and surface as 500s/DoS. — Attackers can send malformed URLs (e.g. `HTTP_ORIGIN: http://[`) that make `urlparse()` raise `ValueError`; catching it and rejecting the request prevents unhandled 500 responses and denial-of-service. See `django/middleware/csrf.py` for the pattern.
<!-- rule:5988 -->
- Use `--` before dynamic filenames in `subprocess.run()` — stops user-provided names starting with `-` from being parsed as CLI options — Without the `--` separator, a filename like `-rf` or `--force` gets consumed as a flag by the invoked tool, silently changing its behavior or causing destructive side effects (see the fix in `django/core/management/utils.py`).
<!-- rule:6432 -->
- Escape `"`, `\`, `\r`, `\n` in `Content-Disposition` filenames and other quoted HTTP header parameters — unescaped values terminate the quoted string and let attackers inject extra header content. — HTTP headers are newline-delimited; a filename containing CR/LF or an unbalanced quote can break out of the quoted parameter and smuggle arbitrary headers or body content into the response.
<!-- rule:6344 -->
- Suppress destructive-action warnings only when non-existence is verified — treat unknown/`None`/remote destinations as possibly existing — Fail-safe default: an unverified destination may well hold data, so silencing the prompt risks silent overwrites that the user never confirmed. Checking only when existence is positively disproven keeps destructive commands safe across local and remote storage backends (e.g. `collectstatic`).
<!-- rule:5451 -->
- Short-circuit empty input (`''`, `None`, `[]`) in optional composite fields (`MultiValueField` with `required=False` and `require_all_fields=False`) — return `None` before validating subfields so they don't raise spurious `incomplete`/`required` errors; add tests asserting this behavior. — Optional composite fields must be genuinely optional — without the early return, an empty submission trips each subfield's validation and produces confusing `incomplete`/`required` errors even though the field is not required.
<!-- rule:3758 -->
- Catch `ValidationError`, `ObjectDoesNotExist`, and `ValueError` together when resolving objects in views — don't special-case field types like `UUIDField`. Let the model field coerce and validate user-supplied identifiers (the ORM's job), and translate the resulting `ValidationError` into `Http404`/404. Field-specific branches duplicate ORM logic and drift as field behavior changes. — The ORM already validates and coerces identifiers per field type and raises `ValidationError`, so generic views that special-case types like `UUIDField` duplicate that logic and break when field semantics change. Catching `ValidationError` alongside `ObjectDoesNotExist` and `ValueError` keeps lookups uniform and turns malformed input into a consistent `Http404`/404 instead of a 500.
<!-- rule:5013 -->
- Validate arguments and raise errors at the top of query methods, before any database connection or feature-flag lookup — avoids opening connections or triggering flag evaluation for calls that will fail anyway. — Cheap validation-first ordering means invalid input fails fast without consuming a DB connection or firing a feature-flag check; in methods like those in `django/db/models/query.py` this also keeps side effects (connection acquisition, flag evaluation) from happening on paths that will raise.
<!-- rule:2234 -->
- Write "origin" (not "server") in origin/referer validation error messages — the message should name the value actually being compared, so CSRF/debug output isn't misleading (e.g. `django/middleware/csrf.py`) — Errors that call an origin a "server" misdescribe what was compared, sending users down the wrong path when debugging CSRF/referer failures.
<!-- rule:6114 -->
- Keep each validator's message scoped to its own constraint — don't duplicate min/max bounds handled by sibling validators (see `django/core/validators.py`); fall back to the base message when an extra clause doesn't apply. Prevents misleading or contradictory errors when validators compose. — Duplicate constraints produce confusing, overlapping error messages when validators are composed, and an extended clause that doesn't apply can surface irrelevant failures.
<!-- rule:6359 -->
- Pass diagnostic names explicitly — don't introspect (`Signal.name`, `__name__`, `extract_stack()`) — Introspecting a diagnostic name via `Signal.name`, `__name__`, or `traceback.extract_stack()` either raises `AttributeError` because Django's `Signal` exposes no public `name`, or yields fragile, version-dependent stack-derived identifiers that mislabel diagnostics and break assertion messages, so the name must be passed explicitly or derived from the object's qualified path/`repr`.
  Rationale: Django `Signal` lacks a public `name`, and `traceback.extract_stack()` inside `__init__` is fragile and slow; introspection-based names (seen in `django/dispatch/dispatcher.py`, `django/test/testcases.py`) break across versions and produce crashes or misleading assertion messages. Require the name or use the object's qualified path/`repr`.
<!-- rule:6075 -->
- ## Consuming traceback/debug position APIs — Parsing `format_tb()` text or private traceback helpers breaks across Python versions and mishandling optional `start_column`/`end_column` or guarding them with `sys._xoptions` crashes or adds a private-API dependency when `None` is a normal result under `no_debug_ranges`.
  - Read positions via public structured APIs (`tb.tb_frame.f_code.co_positions()`, `traceback.StackSummary.extract()`, `FrameSummary.start_column`/`end_column`/`colno`) rather than parsing or slicing `traceback.format_tb()` output or private traceback helpers — `format_tb()` text and private helpers can change between Python versions, while these APIs are stable and give structured data.
  - Treat `start_column`/`end_column` as optional and handle `None` explicitly — Python omits column ranges under flags like `-X no_debug_ranges`, so `None` is a normal case, not an error.
  - In `django/views/debug.py`, don't guard column extraction with `sys._xoptions.get("no_debug_ranges")` — plain `None` handling already covers that case, and the extra guard just adds a private-API dependency.
<!-- rule:4848 -->
- Centralize language-code validation in `django/utils/translation/trans_real.py`; reuse it from `django/core/checks/translation.py` and runtime checks like `check_for_language()` — duplicated guards drift, so system checks end up rejecting tags the runtime accepts. Treat `None` and non-strings as invalid via `tag is None or not language_code_re.match(tag)`. — The validation in `django/utils/translation/trans_real.py` is the single source of truth for what a valid language tag is. When `django/core/checks/translation.py` (or any runtime check such as `check_for_language()`) reimplements the guard, the two copies can disagree, producing system-check errors for tags that runtime code happily accepts, or vice versa. The canonical guard must also treat `None` and non-string values as invalid — `tag is None or not language_code_re.match(tag)` — because `language_code_re.match()` raises `TypeError` on non-string input, which would otherwise surface as an unexpected crash instead of a clean validation failure.

## Documentation

<!-- rule:1367 -->
- In timezone/i18n docs, call the `TIME_ZONE`-configured zone the `default time zone` and link to the canonical `default-current-time-zone` ref; call the `activate()`d zone the `current time zone` — canonical terms prevent contradictions between docs. — These docs and examples are read together, so mixed terminology (default vs. current vs. activated, aware vs. naive) and inconsistent identifiers/snippets make readers think behaviors differ when they don't. Reusing names and cross-references keeps the docs self-consistent as the central timezone reference evolves.
  - Prefer `active timezone` over `activated timezone`, and hyphenated `timezone-aware`/`timezone-naive` — matches established Django vocabulary readers already know.
  - Reuse variable names from sibling examples (e.g. `melb`, not `tzinfo`) and `:ref:` cross-references instead of restating `TIME_ZONE` behavior — duplicated explanations drift out of sync with the central docs.
  - Use realistic IANA identifiers (`"America/Chicago"`, `Europe/Paris`) in examples, constants, and fixtures instead of abbreviations like `"EST"`/`CET`/`CEST` — abbreviations are ambiguous and don't map cleanly to real zones.
<!-- rule:1503 -->
- Write HTML/ARIA attributes with exact hyphenated names (`aria-describedby`) and include value assignment when only one value is valid (`aria-invalid="true"`); state required order for order-sensitive attributes like `aria-describedby` and its accessibility effect rather than vague "correct order" phrasing; link to W3C/MDN for non-obvious attributes. — Hyphenated names and explicit values keep docs greppable and copy-pasteable into real markup; order-dependent attributes silently change screen-reader output, so ambiguity in docs propagates into broken accessibility.
<!-- rule:1366 -->
- Document Django date/time behavior against `USE_TZ`/`TIME_ZONE`/backend tz support — cover aware vs naive, `USE_TZ=True`/`False`, UTC fallback, and backends that lack tz support, with concrete examples. — Readers on non-default settings or non-tz backends otherwise get silently wrong expectations from `docs/ref/models/database-functions.txt` and `docs/ref/models/fields.txt`.
<!-- rule:5257 -->
- Update both the `django/utils/dateformat.py` method docstring and the matching `docs/ref/templates/builtins.txt` entry when a date/time format specifier's output changes or is first documented; add a `.. versionchanged::` note under the formats table for user-facing changes — the two references are read independently, so drift silently misleads users. — Keeping `django/utils/dateformat.py` and `docs/ref/templates/builtins.txt` synchronized with accurate, consistent examples prevents users from trusting stale or incorrect documentation and formatting dates/times in ways that do not match Django's actual output.
  - Match each directive's example string to what that directive actually produces (e.g., no seconds in the `TIME_FORMAT` example) and show padding/leading-zero behavior with concrete boundary values — examples that don't match the real output teach users to expect the wrong format.
  - Keep each directive's description accurate and phrased consistently with related directives, retaining non-obvious explanations unless they're inaccurate or redundant — readers scan the table side by side.
<!-- rule:1441 -->
- Comment internal flags and boolean attributes with what they are, when they're set, and what behavior they control — state flags like `_state.adding` are opaque without context — Internal flags and boolean class/instance attributes (e.g. `_state.adding`, `Meta.abstract`, backend `features.*`) encode state-machine behavior that readers can't infer from call sites; a self-contained comment lets them understand the flag without tracing its writer.
<!-- rule:6315 -->
- Comment non-obvious indices (`-1`, nested like `collection[other[-1][0]]`, partition points) — names the selected element, since readers can't infer it; private helpers returning such an index document what begins there. — Non-obvious indices are a common reading trap — a bare `-1` or partitioned index doesn't say which element is intended, so comments prevent misreads and accidental refactors.
<!-- rule:2094 -->
- Sync `docs/ref/contrib/admin/index.txt` whenever `ModelAdmin.search_fields` behavior changes — search docs that drift from actual semantics mislead users — Docs describing `search_fields` with vague phrasing like "words" and without noting that terms are silently skipped on incompatible lookups or treated as implicit `exact` matches on non-text fields drift from the implementation and cause users to misconfigure admin search and file false bug reports.
    - Describe terms as applied only to compatible lookups and skipped elsewhere; say "search terms", not "words"; document non-text fields with inline examples (e.g. implicit `exact` on `child__age`) and call out limitations/workarounds
<!-- rule:1797 -->
- Mirror condition semantics in inline comments — list all accepted types, match operators (`in` vs `is`), and note subclass cases like `bool` being an `int`. Misleading comments let real bugs hide behind plausible-looking docs. — Prevents comments from drifting from the code they describe, which is how false-assumption bugs survive review.
<!-- rule:3526 -->
- Document delegation with a comment explaining *why* the logic lives elsewhere and any relaxed return-type contract — readers otherwise assume the loosened contract leaks from the delegate. — Delegating methods hide their actual behavior behind the callee. Without a note, maintainers assume the returned type is fully enforced by the delegate, and any unforwarded arguments silently change semantics. Placing the comment before the dispatching branch (e.g. the `isinstance`/`if` on the relevant type) rather than inside a single subtype's block keeps the caveat scoped to every subtype, not just the one branch that happened to be written first.
<!-- rule:5256 -->
- Use `precedence`, `earlier` (not `upper`), and `preceded by` (not `proceeded by`) in comments/docs. — `proceeded by` misspells `preceded by` and reads as the opposite direction, while `upper` is ambiguous about ordering — readers may invert the intended code or documentation sequence.
<!-- rule:1523 -->
- Keep `docs/internals/howto-release-django.txt` matched to the current release scripts — stale steps (e.g., `twine upload -s`, the Jenkins 'confirm release' job, project-scoped PyPI tokens) send releasers down dead paths — Release docs describe a workflow that must match what the tooling actually supports. References to decommissioned jobs or removed upload methods cause releasers to attempt steps that no longer work, so replace them with the verified current method and note explicitly where timing is flexible or prep work can be done earlier.
<!-- rule:2048 -->
- Describe the behavior change, not implementation details — `git log` is the primary tool for debugging and review — commit messages that explain what changed (and name specific identifiers/flags) let future readers find regressions without reading the diff. Keep verb tense consistent and cut content that doesn't explain the change.
<!-- rule:1779 -->
- Document non-obvious descriptors/metaprogramming — explain the mechanism inline, including how the implicit argument is supplied when it isn't `self`, and in API docs list the concrete access contexts (from the class, from class methods) and cite the closest stdlib analogue (e.g. `classproperty` ~ `classmethod`); without this, readers can't distinguish intentional magic from a bug — Non-obvious descriptor and metaprogramming patterns (e.g. `classproperty`, metaclass-assigned properties, passing an Enum member as the implicit property argument) are unreadable to anyone who hasn't written them, so callers can't tell how to access them or whether behavior is deliberate. Inline comments explain the mechanism, and API docs referencing the stdlib analogue (e.g. `classproperty` ~ `classmethod`) give readers a familiar mental model.
<!-- rule:2532 -->
- Document new public `BaseCommand` methods in the `Methods` section and attributes in the `Attributes` section of `docs/howto/custom-management-commands.txt`, with purpose and override behavior, and announce the new extension point in `docs/releases/*.txt` — subclassers can't safely override an extension point they don't know is public, and undocumented additions drift from the how-to. — Management command extension points become part of the public API the moment they land in `django/core/management/`; keeping the how-to and release notes in sync is what makes them discoverable and safe to override.
<!-- rule:3636 -->
- Call `to`/`cc`/`bcc`/`reply_to` "address lists"; define their accepted types (strings, `email.headerregistry.Address`) in `docs/topics/email.txt` — one canonical definition avoids drift with `docs/ref/settings.txt` — Django's email docs span two files: `docs/topics/email.txt` (the topic definition) and `docs/ref/settings.txt` (settings reference). Splitting the address-list definition across both invites inconsistent terminology and missed `str(...)` wrapping caveats, which readers only discover when a setting fails at load time. Pointing readers to RFC 5322 or `email.utils.formataddr` for display names keeps the reference pages focused on accepted types rather than duplicating how-to examples.
  - In `docs/ref/settings.txt`, flag each setting where `Address` must be wrapped in `str(...)` — omitting the wrap silently breaks the setting at config-load time
  - Defer construction and display-name examples to the how-to topic; point to RFC 5322 formatted addresses or `email.utils.formataddr` instead — keeps reference pages type-focused and avoids duplicated examples
<!-- rule:6068 -->
- Use `pageshow` — with an inline comment naming the triggering scenario (e.g. browser back nav) — for JS init/state sync that must run after a back-forward cache restore — `DOMContentLoaded`, `load`, and custom `ready()` helpers don't re-fire on bfcache navigation, so init gets silently skipped — Explains why `pageshow` is required and why the comment is needed: `DOMContentLoaded`, `load`, and custom `ready()` helpers don't re-fire when the browser restores a page from the back-forward cache, so relying on them alone leaves initialization and state synchronization partially applied after back/forward navigation.
<!-- rule:2624 -->
- Pass explicit sentinels like `"<<unknown>>"` for unused args in legacy paths — no speculative or reverse config lookups — Reverse-searching config or adding lookup logic for an arg the code never uses adds coupling and hides intent; an obvious placeholder makes the 'unused here' case self-documenting and unlikely to collide with real values.
<!-- rule:4212 -->
- ### Documentation — The original rule bundles two distinct concerns: accurate/symmetric documentation of existing lifecycle signals (with worked examples from `django/` migration hooks like `post_migrate`/`pre_migrate`), and a prohibition on speculative hooks. Splitting them makes each actionable while preserving the project-specific signal names. This scope is global because the behavior spans `django/`, `tests/`, and `docs/`, so it belongs in the repository-root `AGENTS.md`.
  - **Document signals by exact name and firing phase; pair related ones** (e.g., `post_migrate` injecting permissions on model creation ↔ `pre_migrate` updating permissions on renames, including how duplicate/conflicting permissions resolve) — one-sided or vague timing descriptions mislead users about when hooks actually run.
  - **Don't invent speculative complementary hooks** (e.g., a `pre_*` mirroring an existing `post_*`) without a concrete consumer in the codebase — documenting imaginary extensibility points diverges from real behavior and invites broken integrations.
<!-- rule:5485 -->
- Keep `docs/topics/forms/formsets.txt` examples valid under runtime constraints (e.g. `absolute_max >= max_num`, given `DEFAULT_MAX_NUM = 1000`) — stale examples teach broken usage. — Doc examples in `docs/topics/forms/formsets.txt` run through the same validation as `django/forms/formsets.py`, so parameter defaults like `DEFAULT_MAX_NUM = 1000` must satisfy `absolute_max >= max_num`; otherwise readers copy invalid code. When count semantics or `ManagementForm` fields change, the management-form note must name the exact formula (`extra + min_num`) and the options (e.g. `validate_min`) that don't affect displayed count, since readers rely on it to compute form indices.
  Update the "Understanding the management form" note whenever `ManagementForm` count semantics change: state the formula (`extra + min_num`) and clarify that options like `validate_min` don't affect displayed count — keeps docs aligned with `django/forms/formsets.py`.
<!-- rule:2129 -->
- Comment resource ownership at return points — state the target pool or caller cleanup duty; prevents leaks — Resource lifecycle code (e.g., returning pooled connections in `django/db/backends/postgresql/` or releasing resources in `django/db/models/sql/`) is easy to misuse when the owner is implicit — an inline comment makes the invariant explicit so callers don't double-close, leak connections, or assume they own cleanup they don't.
<!-- rule:4145 -->
- Comment why non-obvious type-guards exclude specific types (e.g., `str`/`bytes` in `Iterable` RHS checks in ORM query construction) — Without the concrete failing input (e.g., `["ab"]`) and any existing safeguard (e.g., the related model system check), future readers can't verify whether the exclusion is still needed or safely removable.
<!-- rule:5837 -->
- Comment intentional parsing quirks — discarded data, documented-behavior changes, magic-number adjustments — with the domain rationale and the specific formats/paths kept consistent — Parsing code in modules like `django/utils/dateparse.py` and `django/http/multipartparser.py` often deliberately drops data or tweaks values to match a sibling path; future readers otherwise mistake these for bugs and "fix" them into regressions. Cite the domain reason and the formats/paths being aligned, not incidental implementation details or unrelated checks, so the intent survives refactors.
<!-- rule:3311 -->
- Give dynamically generated and sibling controls descriptive, symmetric accessible labels/`placeholder`s — include the field/item name when duplicate text would collide, and don't label every sibling except the current/selected item (e.g. options in `contrib/admin/static/admin/js/SelectFilter2.js`, rows in `contrib/admin/templates/admin/object_history.html`) — ambiguous or one-sided text leaves screen-reader users unable to tell fields apart. — Ambiguous or asymmetric labelling on dynamically generated controls (e.g. `contrib/admin/static/admin/js/SelectFilter2.js`, `contrib/admin/templates/admin/object_history.html`) makes fields indistinguishable for screen-reader users because accessible names duplicate or skip siblings; including the field/item name and labelling all siblings keeps orientation consistent.
<!-- rule:4417 -->
- Comment non-obvious version/introspection probes (`inspect.signature`, `__code__.co_argcount`) with what they detect and why they're needed — These branches silently guard against Python-version differences and callable-signature drift; without the stated intent, later readers can't tell whether the check is still required or safe to delete.
<!-- rule:6285 -->
- Comment non-obvious parsing/encoding/decoding logic with the *why*, not the *what* — future readers can't reconstruct the reasoning from the code alone. — In modules like `django/core/mail/message.py` and `django/utils/encoding.py`, parsing and encoding choices (e.g. character-set handling) look arbitrary without context, so reviewers and maintainers otherwise risk 'fixing' or reverting deliberate decisions and reintroducing bugs.
<!-- rule:2188 -->
- When documenting type-coercing fields in `docs/ref/models/fields.txt` and `docs/ref/forms/fields.txt`, add a warning that leads with the recommended usage, states whether coercion affects storage, comparison, or both, and cross-references the alternative field that accepts the other type — then mirror the same admonition in the complementary field's docs so the two reference each other — helps users pick the right field and avoid silent type surprises. — Both reference files are consulted together; without symmetric warnings users may store or compare values expecting one type while the field silently coerces them, and won't discover the type-matching alternative field exists.
<!-- rule:4827 -->
- Document request-data population by runtime conditions (was the field actually posted?), not static form/input presence — keep `docs/topics/http/file-uploads.txt` in sync with `django/test/client.py`, since docs that describe only form markup lead users to expect uploads that were never sent. — The docs and `django/test/client.py` describe the same request-data behavior from two angles: static form/input markup vs. what was actually transmitted. When the docs drift or describe only markup, users debugging `cross_file`/upload behavior in tests are misled about whether fields were populated at all — synchronization with the implementation is what makes the docs trustworthy.
<!-- rule:2458 -->
- Document the full set of supported compression formats (`gz`, `bz2`, `lzma`, `xz`, `zip`) for a management command in `docs/ref/django-admin.txt`, and update `docs/releases/` in the same change — a partial list drifts from the command's actual behavior and misleads users. Use plural "formats", prefer bare identifiers over full names with dotted extensions, and include a concrete example like `django-admin dumpdata -o mydata.json.gz`. — Documenting only a subset of accepted formats in `docs/ref/django-admin.txt` makes the reference wrong the moment args are added, and release notes under `docs/releases/` must land together with the reference or users see inconsistent capability claims across docs. Bare identifiers (
<!-- rule:6340 -->
- Explain caching/precomputation in active voice with concrete runtime effects — vague rationales like "for purity" hide when the trade-off can be safely removed — The trade-off between caching and precomputation is a design decision future readers must re-evaluate as the code changes. Stating the concrete effect (e.g., "avoids computation on requests that don't render templates") lets them judge whether the cache is still worth its cost, while subjective justifications leave them unable to tell.
<!-- rule:42 -->
- Write numeric bounds as explicit inequalities (`0.0 ≤ x < 1.0`), not interval notation like `[0.0, 1.0)` — the inclusive/exclusive edge stays unambiguous to readers skimming docs — Interval notation is easy to misread at a glance, and docstring readers can't rely on parsing conventions; spelling out each bound prevents off-by-one interpretation errors in prose and code docs.
<!-- rule:6094 -->
- In middleware docs (`docs/topics/http/middleware.txt`) and code comments (e.g. `django/middleware/locale.py`), keep control flow explicit: write `response = get_response(request)` and `return response` as separate statements, leave a placeholder comment at the response-processing slot, and define 'short-circuit' as returning a response without calling `get_response`. — Middleware examples are the primary reference users copy into their own code; collapsing the call and return into one expression, or using terms like 'short-circuit' without definition, hides the exact hook points where response processing belongs, leading to broken or misplaced middleware. Scope: middleware docs and middleware code comments.
  - Write comments that state intent rather than restating the code — middleware examples are the reference readers copy, so ambiguous control flow or undefined terms hide where new behavior belongs.
<!-- rule:4569 -->
- Keep isort references in sync across `docs/internals/contributing/writing-code/unit-tests.txt`, `coding-style.txt`, and `.pre-commit-config.yaml` — mismatched install specs, CLI flags, or repo URLs ship broken examples and failing hooks — The docs advertise a minimum isort version, so the install spec and CLI flags in every related file must match it; and the pre-commit hook must point at the current repo URL or the hook installation breaks.
<!-- rule:2453 -->
- Match docs to actual runtime behavior — mark attributes writable vs read-only, note overridden/merged attributes (e.g. `Meta` options, `Form`/view attrs), skip guards for attributes the base `__init__` always sets, and avoid subjective qualifiers; inaccurate docstrings and examples become user-facing bugs. — Docstrings, attribute docs, and code examples are the public contract for `django/forms/`, `django/views/`, `django/utils/`, and `docs/ref/` — if they describe attributes as writable when they're read-only, omit reserved/overridden attributes and merging semantics, or hedge behind guards the base initializer never needs, users write code against a contract that doesn't exist at runtime.
<!-- rule:4124 -->
- In `docs/ref/models/querysets.txt`, `docs/topics/db/examples/`, and the `ModelAdmin.get_deleted_objects` docstring, state enabling conditions and extension points before the resulting behavior, and keep cascade-deletion notes next to the relation query they affect — readers can't interpret a delete without first knowing what triggers it — The rule spans multiple locations (`docs/ref/models/querysets.txt`, `docs/topics/db/examples/`, and the `django/contrib/admin/options.py` `ModelAdmin.get_deleted_objects` docstring), so it applies wherever deletion semantics are documented rather than to a single module. Leading with prerequisites (enabling conditions, extension points) and keeping cascade notes adjacent to the relation query they describe prevents readers from misreading deletion behavior as unconditional; a concrete example with the resulting queryset or affected-object count gives verifiable evidence instead of prose the reader must take on faith.
  - Show a concrete delete example that displays the resulting queryset or affected-object count — abstract descriptions of cascade behavior are hard to verify against real output
<!-- rule:2062 -->
- Use canonical spelling and a single capitalization convention for GitHub labels, Trac flags, and tool names across contributor-facing docs — differing case makes duplicate-looking labels that contributors can't match when applying them by hand — Inconsistent spelling, capitalization, or quoting of project metadata such as GitHub labels, Trac flags/properties, and project tool names in contributor-facing docs, PR templates, and messages makes those names ambiguous, so contributors can mistake a label for prose or apply the wrong flag/property. The cost compounds for multi-word names and rendered Trac properties such as `_"Has Patch"_`, where mixed quoted/unquoted or differently cased forms break searches, filters, and the clarity of contributor messages.
  - Quote multi-word label/flag names (e.g. `"Has Patch"`) instead of code-formatting them — backticks imply code/commands, quotes signal a literal label to copy
  - Keep quoting/casing identical across `.github/pull_request_template.md`, `.github/workflows/new_contributor_pr.yml`, and `docs/internals/contributing/triaging-tickets.txt` — mixed forms read as different labels and confuse triagers
  - Render named Trac properties with the project's Markdown emphasis convention, e.g. `_"Has Patch"_` — matches the triage docs so contributors recognize the flag across surfaces
<!-- rule:4038 -->
- In template docs and test fixtures, use `{% templatetag %}` only for short single-delimiter examples; use `{% verbatim %}` for longer literal snippets, and include complete constructs (e.g., both `{% partialdef %}` and `{% endpartialdef %}`) to mirror realistic usage. — Short `{% templatetag %}` chains stay readable, but longer ones become an unreadable escape soup that hides the actual template syntax. `{% verbatim %}` preserves the real markup, and keeping constructs complete (matching `{% partialdef %}` with `{% endpartialdef %}`) means readers and copy-paste users get working examples instead of fragments that fail on paste. Applies to template examples in `docs/` and test fixtures alike.
<!-- rule:5100 -->
- Document secret-key settings with plural wording ("secret keys") when multiple key settings exist — guidance must cover `SECRET_KEY`, `SECRET_KEY_FALLBACKS`, and any related values across `docs/ref/settings.txt`, `django/core/signing.py`, and `django/conf/project_template/.../settings.py-tpl`, so readers applying it to any one setting get wording that fits all of them. — Multi-key settings are documented in several top-level areas of the repo (settings docs, `django/core/signing.py`, and the `project_template` settings template). Singular wording misleads readers into thinking only one key matters, so plural phrasing and an explicit "all entries may be checked / don't add unnecessary ones" note keeps the guidance correct no matter which setting a reader starts from.
  - State that all list entries may be checked and that unnecessary entries should not be added — prevents operators from stuffing extra `SECRET_KEY_FALLBACKS` values under the assumption that only the first is validated.
<!-- rule:3159 -->
- Describe redirect method/body preservation as instructing the *user agent*, not the server or framework — in `redirect()` docstring, `docs/topics/http/shortcuts.txt`, and comments in `django/test/client.py`. Make the redirect handler the grammatical subject, not the status code (e.g., `# Preserve request method post-redirect for 307 and 308 responses.`). Readers otherwise assume preservation is enforced or guaranteed server-side, misdiagnosing client-side behavior. — Redirect method preservation is a client-side contract; phrasing that centers the server or status code implies guarantees the framework does not make, leading users to debug the wrong layer.
<!-- rule:2909 -->
- Keep `docs/ref/request-response.txt` synced with `HttpRequest` method behavior — update the reference when changing `get_host()`/`get_port()` or `USE_X_FORWARDED_HOST`/`USE_X_FORWARDED_PORT`, and preserve existing caveats when refactoring related methods. — The reference is the documented contract for `request.get_host()`/`get_port()` and the `USE_X_FORWARDED_*` settings; stale docs mislead users and dropped caveats hide edge-case behavior. Carrying caveats forward when adding sibling methods keeps the API surface consistent.
<!-- rule:2509 -->
- Update `docs/releases/` for any `PASSWORD_HASHERS`, `django/contrib/auth/hashers.py`, or `django/conf/global_settings.py` default change, stating rationale and security tradeoffs — upgraders must knowingly accept any weaker fallback. — Password hashing weakens silently on upgrade if users don't understand why a lighter default was chosen; release notes are the only place they'll see it.
<!-- rule:410 -->
- In docs and docstrings, state the general problem or missing property first, then concrete examples, then consequences/remedies — readers need the abstraction to know when the rule applies to them, and a leading specific case (e.g., a particular contrib app) reads as a one-off fix rather than a general gap. — Readers need the abstraction to know when the rule applies to them, and a leading specific case (e.g., a particular contrib app) reads as a one-off fix rather than a general gap.
<!-- rule:2205 -->
- Scope `Field` check/warning messages to the exact config that triggers them and name the real behavioral concern — asserting two configs are equivalent misleads users into the wrong fix — A check message like `django/db/models/fields/related.py`'s claims are read as authoritative, so an unscoped or equivalence-flavored message sends users hunting for a non-existent difference (or ignoring a real one). The same asymmetry in `docs/ref/models/fields.txt` — warning about one option while silently accepting its analogues — makes the reference docs look inconsistent, which erodes trust in the whole check framework.
  - Add such a note to `docs/ref/models/fields.txt` only when analogous options get comparable notes — isolated warnings read as arbitrary and unfairly single out one option
<!-- rule:2961 -->
- Document env vars where they're consumed — CLI `help` text and a comment above the assignment — they're implicit interfaces: say what they supply (settings module, library paths) rather than the runtime mechanism, and note plainly when setting the var alone replaces a flag. — Env vars are set outside the code that reads them (e.g. `tox.ini`, `tests/runtests.py`, `django/test/runner.py`, `runtests.py`), so the point of consumption is the only place a user or assistant can discover them. Describing the supplied value instead of the lower-level mechanism keeps the interface useful to callers, and stating that a var alone replaces a flag prevents users from passing both.
<!-- rule:997 -->
- Name the exact condition or model attribute behind each migration branch instead of vague qualifiers like "unless required"; add inline comments for non-obvious branches in `django/db/migrations/executor.py` and state conditions explicitly in `docs/topics/migrations.txt` — vague qualifiers force readers to guess when a migration actually applies or runs. — Vague qualifiers hide the real trigger for a conditional branch, so readers of the executor/state-building code and the migration docs can't tell what causes a behavior — naming the controlling attribute or condition prevents that ambiguity.
<!-- rule:1535 -->
- Document side effects at lazy access/deferred sites (`django/db/models/lazy.py`, `django/contrib/gis/gdal/driver.py`) — hidden side effects surprise callers — Lazy properties and deferred checks hide expensive or side-effectful work behind what looks like plain attribute access. Documenting the side effect (or the deferred trigger) at the point of access warns callers who might otherwise trigger I/O, queries, or mutations unintentionally.
<!-- rule:1785 -->
- Write `backward compatibility` (no hyphen) in comments and docs prose, fixing grammatical fallout in the sentence as you edit — Matches terminology already used across `docs/` and source comments (e.g. `django/contrib/gis/gdal/geometries.py`, `docs/ref/utils.txt`); inconsistently hyphenated prose reads as a different term and leaves the surrounding sentence ungrammatical.
<!-- rule:2058 -->
- In triage docs and PR templates (`docs/internals/contributing/triaging-tickets.txt`, `.github/pull_request_template.md`), call the ticket's work a `solution`/`contribution`, not `pull request`/`patch`/`commit` — one ticket can span multiple commits or contribution forms, so mechanism-specific terms misdescribe the tracker. — Precise terminology prevents a mismatch between docs and the tracker's model; conflating a code change with its PR container misleads triagers and reviewers.
  - In review docs, refer to the assessed artifact as the `patch` or `diff` (the code change), not the `pull request` — keeps focus on the change, not its container.
  - Match terminology to the tracker's own flag descriptions — divergence makes flags and docs disagree.
  - Don't reword existing prose or headings for style alone when the current phrasing already describes the workflow accurately — style churn on contributor docs creates needless review noise and regression risk.
<!-- rule:1199 -->
- Document test conditions and rationale explicitly, no vague qualifiers — unclear intent lets valid tests get deleted later. — Prevents future maintainers from removing tests whose purpose is unclear, and keeps reviewers aligned on what each test actually verifies.
  Rationale: Tests whose preconditions and purpose are implicitly assumed ("usually works", "should be fine") are re-evaluated inconsistently by reviewers and deleted by future maintainers who can't tell what they protect. Naming the exact condition and the bug it guards makes the test's value legible without the original author.
<!-- rule:5641 -->
- Document framework-invoked hooks/callbacks with `Called when ...`; skip internal, self-explanatory ones — uniform phrasing lets users scan trigger points, and internal hooks just add noise to reference pages. — Uniform phrasing lets users scan trigger points, and internal hooks just add noise to reference pages.
<!-- rule:1183 -->
- Update `django/conf/app_template` and related scaffolding together with its docs — `docs/intro/tutorial01.txt` and `docs/howto/override-project-templates.txt` ship in the same change — Docs drift from generated code leaves tutorial followers with broken examples and no way to discover manual integration steps.
<!-- rule:5142 -->
- Keep inline comments on parsing/control-flow logic to one capitalized line of intent; add a concrete input example when the format isn't obvious (e.g. `# Check for subtags, e.g. 'ab-cd-x-informal'.`). Multi-line case enumerations belong in tests or docstrings — duplicated there, they drift out of sync with the parser. — Consistent, single-line comments keep intent readable at the point of code, while avoiding stale case lists that no longer match tests or docstrings.
<!-- rule:4701 -->
- Guard non-obvious query-compilation invariants (e.g. alias positions, `change_map` cycles) with an inline comment and a named regression test citing the ticket — these break subtly and only surface on rare query shapes, so the comment plus test is what keeps a future refactor from silently reintroducing the bug. — Query-compilation invariants like alias positions or circular references in `change_map` (see `django/db/models/sql`) aren't obvious from the code, and violating them doesn't error immediately — it produces wrong SQL or `DistinctOn` annotation failures only for specific query shapes. A comment explaining the invariant plus a ticket-referenced test named for the guarded case (as in `tests/queries` and the `distinct_on` annotation tests) is what lets the next reader know the constraint is load-bearing and reproduce the original failure.
<!-- rule:5819 -->
- In `django/db/models/sql/compiler.py` and `django/db/backends/*/introspection.py`, comment any table/column reference that is only valid because of an inner join, and keep comments explaining raw/unqualified table names (e.g. before `qualified_table_name()`) unless you update them — these SQL invariants are invisible in the code and easy to break when joins or qualification change. — Without these comments, someone altering or removing an inner join can't tell that a table or column reference depended on it, so the SQL silently becomes invalid or resolves to the wrong column, and outdated comments about raw or unqualified table names mislead anyone touching the qualification logic.
<!-- rule:6268 -->
- Preserve tense markers like `has been` in bios/profiles — dropping them implies the involvement ended — In biography and profile prose (`contributors.md`, team pages, `docs/` narratives), present perfect signals ongoing involvement that started in the past. Rewording that strips the auxiliary verb or shifts to simple past silently changes the claim — readers conclude the person or project stopped. Keep idiomatic verb forms intact when editing existing sentences.
<!-- rule:2246 -->
- In CSRF docs (`docs/howto/csrf.txt`, `docs/ref/csrf.txt`, `django/views/templates/csrf_403.html`), write idiomatic, copy-paste-safe snippets: — CSRF examples are copy-pasted verbatim into user projects and templates, so anything non-idiomatic propagates as a bug; the FAQ is the only place readers assess whether their own app is exposed.
    - Use `const` for non-reassigned token variables — signals the token is fixed, so readers don't hunt for reassignment.
    - Parse the `csrftoken` cookie with a regex, not `URLSearchParams` — the latter targets query strings, not cookie headers, and is unavailable in older browsers.
    - Wrap literal setting and header names in `<code>` — readers copy these verbatim and need them visually distinct from prose.
    - In FAQ answers, state concrete attack preconditions instead of vague hedging — readers can't judge their own exposure from generalities.
<!-- rule:5435 -->
- Name check modules, tags, and IDs after the literal condition they detect, not protocol or environment names or interpretive labels — `docs/ref/checks.txt` and `docs/ref/checks` are indexed by symptom, so vague labels send users to the wrong page and hide the real safety concern. — Naming check modules/tags after a protocol name instead of the safety concern they enforce, or describing detection criteria with interpretive labels instead of exact literal values, makes the check framework misleading and lets a passing or failing check be attributed to the wrong condition. Maintainers then cannot reliably discover the literal detection criteria or the actual risk from the check’s name and description, leading to overlooked, duplicated, or wrongly trusted checks.
<!-- rule:5418 -->
- Phrase filtering/exclusion comments as `Filter out X`, not `do not return X` — direct phrasing describes actual behavior; negation reads like a caller-side constraint — Direct imperative comments describe what the code does, keeping them unambiguous when behavior is later changed or reused. Negative phrasing blurs the line between a description of the implementation and a requirement imposed on callers, which is especially confusing in shared query/filter code.
<!-- rule:5798 -->
- Write `sanitization` (not `sanitation`) in `docs/ref/unicode.txt` and Django filename docstrings — consistent terminology across docs prevents reader confusion — Two documentation conventions for Django filename validation: `sanitization` is the established term across `docs/ref/unicode.txt` and source docstrings, and Unicode filename handling should be framed as a requirement whenever uploads are enabled ("If you intend to allow...") rather than an optional feature—otherwise developers may ship upload paths that corrupt non-ASCII filenames.
  - Describe Unicode filename handling as required for uploads (phrase as "If you intend to allow...") — presenting it as an optional developer choice lets devs ship with broken non-ASCII filenames
<!-- rule:676 -->
- Use generic `query` terminology in ORM subquery APIs and docs when both `Query` and `Subquery` are accepted — naming only `Subquery` misleads users when either works — Django's ORM accepts both `Query` and `Subquery` in several subquery APIs, so using the specific term can wrongly imply `Subquery` is required. Spelling `subquery` consistently (unhyphenated) matches the class name and existing docs/code. The `.aggregate()` limitation is a real, undocumented footgun: users assume any query works inside `Subquery`, but only annotations with an `output_field` do, so documenting it prevents silent failures and repeated bug reports.
  - Write `subquery` as one unhyphenated word — matches the `Subquery` class and existing `django/db/models/expressions.py` and `docs/ref/models/expressions.txt` conventions
  - Document in `docs/ref/models/expressions.txt` that `Subquery` cannot wrap `.aggregate()` results; only annotated aggregations inside a subquery work, and only with an explicit `output_field` — users otherwise hit confusing failures
<!-- rule:1920 -->
- Document build/release steps with standard tooling (`python -m build`, `twine`), not project-local Makefile wrappers — wrappers duplicate the commands and go stale, while the underlying tools work in any environment. — Copy-pasteable commands keep build/release docs accurate as `extras/Makefile` changes and let contributors run steps outside this repo's make targets.
<!-- rule:6311 -->
- Document non-obvious test infra constraints on third-party packages — otherwise downstream users debug blind (e.g. `settings.AUTH_USER_MODEL` for custom users) — Third-party code can't see why their tests fail against your infrastructure; documenting the required pattern (like referencing `settings.AUTH_USER_MODEL` for custom users) saves them hours of trial-and-error.
<!-- rule:6435 -->
- Use "complement" for things that complete or pair with each other; reserve "compliment" for praise. — These homophones are easily swapped, and a misspelling in docs makes the text look careless and can mislead readers about whether you mean pairing or praise.
<!-- rule:2155 -->
- Use IRI-specific inputs (non-ASCII, `%`-needing chars) in `iriencode`/`iri_to_uri` examples, not inputs already escaped by unrelated code — shows the function's real effect. — Examples that produce output from auto-escaping elsewhere mislead readers about what `iriencode`/`iri_to_uri` in `django/utils/encoding.py` actually does; state string-vs-bytes input assumptions too, and keep `docs/ref/templates/builtins.txt` in sync with the docstring.
<!-- rule:6362 -->
- Comment auxiliary bookkeeping state at its declaration — e.g., a flag tracking `RLock` reentrancy count, since `RLock` exposes no recursion depth — prevents future maintainers from deleting it as redundant — When you add state to compensate for a standard-library primitive that doesn't expose the internal state you need (like `RLock` hiding its reentrancy count), the extra variable looks unnecessary in isolation. An inline comment at the declaration explaining what it tracks and why keeps it from being refactored away, which would silently reintroduce the bug it was added to fix.
<!-- rule:4326 -->
- In contributing/review-process docs (e.g. `docs/internals/contributing/`), link mechanical review rules to the authoritative checklist instead of restating them, and delete checklist questions duplicated inside adjacent procedure steps — copied checks drift out of sync and bury the subjective judgment those steps exist to support. — Contributing and review-process docs are edited by different maintainers over time; when the same mechanical check is written in both the checklist and a procedure step, one copy inevitably goes stale and reviewers follow the wrong version. Keeping objective checks in one linked place also keeps procedure steps short enough that the subjective judgment calls — the part reviewers actually need prose for — stay visible.
<!-- rule:2698 -->
- Write `project-wide`, not `project-wise`, in documentation prose — keeps terminology consistent across docs — `project-wise` is a nonstandard variant that breaks the established wording used throughout the docs, making search and terminology inconsistent.
<!-- rule:3674 -->
- In `docs/internals/security.txt`, state the reporter's full expectation timeline — acknowledgment in working days, analysis taking weeks after, 90-day resolution window, and no chasing needed unless new info surfaces — and keep it synchronized with the acknowledgment/reply templates so reporters see the same timeline everywhere. — Mismatched or missing timelines between the docs and acknowledgment/reply templates cause reporters to chase the security team and erode trust; a single stated timeline sets expectations and reduces duplicate follow-ups.
<!-- rule:4608 -->
- Comment intentional quirks in format strings, hashes, and cache keys — don't "fix" them — Trailing delimiters or stray punctuation in a format string, hash input, or cache key often look like typos; a brief comment prevents a future reader from silently changing serialization output or invalidating existing cache keys.
<!-- rule:4838 -->
- Describe derived values source-to-result in docs (e.g. "`initial` supplies the field's value") — reversed phrasing makes readers invert the data flow — When prose says the source field is "set to" the derived value, readers learn the derivation backward and may pick the wrong override or assume the derived value is authoritative. Stating that the source supplies/initializes the derived value keeps the direction of derivation — and any precedence rules that depend on it — unambiguous.
<!-- rule:4585 -->
- Show at least two records in `JSONL` and other line-delimited format examples — one record hides the newline separator, so readers can't tell records are newline-delimited. — A single-record example gives no clue that the format is line-delimited; two or more records make the newline separator (and the no-trailing-comma/multiline-JSON constraint) visible, so users don't write a JSON array by mistake.
<!-- rule:5902 -->
- Cover all field types in tests, `docs/releases`, and `docs/ref` for new features like F-expression slicing — a subset in the docs signals unsupported fields/ops work — When docs list only some of the supported fields or operations, users assume the unlisted ones behave the same way; covering every variant in tests and reference docs keeps the documented surface matching the real one.
<!-- rule:5873 -->
- Report resolved CLI option values in diagnostics via the same helper that computes defaults — Printing only the raw flag value hides env-set options like `DJANGO_TEST_PROCESSES` / `--parallel` (see `tests/runtests.py`), so users debug against a value that never applied.
  **Rationale:** Printing only the raw flag value hides env-set options like `DJANGO_TEST_PROCESSES` / `--parallel` (see `tests/runtests.py`), so users debug against a value that never applied.
<!-- rule:6431 -->
- Describe and name crypto/binary length limits in bytes, not Python `str` characters — avoids misleading `[:N]` slicing equivalence (e.g. bcrypt's 72-byte cap) and `_chars` identifier mismatches — Password hashing and other byte-oriented algorithms truncate on bytes, while Python `str` slicing (`[:N]`) counts characters — conflating them leads to silent data loss for multi-byte input (e.g. UTF-8 passwords over bcrypt's 72-byte limit) and misleads callers reading `_chars`-suffixed names. Keeping docs and identifiers byte-aligned (e.g. `MAX_PASSWORD_BYTES`) makes the real constraint visible.
<!-- rule:6334 -->
- Describe `precision` params by the unit they actually control (e.g., GeoHash: alphanumeric characters, not "decimal digits") — prevents docs from misleading users about output length. — `precision` values map to different units per domain; calling GeoHash precision "decimal digits" misdescribes the alphanumeric string length users actually get, causing confusion and incorrect expectations about returned results.
<!-- rule:6402 -->
- Document the return contract of base-class helper methods in a docstring — subclasses override these, and implementers need to know what to return (e.g. for `__repr__` helpers: `Return a dict of extra __init__() options to include in the repr.`). — Helpers on base classes are extension points: subclasses override them and callers consume whatever they return. Without an explicit docstring, overriders in other modules must reverse-engineer the expected return value, which leads to inconsistent implementations.
<!-- rule:4776 -->
- Document template-loader default changes in `docs/releases/*.txt` under backwards-incompatible changes — note that `OPTIONS['loaders']` overrides the new default (e.g. cached loader when `DEBUG=True`), so users can find their escape hatch and won't be surprised by dev behavior shifts. — Silently flipping a loader default (like enabling the cached loader under `DEBUG=True` when `OPTIONS['loaders']` is unspecified) breaks dev workflows that rely on uncached template reloads; surfacing it in the Django 4.1 release notes with the `OPTIONS['loaders']` override gives users a documented workaround instead of a mystery.
<!-- rule:2493 -->
- State a behavior's important exceptions inline, where users hit them in the workflow — one passing mention gets missed mid-task — Users follow steps like "restart the dev server if it's already running" literally; if the exception (e.g., static file changes aren't picked up automatically) is buried in a single earlier mention, they silently test stale output — see `docs/intro/tutorial06.txt`.
<!-- rule:2749 -->
- Document installation-dependent behavior as "installation-dependent" and cite channels like `Microsoft Store` as examples only. Let me finalize with the rationale: — Users installing through different channels (pip, source, Microsoft Store) read the same docs; naming only one channel as the cause implies the behavior doesn't apply elsewhere, sending other users down the wrong debugging path.
  - Describe behavior tied to the install method as *installation-dependent* and list channels (e.g., `Microsoft Store`) as examples, not sole cause — avoids misleading users who installed via other channels (see `docs/howto/windows.txt`)
<!-- rule:5415 -->
- In `docs/ref/django-admin.txt` and `docs/howto/windows.txt`, keep Windows color setup minimal: `pip install colorama`, `set ANSICON=on`, point to `manage.py`'s `if __name__ == '__main__'` block, and drop colorama notes already in install instructions — duplication and a nonexistent `manage()` reference send readers chasing code that doesn't exist. — The two files describe the same Windows color-output setup, so overlapping notes drift out of sync and a `manage()` reference doesn't match the actual `manage.py` template users see.
<!-- rule:5410 -->
- Write helper method docstrings as one-line imperatives describing the behavior (e.g. `"Exclude tables with zero rows from the provided list of tables."`), not two-part `"Take X, return Y"` descriptions — the signature already shows inputs and outputs, so restating them just adds noise. — This prevents docstrings from redundantly restating the signature, which adds noise and risks drifting out of sync when parameters or return types change.
<!-- rule:5925 -->
- Lead diagnostic and prompt messages with the concrete finding, then the implication — e.g., "Found X on Y." before "This means Z." Users scanning output need the fact first; only break this when an existing prompt cadence in the same flow would otherwise be disrupted. — When the implication is printed before the finding, users scanning a long stream of diagnostics cannot tell whether a message concerns them until they read the whole sentence, so they either skip past real failures or re-read the same output twice to extract the fact.
<!-- rule:3399 -->
- Document non-obvious transformations with docstrings for methods, inline comments for expressions. — Complex math/logic is hard to verify from code alone; a docstring on a method explaining the transformation and its correctness keeps intent reviewable, while terse inline comments naming the input, target, and reason (operation/theorem) explain one-off coercions like `int(round(...))`, slicing, or escaping without narrating every line. Prefer folding a short chain of transforms on one value into a single returned expression over intermediate reassignments to keep the data flow linear and readable. Reserve docstrings for methods and inline comments for in-expression conversions so readers looking for the 'why' of a technique find it in the right place; avoid multi-line informal explanations that rot as code changes.
<!-- rule:3759 -->
- Pass `from_field` to `get_object()`-style lookups only for non-PK lookups — PK lookups resolve directly via the model's primary key, so supplying `from_field` misroutes the query — The `get_object()` contract differs between PK and non-PK lookups, and the two failure modes (missing kwargs vs. no match) surface as different exceptions; documenting them together prevents users from treating an `AttributeError` from a missing kwarg as a not-found response.
  - In `django/contrib/admin/options.py` lookups and `docs/ref/class-based-views/mixins-single-object.txt`, document that missing required lookup kwargs raise `AttributeError` while a complete lookup with no match returns `Http404` — without this, users misread `AttributeError` as a 404 and chase the wrong bug
<!-- rule:4355 -->
- Cover the key-present-but-empty-data edge case for `SessionBase.is_empty()` in both `docs/topics/http/sessions.txt` and `tests/sessions_tests/tests.py`, checking expected results against the implementation — keeps docs and tests from drifting on empty-session semantics. — This rule spans two distinct locations (`docs/topics/http/sessions.txt` and `tests/sessions_tests/tests.py`) and cannot live in a module-level `AGENTS.md`. Since documented behavior and test expectations for `SessionBase.is_empty()` must reflect the same semantics, place it where an assistant editing either file will find it — a root-level `AGENTS.md` — so the empty-session contract stays consistent.
<!-- rule:4277 -->
- Accept only `GiST`/`SP-GiST` for `ExclusionConstraint.index_type` in both `django/contrib/postgres/constraints.py` and `docs/ref/contrib/postgres/constraints.txt` — PostgreSQL rejects other values (e.g. `Hash`) at execution time, so advertising them in either place produces runtime failures and misleading docs. — The rule is fundamentally about keeping the accepted PostgreSQL exclusion-constraint index types synchronized between the code validation in django/contrib/postgres/constraints.py and the documentation in docs/ref/contrib/postgres/constraints.txt. Evidence explicitly lists both files as requiring coordinated updates, and the rule text itself states 'keep the accepted-values list in the code validation and in the docs in sync.' This is a clear cross-file relationship rather than a single-file rul
<!-- rule:5524 -->
- State the assumed locale/settings context above locale-dependent examples — Docs and test docstrings that show locale-dependent output (e.g. `django/template/defaultfilters.py`, `tests/template_tests/filter_tests/test_date.py`) are ambiguous without the driving value. Naming it (e.g. "When the active locale is English (`en`):" followed by "the active language's `DATE_FORMAT`") lets readers know whether the output is universal or specific to that configuration.
<!-- rule:5783 -->
- Write `updated objects` (not `objects updated`) for the `bulk_update()` return value in `docs/ref/models/querysets.txt` and matching release notes — keeps terminology greppable and consistent for readers cross-referencing both docs. — The API reference and the release notes describe the same return value; identical phrasing lets users search one string and find both, and avoids implying the two documents describe different behavior.

## Testing

<!-- rule:785 -->
- Sync Selenium tests on explicit conditions, not fixed delays: wrap navigation-triggering actions in `wait_page_loaded()`, use `WebDriverWait`/`wait_until` with built-in `expected_conditions` (custom predicates only for browser-specific quirks like Firefox), and wait for AJAX/network/load before asserting — fixed `sleep`s flake under CI load. — Placed under a `Testing / Browser & Selenium` section in the repo-root `AGENTS.md` because the rule spans Selenium tests under `tests/` and shared wait helpers in `django/contrib/admin/tests.py`; fixed delays cause CI flakiness, swallowed `TimeoutException`s obscure real timeouts, and the assert guidance balances noise against missed regressions.
  - Let `TimeoutException` propagate from `wait_until()` and expected conditions in Selenium wait helpers (e.g. `django/contrib/admin/tests.py`) — catching it hides timeouts and reports failures far from the cause.
  - Drop redundant post-wait assertions when the wait already enforces the condition, but keep them if the helper can return without raising on timeout — redundant asserts add noise, missing ones let regressions slip.
<!-- rule:313 -->
- Match `call_command` input mocking to interactivity: pass `interactive=False` and skip `@mock_inputs` for non-interactive commands; for interactive ones pass `interactive=True` with `stdin=MockTTY()` (or use `@mock_inputs`) — real stdin/stdout hangs CI and forces coverage exclusions. — These conventions keep interactive management command and prompt-driven class tests deterministic: real stdin/stdout blocks test runs and forces `# pragma: no cover` exclusions, unmocked `getpass.getpass`/`sys.stdin.readline` leave failure paths untested, and mutating the shared `MOCK_INPUT_KEY_TO_PROMPTS` mapping causes values to leak between tests. Injecting a `StringIO`-backed `self.input()` also lets tests assert on prompt output directly.
  - In prompt-driven tests, mock `getpass.getpass` and `sys.stdin.readline` (including failure paths) — hang-free, fully-covered error branches.
  - Route prompts through an injectable `self.input()` writing to a `StringIO` configured in `setUp()` — makes prompt output assertable without touching real streams.
  - Use callables in `MOCK_INPUT_KEY_TO_PROMPTS` for runtime-dependent prompt values instead of mutating the shared mapping — avoids cross-test state leakage.
<!-- rule:7 -->
- Configure mocks at the `mock.patch`/`mock.patch.object` call site via `return_value=`/`side_effect=`/`wraps=` (with `autospec=True` for spies), dropping config the test never reads — keeps test intent explicit and consistent across modules, instead of custom replacement functions, in-`with` attribute assignment, or manual monkeypatching. — Rationale: declarative kwargs keep the mock's behavior visible at a glance, and removing unused config (e.g. an unread `return_value=`) prevents misleading tests and copy-paste drift across the 60+ test modules that share this pattern.
<!-- rule:275 -->
- In tests and test setup, index required mapping keys directly (`d[key]`, `pop(key)`) instead of supplying defaults — a misspelled key then raises `KeyError` at the point of use rather than silently passing with a fallback value. — Rephrased into three imperative bullets with inline why-clauses, preserving the `d[key]`/`pop(key)`/`.get()` patterns, the `KeyError` failure mode, and the specific test directories named in the placement rationale.
  - Explicitly assert that expected keys are present before asserting on (or looping over) them, and avoid `if key in d` guards inside assertion loops — conditional skips let a broken fixture or stale key set pass the test.
  - Use `.get(key)` or `.get(key, explicit_default)` only when a key's absence is legitimate — keeps the missing key a clean assertion failure or intended fallback instead of an unexpected `None` crash.
  - Applies to test modules and test setup across the repo (`django/test`, `django/contrib/admin`, `django/db/backends`, `tests/`, `tests/auth_tests`, `tests/inspectdb`, `tests/staticfiles_tests`), since silent key fallbacks hide the same class of bug everywhere.
<!-- rule:749 -->
- Reserve Selenium for real-browser behavior (JS events, screenshots); use Django/unit tests otherwise, setting up model state via the ORM — browser tests are slow and brittle — Splits the repo-wide testing philosophy into a rule and a pre-condition. The first bullet keeps the core guidance (Selenium is reserved for genuinely browser-dependent behavior; unit/Django tests via the ORM otherwise) and states why — browser drivers are slow and fragile compared to ORM-driven state setup. The second bullet preserves the 'fix related failures first' clause as its own actionable item, since it applies to any new Selenium coverage regardless of which behavior is being tested. Together they read as flowing guidance rather than a checklist, and the specific concepts (ORM setup, Selenium, browser events, screenshots) are preserved.
  - Fix existing failures in related tests before adding Selenium coverage — don't layer new tests over known breakage
<!-- rule:2133 -->
- Test RTL/bidi with a real locale via `override_settings(LANGUAGE_CODE="ar")` instead of mocking `django.utils.translation.get_language_bidi` — mocks bypass the real translation path and hide rendering bugs — Mocks on `get_language_bidi` bypass Django's actual translation and rendering pipeline, so RTL bugs (misplaced elements, low contrast in mirrored layouts) slip through. Real locales like `ar` exercise the full path. Admin CSS and translation changes are especially prone to direction-specific regressions that only surface when rendered under RTL.
  - Use a single representative RTL locale unless a known locale-specific difference justifies more — extra locales slow tests without adding coverage
  - Verify directional CSS/layout changes under an RTL locale (e.g. RTL screenshots via `django/test/selenium.py`) — mirrored layouts can misplace elements or reduce contrast
<!-- rule:2144 -->
- Verify `aria-labelledby`/`aria-describedby`/`aria-label` targets exist and IDs are unique; assert accessible names via `to_have_accessible_name` rather than raw attribute values — dangling IDs render as unnamed controls, and helper assertions compute the name the way assistive tech does. — A dangling `aria-labelledby`/`aria-describedby` target silently yields an unnamed control that sighted tests still pass, and asserting raw attribute values instead of `to_have_accessible_name` lets a name that assistive tech never actually computes look correct.
<!-- rule:4729 -->
- Run the relevant test suite locally before pushing — CI failures in `tests/`, `django/`, and `js_tests/` waste review cycles and mask real regressions — Import errors and collection errors from edits to files like `django/db/backends/mysql/base.py` or `tests/generic_views/test_base.py` surface immediately when run locally, but otherwise burn a CI round-trip and a reviewer's time before anyone sees the actual change.
<!-- rule:3918 -->
- Exercise lazy/proxy/wrapper tests through the proxy — never resolve the wrapped resource at import/reimport, access lazy attributes via the proxy (asserting the expected first-access errors), force setup before checking `repr`, and compare `repr(original)` against `repr(proxy)`, including non-string wrapped values — catches eager-resolution regressions that silently break laziness or mask the proxy contract. — Testing via the proxy verifies the actual contract users depend on; eagerly resolving the underlying resource hides broken laziness or missing error handling.
<!-- rule:566 -->
- Test custom pickling with `pickle.loads(pickle.dumps(obj))`, not `__getstate__`/`__setstate__` — exercises the real public contract that direct calls skip — Testing pickling through the real `pickle.loads(pickle.dumps(obj))` contract instead of poking `__getstate__`/`__setstate__` directly, and asserting exactly which fields survive the round-trip, catches objects that silently fail or lose state under actual serialization (including transient or unpickleable attributes that direct calls mask), while the single-pass `pickle.Unpickler` pattern keeps these checks cheap enough for test-runner hot paths.
  - Assert which instance state fields survive a pickle round-trip and which are intentionally dropped (transient, weakref, unpickleable) — keeps omissions documented rather than accidental
  - In test-runner hot paths, avoid per-attribute or per-test round-trips (slow under parallel execution); use a single-pass `pickle.Unpickler` approach like `django/test/testcases.py::is_pickable`, which drops unpicklable nested entries such as `dict` keys
<!-- rule:121 -->
- ## Date/time tests — These practices prevent date/time tests from passing or failing for the wrong reason — real clock and local-TZ leakage, dependency drift, and hardcoded literals producing false confidence or false failures — so that regressions in date parsing, DST handling, and relative-time formatting are actually caught.
  - Freeze/mock the clock at the exact import path the module under test reads (e.g. `{module}.datetime.datetime.utcnow`) — patching a different path lets real time leak in
  - Prefer `django.test.utils.freeze_time` over adding `freezegun` — avoids a third-party dependency for something the framework already provides
  - Keep tests timezone-independent (explicit TZ control or TZ-agnostic values) — results must not shift with the local machine's TZ or the real current date
  - Derive expected time/date values with the same formatter that produced them (e.g. `email.utils.format_datetime(..., usegmt=True)` for Date headers) — hardcoded formatted literals drift when the formatter changes
  - Preserve intended boundary coverage instead of substituting unrelated dates: keep `31-Dec-69`/`31-Dec-70` in `test_parsing_rfc850` (correct expectations per RFC rather than deleting), cover the two-digit-year 50-year pivot, and month-end (Jan 31 → Feb 28) / leap-year (Feb 29) for `timesince()`/`timeuntil()`
  - Exercise both `is_dst=True` and `is_dst=False` for nonexistent/ambiguous local times (e.g. `test_trunc_timezone_with_nonexistent_or_ambiguous_result`), mirror the same values across `TruncDay`/`TruncHour`, and include at least one `tzinfo` offset whose `timedelta` has non-zero minutes, asserting the sub-hour component
  - Comment non-obvious hardcoded dates, mocked timestamps, elapsed intervals, DST/TZ boundaries, and implicit year interpretations — a reader can't recover the intent from the literal alone
<!-- rule:3141 -->
- Test slice/index edge cases: negative `start`/`stop` separately, inverted bounds (`stop < start`), and offset arithmetic — silent regressions here change query results — Slice and index handling in `django/db/models/expressions.py` has non-obvious semantics (negative components, intentionally supported inverted bounds that return empty, offset terms). Tests that only hit the happy path miss regressions where these edge cases quietly return wrong rows instead of raising.
<!-- rule:1651 -->
- Close only connections/pools the test created in teardown — closing shared ones corrupts Django test state (`django/test/testcases.py`) — Shared connections in `connections_override` are owned by Django's test framework and closing them breaks subsequent tests; copied connections leak pools unless explicitly closed with the backend-specific `close_pool()` (present only on some backends, hence `hasattr`).
  - Call `connection.close_pool()` (guarded by `hasattr` for cross-backend support) on pools from copied connections; never close entries listed in `connections_override`
<!-- rule:3753 -->
- Add focused regression tests when modifying, moving, or refactoring normalization/validation logic (e.g. in `django/` and `tests/`) — refactors silently drop coverage for original edge cases, non-standard input types, and non-trivial branches, so without pinned tests those behaviors regress unnoticed. — Normalization/validation code accumulates subtle edge-case fixes that rarely have obvious call sites; moving or reworking it without regression tests lets those fixes silently disappear.
<!-- rule:802 -->
- Update `tests/staticfiles_tests` for any regex or CSS/JS URL-rewrite change in `HashedFilesMixin` (`django/contrib/staticfiles/storage.py`) — regex edits silently miss forms like `@import url(...)`, `url(...)` in shorthand properties such as `background`, absolute `sourceMappingURL` values outside `STATIC_URL`, and trailing whitespace. — ...
  - Keep `ValueError` handling in `_url()` for absolute URLs not prefixed by `STATIC_URL` and test that missing-file path — missing target files raise it, so dropping the handling breaks URL rewriting.
<!-- rule:5618 -->
- When fixing SQL injection in query construction (e.g. `django/db/models/functions/comparison.py`, `django/db/models/lookups.py`), add a regression test that replays the injection vector with malicious identifier payloads (quotes, comment sequences, boolean conditions) and asserts only the affected SQL fragment — not the whole generated query. — Asserting the full SQL string makes tests brittle to unrelated formatting changes in `tests/model_fields/test_jsonfield.py` and elsewhere in `tests/`, so future refactors silently break the suite; asserting just the fragment keeps the injection regression pinned while letting query construction evolve.
<!-- rule:3119 -->
- In redaction tests, assert both that the raw value is absent (`assertNotContains(response, raw_value, status_code=500)`) and that the masked placeholder or key is present — Absence-only assertions can pass trivially (e.g. wrong key, empty response) and miss full leaks; pairing with a presence check proves the redaction path actually ran. For multiple sensitive settings, iterate over key/value pairs to cover each one individually.
<!-- rule:4337 -->
- Mock random sources (`random.random`, `random.randint`, `random.choice`) to force each branch — unseeded results make failures flaky and unreproducible — Unseeded randomness makes tests nondeterministic: failures can't be reproduced, and branches may only be covered by luck. Mocking the RNG (e.g. `random.random`, `random.randint`, `random.choice`) lets each branch be forced and asserted; seeding once before a loop or using fixed inputs keeps generated test data stable across runs. When the behavior under test is deterministic, random values add noise without changing coverage.
