  - Seed once before the loop or use fixed inputs for generated test data — unseeded generation makes failures flaky and unreproducible
  - Skip random values in tests where the behavior under test is deterministic — they add noise without exercising anything
<!-- rule:933 -->
- Give each configuration variant (managed/unmanaged, partial/full, per-port, mocked internals) its own named test method, config subclass, or hook — a single parameterized method hides which scenario failed and lets one variant's setup leak into another. — The rule targets configuration-heavy tests where variants are often collapsed into one method, hardcoded literals, or conditional branches. Separating variants and reading from fixtures keeps failures attributable and setups isolated, while distinct-path coverage and CI-env validation prevent unusable configurations from reaching users.
  - Read config values from shared fixtures like `base_params` instead of hardcoding literals in the test — keeps variants consistent and avoids drift when defaults change.
  - Comment the exact scenario each variant branch covers and assert the observable that distinguishes it (e.g. presence/absence of a header, the differing attribute) — otherwise the test name claims a condition the body never exercises.
  - Keep independent configuration concerns decoupled; don't mutate flags for another scenario or branch on conditionals to pick test behavior — hidden coupling makes failures point at the wrong variant.
  - Cover only variants that traverse distinct code paths, and ensure env-toggle-dependent tests pass under every CI configuration — untested CI combos ship broken variants.
<!-- rule:5125 -->
- Django M2M/`through` tests: pin deterministic values and verify relationships end-to-end. — Tests in `tests/m2m_through/`, `tests/unmanaged_models/`, and `tests/schema/tests.py` often fail non-deterministically when they compare non-deterministic values (e.g., `datetime.now()`) or only introspect tables/columns. Setting up views/tables for unmanaged models and exercising save → attach → query through the M2M table catches real regressions. Add cases for `through` intermediaries in migration executor table-detection tests (`django/db/migrations/executor.py`) so cross-app `through` wiring is covered.
<!-- rule:4307 -->
- Test the failure/non-default branch of conversion helpers (`force_text`, `force_bytes`, `formfield()`) — catches regressions when a wrong API, dropped call, or unused arg silently changes output — Conversion/coercion helpers are used across `contrib/admin`, `db`, `core/checks`, and `utils`; only the happy path is typically exercised, so a removed conversion call or untested argument produces no test failure even though behavior changed. Constructing inputs where the wrong API or alternative path yields different output makes the test fail on regression.
<!-- rule:1125 -->
- Test sibling ORM write entry points when adding field-name validation coverage — `create()`/`get_or_create()`, `update()`/`update_or_create()`, `save()`/`bulk_create()`/`bulk_update()`, and `force_insert`/`force_update` share lookup/validation syntax, so a fix in one entry point commonly leaves the others broken; cover both writable and read-only model properties against the standard invalid-field error, and include the exact argument combination that originally triggered the bug. — ORM write paths share lookup/validation logic, so testing only the entry point where a bug surfaced lets the same error survive in its siblings. Exercising every entry point (including `force_insert`/`force_update` and the read-only-property case) plus the original failing arguments ensures the fix is actually complete and prevents regressions in `tests/get_or_create`, `tests/update`, `tests/one_to_one`, `tests/generic_relations`, and `tests/model_inheritance`.
<!-- rule:3303 -->
- Update `tests/utils_tests/test_text.py` when changing slugify in `django/utils/text.py` or ID generation in `django/contrib/auth/management/__init__.py` — Slug/identifier normalization handles non-ASCII input, empty slugs, case-only diffs, collision suffixes, and mixed separators like `'_ -value _-'`; tests must assert no trailing hyphens, verify output matches the new format, and prove loose-matching branches are reachable or intentionally dead so silent regressions in generated IDs don't ship.
<!-- rule:5032 -->
- Test negative durations with a whole-duration sign (`-MM:SS` → `timedelta(minutes=-MM, seconds=-SS)`, `-H:MM:SS` → `timedelta(hours=-H, minutes=-MM, seconds=-SS)`), not component-level signs like `4 -15:30` — `standard_duration_re` applies a sign to the whole duration, so component-level negatives like `4 -15:30` describe behavior the parser never produces — asserting them locks in a nonexistent contract. Any regex change to sign groups is untested unless both the parser tests and the `DurationField` cast tests are updated.
  - Only extend negative-sign handling in `standard_duration_re` (`django/utils/dateparse.py`) alongside matching tests in `tests/utils_tests/test_dateparse.py` and `tests/db_functions/comparison/test_cast.py`
<!-- rule:2965 -->
- Group failure-mode tests with the related API method's existing tests — extend edge-case tests (empty/large input) and the consolidated `test_<function>_fail()` method instead of adding `test_<function>_<specific_failure>` methods. Name rejection tests for the unsupported condition they verify. — The original bundles four related sub-rules (extend existing edge-case tests, group failure tests by API method, add coverage for each documented error branch, prefer consolidated `test_<function>_fail()` over per-failure methods, and name rejection tests for the condition). The rephrase keeps all the concrete naming/path patterns intact — `test_<function>_fail()`, `test_<function>_<specific_failure>` — since those are the most actionable parts for an AI editing test files. The rationale explains the practical benefit (visibility and discoverability of failure coverage) rather than appealing to convention, and is written to stand alone for a reader who never saw the raw rule.
  Rationale: Keeps every documented failure mode/error branch for an API method in one place, so coverage gaps are visible at a glance and future contributors don't fragment or duplicate rejection tests across the suite.
<!-- rule:2699 -->
- Test `deconstruct()` with non-default values, unpack `name, args, kwargs`, and assert `is` identity plus a custom attribute — defaults let wrapped or replaced instances pass unnoticed — Using default parameter values in `deconstruct()` tests makes it impossible to distinguish a correctly preserved instance from one that was silently replaced or wrapped: identity checks against an unpacked `name`/`args`/`kwargs` tuple, combined with a non-default custom attribute on the original instance, prove the exact object survived storage.
<!-- rule:4300 -->
- Bind test servers to port `0` (OS-assigned ephemeral) instead of hard-coded ports — use `manage.py runserver 0` for Django; default port params to `0` in test HTTP server/thread constructors when backward compatible. — Hard-coded ports collide across parallel test runs and CI, causing flaky failures; `0` lets the OS pick a free port.
<!-- rule:4359 -->
- Rename tests when their assertions or expected behavior change — `tests` and `js_tests` names act as docs, and stale names mislead readers about what's verified — Test names are the primary documentation of what a test verifies. When assertions change but the name doesn't, readers (and AI assistants) trust the stale name and miss the actual behavior, leading to false confidence and duplicated or incorrect coverage across `tests` and `js_tests`.
<!-- rule:1786 -->
- In cross-thread/async tests, wait on `threading.Event` instead of `time.sleep()` — `sleep()` is timing-dependent and flaky; but don't add an `Event` when sequential control flow (starting threads after prerequisites finish) already guarantees ordering. — `time.sleep()` assumes a fixed scheduling window, so tests race or hang under CI load; a redundant `Event` obscures the actual ordering guarantee and adds needless complexity.
<!-- rule:4441 -->
- Add CSS fixtures under `tests/staticfiles_tests/project/documents/cached/css/` for inline comments, multiple inline comments on one declaration, and quoted strings with whitespace when changing regex parsing in `django/contrib/staticfiles/storage.py` — these cases pin the `\n` vs `\s` distinction. — Without these fixtures, a regex tweak can silently match tokens across newlines, breaking CSS rewriting on real stylesheets.
<!-- rule:750 -->
- Keep browser-automation tooling (Selenium, Playwright) out of `django/` core — it stays a test-only concern so Django core keeps no browser-automation dependency. — Adding Playwright/Selenium as core modules would introduce heavy runtime deps into every Django install, not just test setups.
<!-- rule:2936 -->
- Prefer reusable custom test backends over `mock.patch` — define them in `custombackend.py` (importable, referenced via `EMAIL_PROVIDERS['...']['BACKEND']` dotted path) alongside `ErroringEmailBackend`, so multiple test modules can share them. — Mocking the real backend and asserting on internal kwargs couples tests to implementation details that churn across refactors, while a shared always-failing custom backend in `custombackend.py` lets multiple modules verify `fail_silently` semantics — that no exception propagates — against observable behavior instead.
  - Assert observable send behavior, not internal backend kwargs — kwargs are implementation details that churn across refactors.
  - To test `fail_silently`/error-tolerance flags, use an always-failing backend and assert no exception propagates — mocking the real backend and checking call args tests implementation, not the flag's semantics.
<!-- rule:4120 -->
- Set `PYTHONHASHSEED` in tests asserting hash-ordered output; for cross-process/version stability, use `hashlib` instead of Python's `hash()` on strings — built-in `hash()` varies per process and Python version, causing flaky ordering assertions and irreproducible results. — Python's built-in `hash()` on strings is randomized per process (per `PYTHONHASHSEED`) and can differ across Python versions, so any ordering or shuffling derived from it is non-deterministic. Tests that assert on that ordering flake or fail on CI, and code relying on it produces inconsistent results across runs or interpreters. Pinning `PYTHONHASHSEED` in the test environment (or in `tests/` setup) makes hash-dependent assertions reproducible; using a stable `hashlib` algorithm (e.g. `hashlib.sha256`) in `django/` code yields the same hash across processes, machines, and Python versions.
<!-- rule:756 -->
- Add concrete input examples as table-driven test cases, not just a fix — prevents regression of reported failures — When a failing case or a helper that bundles example inputs surfaces, that example is exactly the input that broke behavior. Pinning it as a table-driven test case in the relevant function's existing test data ensures the case is checked on every future change, rather than relying on the one-off fix or leaving the example buried in production code.
<!-- rule:3662 -->
- Add regression tests for any change to postgres field placeholder/value type handling — cover the original edge case (e.g. NULL), the string-declared literal, and `QuerySet.update(field=Value([...]))` with its generated SQL casts. Also test analogous fields sharing the same placeholder mechanism (e.g. `ArrayField` alongside `RangeField`), since fixes in `django/contrib/postgres/fields/array.py` and `ranges.py` have repeatedly shipped with these unguarded cases. — Placeholder/type-handling bugs in the postgres fields are case-specific (NULL, string literals, `Value()`-wrapped updates) and silent until a test hits them; parallel fields like `ArrayField` and `RangeField` reuse the same machinery, so a fix for one can regress the other.
<!-- rule:5597 -->
- Use canonical collation names as keys in `test_collations` — names your backend's introspection queries return (e.g. `utf8mb3_*` on MariaDB 10.6+) or portable ICU identifiers (e.g. `en-x-icu`), and include a `'default'` entry. — Keys must match what the database reports, so tests that reference entries by name (never by index) stay portable across backends and OS locales — aliases like `en_US` and SQL aliases diverge per platform and silently break `DatabaseFeatures.test_collations`; a `'default'` entry ensures the default-collation code path is exercised.
<!-- rule:729 -->
- Start `js_tests/` files with the sibling-test preamble (`/* global ... */`, `'use strict';`) — the QUnit runner loads each file standalone, so undocumented globals and lax scoping break silently. — The QUnit runner in `js_tests/` loads each test file standalone rather than through a populated page, so anything that depends on a global `$` or an extra `<script>` tag simply isn't there at run time. Taking `django.jQuery` from the harness and matching the sibling preamble is what makes the tests actually execute and keeps them from leaking state between files.
  - Pull shared deps from the test harness (`const $ = django.jQuery;`) instead of adding `<script>` tags or relying on a global `$` — external scripts aren't loaded by the runner, so those tests fail or leak across files.
<!-- rule:4430 -->
- Mirror formatting/encoding changes with tests in every parallel formatter module, and keep existing output for common inputs — parallel implementations regress silently and downstream tests depend on stable defaults. — Formatter/encoder output is consumed by many call sites and tested in parallel modules (e.g. per-backend or per-format variants). Changing one path without mirroring tests leaves sibling implementations untested and breaks consumers relying on unchanged output for simple/common inputs.
<!-- rule:4495 -->
- In warning/filter matching utilities with optional constraints, short-circuit only unconstrained filters to match/ignore; when module or line-number constraints exist, require caller/module context rather than guessing, and test both category-only and module+message/line cases to verify filtering scope. — Guessing on constrained filters silently mis-scopes warnings in `django` and `tests`; testing both broad (category-only) and narrowed (module + message/line) paths catches regressions in filter scope.
<!-- rule:731 -->
- Use one explicit literal invalid identifier (e.g., base64-encoded `NOT_A_VALID_UUID`) in view-level exception-handling tests — Deriving invalids from valid objects couples the test to unrelated shape, and enumerating variants already caught by field/model validation is redundant at this layer — the test should exercise only the view's exception path
<!-- rule:1335 -->
- Resolve tag→level mappings via `get_level_tags()` in `tests/messages_tests/`, never by inverting `constants.DEFAULT_LEVELS` — the helper merges `DEFAULT_LEVELS` with reversed `settings.MESSAGE_TAGS`, so a hand-rolled inverse silently drifts from production behavior. — Placement rationale: evidence spans `django/contrib/messages/` and `tests/messages_tests/`, so it's a messages-framework testing convention rather than a single-module rule; apply it whenever these tests are touched.
  - Use `@override_settings(MESSAGE_TAGS=...)` when testing lazy level-tag resolution — it fires `setting_changed` and runs `update_level_tags()` before the test body; assert a tag distinct from the default (e.g. `"very-bad"` vs `"bad"`) so stale cached mappings fail the test.
<!-- rule:5519 -->
- Document classmethods with the `classmethod` Sphinx directive and invoke them on the class under test, not an instance — mislabeling them as `method` or requiring an instance misrepresents their class-level semantics (`docs/topics/testing/tools.txt`, `tests/auth_tests/test_models.py`) — These are class-level constructs: the docs directive should say `classmethod` (not `method`), and tests should call them directly on the class rather than instantiating an object. Preserving the same handling in both `docs/topics/testing/tools.txt` and `tests/auth_tests/test_models.py` keeps the documented API and the tests aligned, avoiding a mismatch where docs imply instance methods the code never requires.
<!-- rule:4987 -->
- Reset state per benchmark iteration (`%timeit` `setup=`) and time with `time.perf_counter()`, not `time.time()` — reused iterators and the coarser, non-monotonic `time.time()` clock skew results — Exhausted iterators make later iterations measure empty loops, and `time.time()`'s low resolution plus susceptibility to system clock adjustments produce unreliable comparisons.
<!-- rule:2916 -->
- Gate test-safety checks and subtle runtime/conditional logic in test utilities behind a dedicated `settings` flag disabled by default — keeps existing tests passing when the check misfires. — Opt-in checks let new detection mechanisms land without silently breaking unrelated tests, since test utilities run against every test in the repo.
<!-- rule:2828 -->
- Test Django template-tag decorators for both default and overridden values of any new public customization argument, and invoke the tag multiple times to assert memoization — i.e., the wrapped function runs only once. — Template tags in `django/template/library.py` cache their wrapped function's result; skipping the override case leaves user-supplied arguments untested, and skipping the repeat-invocation case lets call-count/memoization regressions slip through `tests/template_tests/test_library.py`.
<!-- rule:9 -->
- Override methods on the class under test's existing test subclass instead of `mock.patch`ing them — keeps the stub's behavior visible at the class definition and avoids patch-target/teardown-leak bugs. — When a test already defines a custom subclass, patch-and-restore adds indirection (the override lives far from the class it modifies) and risks leaking mocks into other tests if cleanup fails; a subclass override is plain Python and always scoped to that class.
<!-- rule:5212 -->
- Test FK cache invalidation at the `*_id` assignment, not `save()` — assert `is_cached()` / cached object right after assigning the FK ID, name tests after the triggering op (`assignment` vs `save`), and add no-op regression tests (assigning the same FK ID keeps `is_cached()` true). Django invalidates the related-object cache on FK ID assignment, so testing after `save()` masks bugs where invalidation fires at the wrong operation or where no-op assignments spuriously clear the cache. — Django's related descriptor invalidates its cache when the `*_id` field is assigned, not when the instance is saved; testing only after `save()` hides regressions where cache clearing fires on the wrong operation. No-op assignments (same FK ID) must not clear the cache, and these regressions only surface when asserted at the assignment site — hence tests in `tests/many_to_one` and `tests/one_to_one` should be named after the triggering op and include explicit no-op coverage.
<!-- rule:2860 -->
- Test ordering-like params (`ordering`/`order_by`) with both `list` and `tuple` inputs, plus the mutually-exclusive-arg `TypeError` — users pass either type, so untested variants can silently break and error contracts drift. — Ordering-like parameters such as `ordering` and `order_by` are called by users with both `list` and `tuple` values. Testing only one input type leaves the other uncovered, so a refactor can silently accept only one and regress the API. The mutually-exclusive-argument `TypeError` is part of the public contract (e.g. in `django/contrib/postgres/aggregates` and `ref/models/options`) and will drift if not asserted. Covering each accepted input plus the error path locks the behavior in.
<!-- rule:5711 -->
- Toggle global/process-wide flags via a pytest fixture that restores the original in `finally`; use a plain `bool` (`global`/`nonlocal`), not a one-key dict — prevents state leaking across tests and keeps the flag readable. — A fixture that sets the flag and restores it in `finally` guarantees cleanup even if the test fails, so global state doesn't bleed into later tests. A plain boolean is the idiomatic representation for a single flag; wrapping it in a one-key dict adds indirection without benefit.
<!-- rule:3074 -->
- Wrap readonly fieldset contents in `<div class="readonly">` in Django admin fieldset templates — existing test assertions and admin CSS depend on this exact markup — Removing or renaming the `<div class="readonly">` wrapper breaks the admin CSS selectors and existing assertions that depend on that exact markup, while matching raw readonly markup in tests instead of calling `get_admin_readonly_field()` causes unrelated template edits to fail the suite.
  - In admin view tests using `AdminFieldExtractionMixin`, assert readonly presence via `get_admin_readonly_field()` with `assertIsNone`/`assertIsNotNone`, not raw markup — keeps tests stable against template changes
<!-- rule:4293 -->
- Enable all optional app variants (e.g. `gis_enabled=True`) in migration checks — otherwise backend-specific apps' migrations go unexercised. — Migration-check scripts in `scripts/` default to a single app variant, so migrations from backend-specific apps never run and drift only surfaces later in CI or production.
<!-- rule:5492 -->
- Guard optional process-wide runtime/debugging enablement with `is_enabled()` plus the config flag — `if not faulthandler.is_enabled() and enable_faulthandler:` prevents re-enabling an active feature or overriding the user's setting. — Checking only the enable flag re-runs `faulthandler.enable()` on an already-active process, and checking only `is_enabled()` silently ignores the user's config; requiring both keeps enable calls idempotent and honors the flag.
<!-- rule:5269 -->
- Mirror `django/utils/archive.py` permission handling in `tests/utils_tests/test_archive.py` — permissions apply to both files and directories, and tests must be umask-aware — Template scaffolding (`startapp`/`startproject`) uses archive extraction, so users expect extracted files/dirs to be at least readable/executable. Copy permission bits for both files and directories when preserving exactly; otherwise OR the source's permission bits so output isn't accidentally locked down. In tests, assert readability (or an expectation like `0o777 & ~umask`) rather than exact bit-for-bit modes, since umask varies across environments and would cause flaky failures.
<!-- rule:683 -->
- Extract non-trivial escaping/quoting/encoding into a module-level helper; test it table-driven in `{module}.test.js` with escaped and edge-case chars — these bugs stay invisible until hostile input hits them and regress silently. — Encoding/quoting bugs are rare, subtle, and easy to reintroduce; a named helper plus input/output tables makes the contract explicit and catches regressions on characters that never appear in normal usage.
<!-- rule:494 -->
- Update CSRF tests when changing template output or failure context — Tests in `admin_views` assert rendered output and context of `views/csrf.py` and admin CSRF templates — new `csrfmiddlewaretoken` inputs or context vars like `bad_origin`, `forwarded_may_fix`, and forwarded header values silently break them unless asserts are updated in the same change.
<!-- rule:4343 -->
- Update `tests/model_fields/test_filefield.py` to assert `SuspiciousFileOperation` on path-traversal input whenever path handling changes in `django/db/models/fields/files.py` — asserting a sanitized filename instead hides the validation contract and lets traversal regressions slip through. — `django/db/models/fields/files.py` validates filenames and raises `SuspiciousFileOperation` for path-traversal attempts; the paired tests in `tests/model_fields/test_filefield.py` must lock that contract in. Asserting a transformed/sanitized name instead lets future refactors silently drop the exception and reintroduce path-traversal bugs.
<!-- rule:2528 -->
- Add regression tests asserting earlier-app models win namespace collisions — reversing app iteration order silently swaps which same-named model gets registered in Django's registry builders. — Django's namespace/registry construction relies on app ordering, so a same-named model from an earlier app must win. Without a collision regression test, a later refactor can reverse the iteration and silently change which model is registered — a subtle behavior change that breaks consumers relying on precedence.
<!-- rule:5706 -->
- Test `setup_join`/`setup_joins` changes with a real JOIN followed by the transform — a transform on a direct, non-joined field never exercises join wiring, so broken join setup ships silently — Testing a transform on a direct field instead of a real JOIN leaves `setup_join`/`setup_joins` unexercised, so a broken join setup passes CI and only surfaces later as failing relation-traversing queries in production.
<!-- rule:3641 -->
- Exercise the actual leak-triggering operation — just construct models and call `gc.collect()` and the test passes even with the fix reverted — Memory leaks in this codebase surface only from real operations (e.g. ORM `select_related` queries, model-state mutations), so `tests/model_state/test_memory_leak.py`-style tests must run that operation before checking weakrefs/GC to remain a valid regression guard.
    Rationale: Memory leaks in this codebase surface only from real operations (e.g. ORM `select_related` queries, model-state mutations), so `tests/model_state/test_memory_leak.py`-style tests must run that operation before checking weakrefs/GC to remain a valid regression guard.
<!-- rule:6380 -->
- Add/update caller tests when swapping manual parsing or version logic for a shared helper — Cover both the normal parsed result and the error/validation path (malformed input, failed length checks) — the old inline logic's edge-case behavior is exactly what breaks silently when it moves behind a helper.
<!-- rule:6096 -->
- Name or document fallback defaults in `django/utils/numberformat.py` (e.g. numeric precision) and exercise each in `tests/utils_tests/test_numberformat.py` — untested defaults silently drift, changing output when the constant is edited. — Formatting utilities in `django/utils/numberformat.py` rely on fallback values like precision defaults; if those aren't named/documented and pinned by tests in `tests/utils_tests/test_numberformat.py`, an edit to the default changes rendered numbers with no failing test to catch it.
<!-- rule:3599 -->
- Test conditionally-skipped init steps (e.g. checks bypassed by a parallel start method) — untested skip branches regress silently when the condition changes — When an initialization step is gated behind a condition (such as skipping `checks` under a specific parallel start method), the skip path is easy to break during refactors and produces no visible failure unless a test explicitly asserts the step was not executed. Without that assertion, a regression that re-enables the step — or skips it in the wrong configuration — ships unnoticed.
<!-- rule:2590 -->
- Rule text: When testing or modifying admin changelist bulk-action row selection, reproduce the row-selected state: in screenshot tests check a row's checkbox before opening the actions dropdown, and when changing `:has()` row-selection selectors manually verify in Chrome by selecting rows, triggering an action, and using the 'No, take me back' button on the confirmation page. — The `:has()` row-selection selectors only activate when a row's checkbox is checked, so unselected-state tests silently miss or falsely validate dropdown and matchMedia behavior.
  Category: testing
  Scope: project
  Placement rationale: The rule governs testing/verification procedure for admin changelist bulk-action row selection, and the evidence spans two distinct directories (tests/ and django/contrib/admin/static/admin/css/). Since it applies wherever changelist selection behavior is tested or modified—not to a single module—root-level placement ensures AI assistants find and apply it regardless of which side (test code or admin CSS/JS) they touch.
  Example of bad code: None
  Example of good code: None
<!-- rule:5465 -->
- Set `reset_sequences = True` on `TransactionTestCase` subclasses that need reset auto-increment counters — backend `sql_flush` side effects aren't guaranteed across databases. — Sequence reset behavior varies by backend and Django version, so tests that implicitly depend on `sql_flush` can silently fail or pass for the wrong reasons when the database or dependency changes.
<!-- rule:1113 -->
- Write test-generated artifacts (baselines, HTML reports) to a configurable directory outside the source tree (`temp`/user-home), named from stable test context like the test name — keeps the source tree clean and artifact names reproducible across runs instead of drifting with URL fragments, counters, or hardcoded host details. — Artifacts written into the source tree pollute checkouts and conflict with VCS; names derived from dynamic URLs, counters, or host details produce mismatched baselines between environments (e.g., `django/test/selenium.py`, `tests/baselines/...`) and make diffs noisy and flaky.

## Config

<!-- rule:1486 -->
- Pin lint/format hook `rev`s only in `.pre-commit-config.yaml` — single source of truth; don't duplicate versions in `docs/requirements.txt`, CI, `tox.ini`, or other dep files (prevents version drift and spurious formatting diffs). — Centralizing pins in `.pre-commit-config.yaml` keeps pre-commit-managed tools out of sync with duplicated entries in `docs/requirements.txt`, CI, and `tox.ini`; routing calls through pre-commit ensures local runs match CI output, and cadence-based updates prevent unnecessary churn and broken-revision chases.
  Route lint/format tool invocations through pre-commit using its pinned version — running an unpinned or newer major release produces diffs that disagree with CI.
  Update pinned `rev`s on a fixed cadence via `pre-commit autoupdate` or pre-commit.ci, not every upstream release; hold the last known-good revision if a newer release breaks pre-commit — avoids CI churn.
<!-- rule:2929 -->
- Reference storage/email alias constants — `DEFAULT_STORAGE_ALIAS`, `STATICFILES_STORAGE_ALIAS`, `DEFAULT_EMAIL_PROVIDER_ALIAS` — instead of literals like `'staticfiles'`; alias names then resolve in one place across `django/conf/` and tests, so renames can't silently drift. — Alias names like 'staticfiles' appear in settings, Django internals, and tests; hard-coded literals mean a rename or typo drifts out of sync. Resolving through constants and defining the STORAGES defaults explicitly in `django/conf/global_settings.py` keeps Django's internal aliases consistent everywhere they're referenced.
  - Define the `STORAGES` default mapping in `django/conf/global_settings.py` with keys `'default'` → `django.core.files.storage.FileSystemStorage` and `'staticfiles'` → `django.contrib.staticfiles.storage.StaticFilesStorage`, using `'default'` as the primary backend — keeps the backend identifiers Django itself looks up stable and explicit.
  - Keep alias-name constants such as `DEFAULT_STORAGE_ALIAS` and `STATICFILES_STORAGE_ALIAS` as internal constants in `django/conf/__init__.py`, not user-configurable settings — these are Django-internal identifiers, and exposing them lets users rename aliases Django code depends on.
<!-- rule:1465 -->
- Lint only hand-authored sources; exclude generated, vendored, and third-party files — styling regenerated output creates churn on every rebuild — The rule matters because scoping linting to hand-authored sources while excluding generated, vendored, and fetch-refreshed files prevents both regeneration churn from style fixes that would be clobbered and silent coverage gaps from over-ignoring maintained code.
  - Use broad include globs (`docs/**/*.css`, `docs/**/*.js` in `biome.json`) with targeted negations (`!docs/_build/**`) — covers all first-party code without per-file upkeep
  - Keep hand-maintained JS (`Gruntfile.js`) and docs sources (`.rst`/`.txt`, docs CSS/JS) in scope — ignored hand-edited files silently drift
  - In ESLint ignore, exclude only generated, vendored, or fetch-refreshed JS — over-ignoring drops coverage of maintained code
  - Check generated/vendored files for syntax only, never style rules like isort or max line length — style fixes get clobbered on regeneration
  - Filter out Django migrations and `.po` catalogs; add per-file exclusions only for vendored files that must stay untouched — keeps the pipeline generic instead of special-cased
<!-- rule:5322 -->
- Use native INI syntax in `setup.cfg`: lowercase booleans, unquoted values, single-line `key = value` — `configparser` reads these natively, so quotes and multiline blocks corrupt single-value options. Reserve indented blocks for multi-value keys like `classifiers` and `project_urls`. — `configparser` parses lowercase `true`/`false` as booleans and unquoted strings/enums verbatim; quoting or line-wrapping a single-value option leaves stray quotes or newlines in the parsed value. Multi-value keys (`classifiers`, `project_urls`) are the only ones that need indented continuation blocks. Apply the same style in INI examples such as `docs/intro/reusable-apps.txt` so copy-pasted snippets stay valid.
<!-- rule:2503 -->
- When changing the test runner CLI (flags, verbosity, warnings, input handling) or adding a non-default test configuration, update the corresponding CI workflows/builders (e.g. `.github/workflows/selenium_tests.yml`, Jenkins builders) to pass the equivalent explicit arguments. Exercise non-default configurations in scheduled/non-PR runs, not only PRs. Rationale: CLI defaults drift from CI settings, so unpinned flags and untested configs silently diverge, leaving the non-default path broken in CI long before anyone notices. — Test runner defaults (e.g. in `tests/runtests.py`) and CI workflow invocations evolve independently; unless CI passes the equivalent explicit arguments and scheduled runs cover non-default configurations, differences go untested and surface as silent CI breakage. Scheduled/non-PR runs catch drift that per-PR CI, using defaults, would mask.
<!-- rule:4194 -->
- Declare licenses explicitly in `pyproject.toml`/`setup.cfg`: list each license file individually in `license-files` — `LICENSE*` globs sweep in non-license files like `AUTHORS`, so shipped metadata stops matching what is actually licensed — Packaging license metadata is duplicated across `pyproject.toml`, `setup.cfg`, and docs examples, and globs or vague labels silently misreport what ships; explicit per-file entries, exact SPDX IDs, and mirrored Trove classifiers keep all three consistent and machine-checkable.
  - Use exact SPDX identifiers in `license` (e.g. `BSD-3-Clause`, not `BSD`) and combine bundled licenses as SPDX expressions (e.g. `BSD-3-Clause AND Python-2.0`) — ambiguous labels defeat automated license detection and legal review
  - Add matching `License :: ...` Trove classifiers whenever additional licensing applies — keep `pyproject.toml`, `setup.cfg`, and documented examples in `docs/intro/reusable-apps.txt` in sync so consumers see one consistent license story
<!-- rule:577 -->
- Give each concern its own storage-neutral setting/constant — never overload cookie-specific names for session storage; use `{FEATURE}_SESSION_KEY` (like Django's `LANGUAGE_SESSION_KEY`) with a separate key per backend, name persistence `_set_token`/`_set_secret` rather than `_set_csrf_cookie`, and keep URL-facing names/tokens distinct — overloading couples storage backends and silently breaks session-mode config. — 
  - In Django CSRF, honor `CSRF_USE_SESSIONS`: resolve the domain via `SESSION_COOKIE_DOMAIN` when sessions are enabled and `CSRF_COOKIE_DOMAIN` otherwise, and reject a set `CSRF_COOKIE_DOMAIN` under sessions via `ImproperlyConfigured` pointing to `SESSION_COOKIE_DOMAIN`, using `CSRF_SESSION_KEY` instead of `CSRF_COOKIE_NAME` — an ignored setting otherwise misconfigures the cookie domain with no error.
<!-- rule:17 -->
- Match CLI flags to the target's actual options — misspelled, unsupported, or duplicate flags silently break on pinned/minimum versions in `run:` steps and scripts — External tools reject or ignore unknown flags, so a typo, a flag added in a newer version than the pinned/minimum release, or a redundant duplicate causes silent failures in `.github/workflows/` `run:` steps and shell scripts.
<!-- rule:1690 -->
- Consolidate related settings into one setting with explicit mode keys instead of parallel top-level names — redundant settings drift apart and must be kept in sync manually — Django's config patterns (e.g., mail/HTTP backend config) favor a single setting with a mode key over multiple top-level settings. Parallel names invite partial updates and silent divergence, since neither enforces which value actually wins. Referencing each config source only once per expression/function keeps lookups consistent and makes conflicting inputs detectable instead of silently masked by a second read.
<!-- rule:1434 -->
- Automate plural-form cleanup for gettext `.po` catalogs in the translation-management script — never hand-edit — Placed at the top level because it governs the gettext pipeline across all `.po` catalogs (django, docs) and its durable fix lives in the shared translation-management script, not any single file. The rationale explains the concrete compile error and why manual edits don't stick.
  - Never modify the `Plural-Forms` header when updating translations; preserve the declared `nplurals`
  - Strip extra `msgstr[n]` forms beyond `nplurals` (e.g., `msgstr[2]` in a 2-form locale)
  Why: invalid extra forms are reintroduced on every translation fetch/regeneration, and `.mo` compilation fails with `nplurals = 2 ... but some messages have 3 plural forms`. Fixing this by hand is futile because the next sync undoes it — only script-level automation persists.
<!-- rule:4210 -->
- Use `post_migrate` handlers (wired in `AppConfig.ready()`) only for data derived from models — `create_permissions()` lifecycle data like content types and model permissions — so state stays in sync with what migrations actually create. — Preserves Django's model/content-type lifecycle conventions, avoids desync between migrations and runtime data, and keeps database backends generic.
  - Return early when `app_config.models_module` is falsy — mirrors `create_permissions()` behavior and safely skips apps with no models.
  - Preserve existing `post_migrate` connections — they support adding an app to `INSTALLED_APPS` after initial migrations and tests that set `MIGRATION_MODULES = None`.
  - Keep app-specific DB introspection customizations in the contrib app's signal handlers, not the database backend — keeps the backend generic and reusable.
  - In migration tests, drive state through real migration steps (`call_command("migrate", app_label, "0001")`) instead of constructing rows via the ORM — the `post_migrate` handler only fires on real steps.
  - Don't use `post_migrate` (or paired custom settings) for data unbound from the model/content-type lifecycle — e.g. bootstrap data for built-in framework apps — since it can silently desync from migrations; prefer a `RunPython` operation or the framework's standard setup mechanism, and document that mechanism instead.
<!-- rule:4803 -->
- Validate sequence-typed Django settings in system checks: reject bare `str`, verify the container matches the documented shape (`list`/`tuple`), check element types, register in `tuple_settings`, and aggregate with `all(val)` — strings iterate char-by-char past naive checks (e.g. `all(val)` on a `str` passes) and non-registered settings skip `tuple_settings` enforcement, so failures surface later as confusing `ImproperlyConfigured` errors — The rule describes a general behavioral pattern for validating sequence-typed Django settings, applied across multiple unrelated directories (django/core/checks/*, django/core/mail, django/core/signing, django/conf) plus tests and docs. Because the evidence spans several modules rather than a single directory, and there is no clear file-coordination relationship (source→target) suggesting cross_file, the rule is best placed globally. Per the guidelines, broader placement is preferred when uncertain.
  - Phrase sequence-setting errors as the required structure (e.g. 'a list of tuples') and test every element — first-element-only checks and vague messages hide which setting is malformed
<!-- rule:2391 -->
- Accept `None` in Django system checks for optional settings; flag only non-`None` values that fail the type check, and validate mapping keys independently of `None` values. — Optional settings are commonly unset, so treating `None` as invalid (or letting a `None` value excuse an invalid key) breaks valid configs in `django/core/checks/security/base.py`, `django/contrib/sites/checks.py`, and `django/core/checks/database.py`. Add a test for the `None` path in each check.
<!-- rule:4133 -->
- When branching on field/column type in backend code, match the underlying storage type, not a single field class — Oracle `NCLOB`/`CLOB` handling must cover both `TextField` and `JSONField`, with `JSONField` routed through the `TextField` path. — Field classes like `JSONField` share the same storage as `TextField` on Oracle; enumerating a single type means new variants silently fall through to the wrong column type.
<!-- rule:3695 -->
- Model user-controllable feature options as tri-state enums (`ALWAYS`/`ALLOW`/`NEVER`), defaulting to `ALLOW`, not booleans — booleans can't express "user may opt in but isn't forced," forcing awkward overloads. — Collapsing user-controllable options into booleans or burying their checks in short-circuits silently loses the user's intent and lets a per-query opt-in override an admin's explicit disable, so a feature the user explicitly forbade can be turned back on without anyone noticing.
  An explicit global/connection disable (`False`/`NEVER`) overrides per-query opt-ins; per-query flags may only restrict behavior, never re-enable a disabled feature — otherwise a query string could silently undo an admin's safety setting.
  Don't put user-facing option checks inside `or`/`and` short-circuits that can skip them, and store raw option values separately from derived state — collapsing an option into a derived boolean loses the user's intent and hides the bypass.
<!-- rule:2061 -->
- **Check `Meta` list options against exact base classes** — e.g. for `Meta.constraints`, compare `type(c) is BaseConstraint`, not `isinstance`, so custom subclasses still pass. — A `BaseConstraint`-only `isinstance` check in `django/core/checks/model_checks.py` would silently reject legitimate user-defined constraint subclasses. The exception path is covered by `tests/invalid_models_tests`, so preserve subclass semantics when adding new `Meta` validators.
<!-- rule:2930 -->
- Default-fill alias config settings (`EMAIL_PROVIDERS`, `STORAGES`) via `setdefault`, keep nested `OPTIONS` optional, and raise `ImproperlyConfigured` only for truly required keys, naming the missing alias/key — lets users configure only the aliases they use and surfaces actionable errors instead of `KeyError`s or silent defaults — Configured aliases are independent, so users typically only define the ones they use; requiring every key would force boilerplate and any missing optional nesting (like `OPTIONS`) would break otherwise-valid configs. Raising `ImproperlyConfigured` with the alias/key name makes missing-required failures diagnosable, unlike raw `KeyError`s.
<!-- rule:5738 -->
- Resolve settings-dependent parsing and execution paths once in `__init__`/`load_middleware`, cache on `self` (e.g., `self._middleware_chain`), and let request-time methods read that state — avoids redundant per-request work and drift from live settings changes. — Django's middleware is initialized once but handles many requests, so re-reading settings and recomputing chains in `__call__`/`process_request` wastes work on every request and can desync from the chain picked at startup (as in `django/middleware/csrf.py` and `django/core/handlers/base.py`, where `load_middleware` builds `self._middleware_chain` up front).
<!-- rule:5959 -->
- For parallel coverage, set `parallel = True` under `[run]` and point `data_file` at an ignored dir like `.coverages/.coverage`; per-process `.coverage.*` files then stay contained and clean up with one `rm -rf`. — Parallel/multiprocess runs write a separate `.coverage.*` file per worker in the directory holding `data_file`; without a dedicated ignored directory those files scatter across the repo, pollute `git status`, and can only be cleaned up file-by-file. Anchoring `data_file` inside an ignored dir (e.g. `.coverages/.coverage`) keeps ephemeral worker output isolated from source and lets a single directory deletion remove all of it.
<!-- rule:5640 -->
- When splitting server addresses on `:`, treat bracketed IPv6 (`[::1]:11211`) as one address — naive splits break IPv6 parsing — Server-address normalization (e.g., `_modify_servers` in `django/core/cache/backends/memcached.py`, `runserver.py` bind formatting) must handle IPv6 literals explicitly because `str.split(':')` shreds `[::1]:11211`. When formatting bind addresses, wrap raw IPv6 in `[addr]` and map `'0'` to `'0.0.0.0'`; this yields valid socket binds even when the underlying client library (e.g., memcached) doesn't yet support IPv6.
<!-- rule:1890 -->
- Validate config-driven conditions with Django system checks at startup (in `contrib/*/checks.py`), not per-request warnings or `ImproperlyConfigured` — misconfiguration surfaces at deploy time instead of on every request, and errors stay out of the hot path. — Per-request warnings fire repeatedly and `ImproperlyConfigured` raised mid-request is harder to diagnose; system checks report the same problems once, before the app serves traffic. This applies to middleware dependency/ordering checks and other stable config-induced states such as middleware forcing sync/async adaptation (see `django/core/handlers/base.py`, `django/middleware/csrf.py`).
<!-- rule:4065 -->
- Set enforcing/blocking limit defaults (`django/conf/global_settings.py`) high enough for real usage (e.g., large admin forms) — low defaults break legitimate inputs when a complementary safeguard already covers the risk — The threshold's purpose determines its value: a blocking default that's too low rejects valid large requests, while a warning-only threshold that's too high stays silent on the outliers it exists to catch.
  - Set warning-only threshold defaults (`django/core/management/templates.py`) conservatively low (e.g., 10–20 MB for template sizes) — high limits never surface unexpectedly large inputs
<!-- rule:1926 -->
- Prefer `pyproject.toml` for Python tool configuration when the tool supports it; otherwise use a dedicated tool-specific config file (e.g., `.flake8`). Do not overload `setup.cfg` with non-setuptools configuration—reserve it for setuptools metadata. — This rule is a repository-wide configuration policy affecting how any Python tool config should be placed (`pyproject.toml` preferred, dedicated files otherwise, `setup.cfg` reserved for setuptools). It is not tied to a single module or directory and applies across the whole project, so it belongs in the root AGENTS.md as a global convention. It is behavioral rather than a strict file-sync relationship, so cross_file (which is for keeping files synchronized after edits) is not the best fit. Overloading `setup.cfg` with non-setuptools sections risks tool-level conflicts and complicates the eventual migration to `pyproject.toml`. A dedicated file (e.g., `.flake8`) keeps each tool's config self-contained and discoverable when `pyproject.toml` support is missing. Keeping setuptools metadata in `setup.cfg` avoids ambiguity about which tool owns which section. Preserve these conventions so contributors place new tool config in the right location rather than scattering it across files. A global rule here prevents inconsistent per-directory config patterns from creeping in. Without this rule, config fragments accumulate in `setup.cfg` and become hard to migrate or reason about. This matters most for projects that ship a package, where `setup.cfg` is read by setuptools at build time—extra sections there are at best ignored and at worst misparsed. Preferring `pyproject.toml` also aligns with PEP 518/621 and the broader ecosystem's direction, so tooling and CI stay compatible as tool versions are bumped.
<!-- rule:4488 -->
- Set an explicit timeout on every external HTTP call (`GitHub` API, `Trac` API) — prevents indefinite hangs when a remote service stalls — For static timeouts, use a module-level constant (e.g. `TRAC_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`). When tuning is expected, expose a `DJANGO_<COMPONENT>_TIMEOUT` env var with a sensible default instead of a numeric literal — External HTTP calls, such as those to the GitHub API or Trac API, can hang indefinitely without an explicit timeout, tying up workers or user requests, while adding configuration for every simple timeout creates premature configuration surface. A fixed module-level constant is appropriate for simple cases, and an environment variable with a sensible default is warranted once a timeout is already being modified or expected to need tuning, so the value remains discoverable and changes do not require churning through literals.
<!-- rule:4823 -->
- In CI container scripts (`.azure-pipelines`, `.github`), address services by Docker DNS name and use `docker port <container>` for host endpoints — never inject IPs scraped via `docker inspect`. — Container IPs are reassigned on every restart and differ across runners, so injecting them via `docker inspect` makes integration tests flaky; user-defined networks and compose service names stay stable.
  - Use `docker-compose` for multi-service CI setups so service DNS wiring is declarative.
  Rationale: Container IPs are reassigned on every restart and differ across runners, so injecting them via `docker inspect` makes integration tests flaky; user-defined networks and compose service names stay stable.
<!-- rule:4087 -->
- Centralize Sphinx build configuration and use built-in Sphinx options for correctness rather than duplicating flags in CI or doing manual shell bookkeeping. Define shared flags (e.g. `-j auto`, `-n`) in `SPHINXOPTS` in both `docs/Makefile` and `docs/make.bat` (kept in sync), and keep default config values in `docs/conf.py` (e.g. `language`, and `spelling_warning = True` for sphinxcontrib-spelling). Use `sphinx-build -W --keep-going` to turn warnings into errors and report all warnings in one run. Reserve CI-only invocations such as `-D` overrides for genuinely build-specific deviations, and never repeat a flag already present in the base command. Avoid capturing warnings to a log, testing for non-empty content, and calling `exit 1`, since GitHub Actions runs steps under `set -eo pipefail` and later commands would not execute after a failed `sphinx-build`. — These rules keep the docs build config coherent across local and CI environments. Centralizing shared flags in `SPHINXOPTS` prevents the Makefile and make.bat from drifting apart; putting defaults in `docs/conf.py` avoids scattering option values across CI steps. `-W --keep-going` turns every warning into a failure in a single run so issues aren't fixed one at a time. Duplicating flags in CI hides drift, and the shell log/`exit 1` pattern breaks under GitHub Actions' `set -eo pipefail` because later steps get skipped after a failed `sphinx-build`. Following these keeps the docs build correct and predictable from one source of truth.
<!-- rule:3883 -->
- Resolve formatters by reading the class path and kwargs from `settings.LOGGING` and instantiating with `django.utils.module_loading.import_string` — `isinstance` checks against a project base class or handler scanning miss user-configured formatters. — Resolving through `settings.LOGGING` keeps user-supplied formatter classes working instead of silently falling back to hard-coded ones. Distinguishing `"class"` vs `"()"` matches `logging.config.dictConfig` semantics so standard formatters instantiate without factory wrappers. Comparing `style`/`format` alongside type prevents mis-identifying a customized formatter as the default.
  In `dictConfig` entries, use `"class"` for formatters accepting standard `logging.Formatter` args (e.g. `"class": "django.utils.log.QueryFormatter"`); reserve `"()"` for factories that truly need custom instantiation — keeps formatters interchangeable.
  When testing whether a formatter is the default, compare class type AND `style`/`format` — type alone treats distinct configs as equivalent.
<!-- rule:463 -->
- Use canonical Django DB backend paths and register alias URL schemes — accept `postgres`/`postgresql` alongside `django.db.backends.postgresql` (not `...postgresql_psycopg2`) so users can reference backends by the names they expect while all aliases resolve to the canonical path. — Legacy driver suffixes (`postgresql_psycopg2`) and missing URL aliases break users' existing `DATABASES` configs and third-party URLs, forcing them to guess a backend path that the parser no longer recognizes.
<!-- rule:2507 -->
- Keep the `PASSWORD_HASHERS` default in `django/conf/global_settings.py` and the class order in `django/contrib/auth/hashers.py` mutually in sync — they encode the same preference ranking in two places, and drift between them produces inconsistent defaults vs. verifiable hashers. — The `PASSWORD_HASHERS` list and hasher class ordering rank password hashers by preference. The first entry is used for new passwords; the rest exist to verify existing hashes. Keeping `django/conf/global_settings.py` and `django/contrib/auth/hashers.py` consistent, including all bundled hashers, ensures existing hashes remain verifiable and lets users opt in. Promoting a hasher with a third-party dependency above `PBKDF2PasswordHasher` can break environments where that dependency isn't installed, since PBKDF2 is always usable.
  - Order hashers secure-first and legacy/insecure/deprecated last — position conveys preference: the first entry is the default for new passwords, later entries exist only to verify existing hashes.
  - Keep `PBKDF2PasswordHasher` as the first/default entry — it has no third-party dependency, so promoting a dependency-requiring hasher (e.g. Argon2) above it can break installations without that package.
  - Insert a new hasher at its security-matching position rather than appending to the bottom — bottom-appending silently demotes it and misstates its preference.
  - Include every bundled hasher in both locations — omitting one makes existing hashes unverifiable and blocks users from opting in.
<!-- rule:1439 -->
- Exclude `**/locale/**/*.po` and `*.mo` churn from code PRs — revert formatting-only diffs, `#: file:line` shifts, `PO-Revision-Date`, empty-`msgstr` entries, and compiled `.mo` files; unrelated catalog noise hides real changes and causes merge conflicts. — Django's `django/conf/locale/` catalogs are managed via Transifex by the release manager, so PR-side edits to them are normally reverted. When the project workflow does require syncing after changing translatable source strings, update only the affected `.po` entries, preserve still-valid existing translations, and never hand-edit `.mo` files — those are regenerated by `compilemessages`.
<!-- rule:5113 -->
- Default security settings to the secure value (e.g. `X_FRAME_OPTIONS = 'DENY'` in `settings.py-tpl`) and update `docs/ref/*.txt` / `docs/topics/security.txt` in the same change — insecure defaults propagate into every generated project, and undocumented ones are never deliberately reconsidered. — Placement: global/security (multi-file coordination between config templates and docs). Chose imperative wording with concrete identifiers: `X_FRAME_OPTIONS = 'DENY'`, `settings.py-tpl` (the project template that generates new Django projects), and the reference docs paths. The rationale explains the actual consequence — a bad default is multiplied across all new projects, and users can't make an informed choice about a setting they can't find documented.
<!-- rule:5697 -->
- Derive locale/language regexes from ISO 639 / IANA standards, not ad hoc bounds or shape guesses — overly broad quantifiers like `{1,8}` for language codes accept invalid tags and diverge from Django's `en_IN` underscore convention. Bound quantifiers to the standard's real maxima (e.g. ISO 639: 2-3 letters), require `_` as separator (reject `en-IN`), handle exceptions like `ar_001` explicitly rather than widening the pattern, and cover the full IANA registry in translation URL prefixes — grandfathered tags (`i-mingo`), variants (`de-CH-1996`, `de-1996`), and variant-like subtags (`kl-tunumiit`) — not just `language[-script][-region]`. — Ad hoc locale regexes either reject valid IANA tags or accept malformed locales, causing translation URL prefixes and locale validation to disagree and fail on real inputs. The conservative, standard-based bounds and minimal patterns from PR#13615 and the valid-IANA-tag tolerance from PR#15098 both need the authoritative standard's real subtag formats. Without that, each new locale or exception invites another one-off regex change and inconsistent validation.
<!-- rule:2927 -->
- Keep provider options and legacy global fallbacks separate in `Django` config migrations — provider-sourced instances (mail backends, template engines) use provider options + component defaults only, never deprecated globals; non-provider instances may fall back to legacy settings so existing configs keep working. Pass required values explicitly instead of relying on a component's back-compat fallback. — Provider-sourced instances that blend in deprecated global settings silently inherit stale config, while non-provider instances need legacy fallbacks to preserve existing deployments; relying on a component's back-compat fallback instead of passing values explicitly breaks that distinction.
<!-- rule:3701 -->
- Check specific MySQL SQL modes (`ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY`, `STRICT_TRANS_TABLES`, `STRICT_ALL_TABLES`) rather than composite modes like `TRADITIONAL` — composite modes expand to different underlying modes across MySQL versions, so matching on them breaks silently when the server's mode set changes. — Composite modes like `TRADITIONAL` are expanded by MySQL into their constituent modes at runtime, and that expansion differs between MySQL versions. Referencing the concrete modes directly keeps validation in `django/db/backends/mysql/validation.py` and docs in `docs/ref/models/querysets.txt` correct across versions and avoids false negatives when the server config changes.
<!-- rule:5584 -->
- Clamp `Decimal` precision to `decimal.getcontext().prec`, not a literal like `1` — respects the caller's configured precision while still rejecting out-of-range values (applies in `floatformat` in `django/template/defaultfilters.py` and similar clamping sites). — Using `decimal.getcontext().prec` as the lower bound keeps `decimal.Context` precision clamping aligned with the active decimal context instead of silently truncating or ignoring the caller's configuration; a hardcoded `1` overrides that intent even though the out-of-range guard is still needed.
<!-- rule:2632 -->
- Map each Django security-header setting 1:1 to its HTTP header — one setting per semantically distinct policy (`SECURE_*`, CSP directives) — so users can read the RFC/header docs and know exactly which setting to touch. — These settings live across the Django settings module, CSP middleware, and `django/middleware/security.py`, and their docs are spread through `docs/ref/`. The 1:1 shape lets a user copy a header value straight from a spec or browser devtools into settings without translating wrapper keys, and comma-separated serialization keeps sequence values byte-identical to what the header would have carried. Sub-keys and mode wrappers break that contract, and generic doc links hide which setting actually emits the header.
  - Keep header settings as flat dicts whose keys/values mirror the header contents; avoid wrapper or mode sub-keys — sub-keys invent vocabulary that doesn't exist in the header and force users to reverse-engineer the mapping.
  - Accept a single value or a sequence for headers that allow multiple policy tokens, serializing sequences comma-separated — RFC list syntax is comma-separated, so both spellings of the header round-trip.
  - Cross-reference the specific header or policy anchor (not a generic one) from setting docs — a generic link drops users on a page they must search, defeating the 1:1 mapping the settings promise.
<!-- rule:2080 -->
- Prefer a third-party GitHub Action's documented placeholder syntax (e.g. `{}`) over raw `${{ ... }}` expressions — the action's own interpolation handles context resolution and escaping; manual substitution can silently drop user logins or mentions. Use `${{ ... }}` only when the action documents support. When the placeholder or expression addresses a user, prefix with `@` (e.g. `@{}`) to render a GitHub mention. — Third-party actions often don't evaluate `${{ }}` in their inputs the way authors expect; using raw expressions can produce empty strings or raw logins instead of resolved values. The `@` prefix turns the resolved user into an actual GitHub mention, which users expect for notification-style actions.
<!-- rule:4375 -->
- Avoid `ALLOWED_HOSTS = ['*']` in tests (`tests/test_client/tests.py`) and testing docs (`docs/topics/testing/advanced.txt`) — wildcards hide host-validation misconfigurations and get copied into production — `ALLOWED_HOSTS = ['*']` disables Django's Host header validation, so tests using it pass regardless of the actual host. That both masks real misconfigurations and produces copy-pasteable snippets that end up in production settings. Explicit hostnames keep tests honest, and explaining the reasoning (not just the recommended value) prevents docs readers from re-introducing the wildcard.
  - In tests, override `ALLOWED_HOSTS` with the explicit hostnames the test needs; in docs, explain why wildcards are discouraged rather than only showing the safe alternative — readers copy examples verbatim, so the "why" is what stops the pattern from spreading
<!-- rule:4476 -->
- Keep `LazySettings`-style settings wrappers exposing only documented/replacement settings — filter deprecated names from `__dir__`/introspection once their replacement is defined, and drop undocumented duplicate accessors/aliases in favor of the documented attribute. Prevents introspection and autocomplete from surfacing stale or duplicate settings, keeping the public API consistent across `django/conf/__init__.py` and `django/db/utils.py`-style wrappers. — Keeps wrappers consistent with what's documented, so users don't discover or depend on deprecated or duplicate settings via `dir()`, autocomplete, or other introspection.
<!-- rule:5375 -->
- Default wrapper parameters from the config-carrying object they wrap: when a wrapper already accepts an object that carries configuration (e.g. a storage/backend/template instance), make the matching wrapper param optional and resolve it from that object, with explicit args overriding. Prevents callers from passing the same setting twice and letting the two diverge. — Two sources for one setting drift out of sync — the wrapper's own default silently wins over the object's config (or vice versa), so behavior doesn't match what the passed object says. Override precedence must be documented: explicit wrapper arg > value carried by the object > hardcoded default.
<!-- rule:5663 -->
- In Django templates needing i18n values, use explicit tags (`{% load i18n %}`, `{% get_current_language %}`, `{% get_current_language_bidi %}`) and enable i18n on the rendering engine — don't rely on `django.template.context_processors.i18n` being active — The context processor isn't always wired up (e.g. custom `Engine` configs, `Engine.get_default()` in Django 4.1+, or `--noscripts` renders), so implicit i18n vars silently resolve empty and templates render untranslated.
<!-- rule:3624 -->
- Store password-hashing secrets (peppers) as an immutable `version → value` mapping and persist the secret version with each password hash — resolve by stored version at verification, highest version for new credentials — so peppers can be rotated gradually without invalidating existing hashes. — A single global pepper can't be changed once hashes exist: every stored hash was derived with it, so swapping it breaks verification for all users at once. Recording the version next to each hash lets old hashes keep resolving their original pepper while new credentials pick up the latest one.
<!-- rule:5378 -->
- Validate client-controlled signature `alg` against a hardcoded minimal allowlist before verifying (`django/contrib/messages/storage/cookie.py`, `django/core/signing.py`) — untrusted input must never select the hash algorithm. Don't derive the allowlist from `hashlib.algorithms_available`, which shifts with environment and Python version. — Prevents algorithm-confusion attacks where a forged signature slips through, and keeps the accepted set stable across environments and Python versions.
<!-- rule:4235 -->
- In `scripts/*.sh`, run every hook-triggering git command (`commit`, `commit --amend`, `cherry-pick`, `merge`, `rebase`) as `git -c core.hooksPath=/dev/null <command>` — local hooks can otherwise block or rewrite the automated flow, and suppressing them on only some calls still lets a hook fire mid-script. — Automation under `scripts/*.sh` (e.g. `scripts/backport.sh`) must be deterministic: user or repo hooks like pre-commit/pre-push can reject or mutate a commit and break the non-interactive run. Suppressing hooks on every such invocation keeps the script reproducible across machines with different `core.hooksPath` settings.
<!-- rule:2134 -->
- Register RTL languages in both places: set `"bidi": True` in `django/conf/locale/__init__.py` and add the code to `LANGUAGES_BIDI` in `django/conf/global_settings.py` — the two configs are read independently, so a mismatch silently breaks bidirectional text rendering. — These two configuration sites are not linked in code, so an assistant updating one without the other leaves `LANGUAGES_BIDI` out of sync with the locale metadata, which disables RTL layout handling for that language at runtime.
<!-- rule:4657 -->
- Keep `exclude_patterns` in `docs/conf.py` as the single source of truth — mirror any changes in `docs/Makefile`, `docs/make.bat`, and the root `Makefile` so every build entry point skips the same dirs — If Sphinx is configured with `.txt` in `source_suffix`, unexcluded dirs like `_ext`, `_static`, or asset folders cause `LICENSE.txt`, `requirements.txt`, and `readme.txt` to be parsed as reST, breaking the build. Prefer `find ... -path './_*' -type d -prune` over hard-coded directory lists so new `_static`/`_ext`/`_theme` dirs are excluded automatically.
<!-- rule:763 -->
- Declare DISTINCT-aggregate support declaratively — put shared validation in `BaseDatabaseOperations.check_expression_support()` and expose per-backend limits as a class-level `unsupported_distinct_aggregates` attribute on each backend's operations class (`django/db/backends/*/operations.py`) — Keeps backend limitations discoverable and testable rather than buried in per-call checks, so auditing a new aggregate means editing one declarative list instead of tracing imperative branches.
- Cover every aggregate and its SQL variant, including paired `*_POP`/`*_SAMP` functions like `StdDev`/`Variance` — half-declared pairs silently allow unsupported DISTINCT queries through.
- Reuse the same entry across every backend whose SQL functions share the limitation — divergent lists drift out of sync and produce inconsistent behavior across backends.
- Add a test per aggregate/DISTINCT combination so omissions fail loudly instead of surfacing as runtime SQL errors.
<!-- rule:5682 -->
- Set DB charset via Django's `OPTIONS` dict (`'OPTIONS': {'charset': 'utf8'}`) — matches Django's upstream convention, so settings and tests stay portable and users don't hit bespoke top-level keys or internal options like `DEFAULT-CHARACTER-SET`. — Django already exposes charset/encoding through the database `OPTIONS` dict, and the tests in `tests/dbshell/test_mysql.py` follow that. Inventing a top-level settings key or surfacing an internal derived option like `DEFAULT-CHARACTER-SET` breaks that convention, so anyone copying Django docs or reusing settings across projects has to translate between two configurations.
<!-- rule:5237 -->
- Map auto fields in the Oracle `data_types` mapping to their integer width: `SmallAutoField` → `NUMBER(5)`, `AutoField` → `NUMBER(11)`, `BigAutoField` → `NUMBER(19)` — copying `AutoField`'s default size would create oversized columns and break introspection. — In Oracle's data_types mapping, the documentation note must record that the mismatch between SmallAutoField's NUMBER(5) and SmallIntegerField's NUMBER(11) is intentional, because copying AutoField's default size for SmallAutoField would create oversized columns and erase the introspection distinction. Without that note, maintainers may mistake the mismatch for a bug and change SmallAutoField to NUMBER(11), silently reintroducing the oversized-column and introspection problem.
  - Document in the Oracle backend notes that `SmallAutoField`'s `NUMBER(5)` intentionally differs from `SmallIntegerField`'s `NUMBER(11)` — the divergence is deliberate, not a bug, and future readers will otherwise "fix" it.
  Scope: `django/db/backends/oracle/base.py` and Oracle backend notes.
<!-- rule:6437 -->
- Gate costly runtime type checks/conversions behind a feature flag — users can disable them if profiling shows overhead (e.g. the `isinstance` in `BytesToCharFieldConversionMixin`) — Lets users with hot paths opt out via configuration rather than forcing them to fork or patch; keeps the check available by default while allowing performance-sensitive deployments to recover the cost when profiling flags it.
<!-- rule:6421 -->
- Keep logging formatter format strings balanced and convert `%`→`{}` atomically — in `django.utils.log` configs (and `docs/topics/logging.txt`) partial edits ship mixed syntax that breaks rendering, and dropping type specs (`%(process)d` → `{process:d}`, `%(asctime)s` → `{asctime}`) silently changes log output. — A partially converted formatter string ships mixed `%` and `{}` syntax that breaks log rendering, and dropping type specs like `%(process)d` → `{process:d}` silently changes the log output, so the rule prevents these two failure modes by requiring atomic, type-spec-preserving edits.
<!-- rule:5295 -->
- Always parenthesize `Collate` output in expression-index SQL and SQL function templates — MySQL only treats a collated expression like `(col COLLATE ...)` as a functional index key part when it is wrapped in parentheses. Because MySQL accepts `COLLATE` inside index definitions, this lets `django/db/models/functions/` and `django/db/backends/mysql/` reuse the existing index machinery instead of adding a dedicated collation feature flag. — Without the parentheses, MySQL will not treat a collated expression such as `col COLLATE ...` as a functional index key part, so Django's generated expression indexes on collated expressions would be misdeclared, silently ineffective, or rejected instead of reusing the existing index machinery.

## Naming

<!-- rule:3241 -->
- Name error identifiers by their actual scope — domain, operation, condition, or lifecycle phase — e.g. `TaskResultDoesNotExist` over `ResultDoesNotExist`, `{Domain}{Operation}Error` over `ProcessingException`, `APPLY_RECURSION_ERROR_MESSAGE` over a generic constant, `post_error` over `error_to_raise`, `enter_exception` over `first_exception` — Generic or overly narrow error names collide across modules and hide which failure domain or lifecycle phase a handler catches — scoped names keep `try`/`except` blocks and error-tracking attributes readable when several error paths meet in the same file.
<!-- rule:4694 -->
- In `CompositeField.__init__` (`django/db/models/fields/composite.py`), set each child field's `.name` to its component alias (`field.name = name`) and validate that names are unique — child fields sharing a name collide when resolving/rendering. — Distinct child names are required for correct field resolution; duplicates cause ambiguous lookups.
<!-- rule:2793 -->
- Name persistence helpers by logical operation, not backend: `create_*` builds a new persisted object, `get_*` retrieves existing stored data, `save_*` (not `store_*`) persists, and avoid backend-specific terms (`store`, `cookie`, `session`) when multiple backends are possible. — Used across Django storage, middleware, and management commands; keeps helper names meaningful as the storage layer varies.
<!-- rule:1107 -->
- Name caches/properties after the single concept they store (`fields_cache`, `local_cache`, `_concrete_field_names`); add a new attribute for each new concept — combined caches go stale across concepts — Combined caches go stale across concepts.
<!-- rule:6213 -->
- Name count/quantity vars `num_<domain/unit>` (e.g., `num_post_keys`, `num_args`, `num_bytes`) — not vague terms like `num_inserted_fields` or misleading suffixes like `fields_len`, `_chars` — The `num_` prefix plus a domain- or unit-specific descriptor makes the unit and subject unambiguous at call sites, while vague implementation words and suffix-based names (`_len`, `_chars`) hide what's actually being counted and lead to unit mix-ups.
<!-- rule:1215 -->
- Name enum members in `UPPER_CASE` and keep casing uniform within an enum — mixed casing signals unintended distinction and breaks `Enum` value lookups that assume one style — These conventions keep enums stable across refactors and consistent across the codebase's Django subpackages (`db/migrations`, `db/models`, `template`), so imports and value comparisons don't break when implementations or attributes change.
  - Name enum classes after the domain concept they enumerate, not an incidental attribute — later attribute changes won't force a class rename and churn importers
  - Prefer category-based enum member names over names tied to one built-in implementation — swapping the underlying backend shouldn't require renaming members or callers
<!-- rule:2822 -->
- Match template tag functions to their registered tag names with `<feature>_<purpose>` — keeps tag, function, tests, and docstrings aligned — This convention prevents Django template tag registrations, docstrings, and tests from drifting apart—so `_attr` helpers aren't mistaken for raw values and `simple_block_tag` helpers aren't misused as inline tags.
  Suffix `_attr` on tag functions that emit HTML attributes — separates attribute output from raw values
  Use underscores for multi-word test helper tags in `tests/template_tests/templatetags/custom.py` — mirrors existing `simple_tag` helpers
  Describe `simple_block_tag` helpers as block tags in docstrings — their content-wrapping differs from inline `simple_tag`s
<!-- rule:5314 -->
- Pass arguments by explicit keyword, matching the callee's parameter names exactly (watch singular/plural) — e.g. `cache.set(timeout=60, instance=..., value=...)`; positional order drifts and name mismatches cause silent bugs. — The evidence spans 5 PRs across `db/models`, `contrib/auth`, `contrib/contenttypes`, `utils`, and `db/backends` — argument-passing conventions are a codebase-wide concern, not module-specific, so this belongs in the root `AGENTS.md` for maximum findability. Keyword args make call sites self-documenting and immune to parameter reorders in the callee. Exact-name matching (including singular vs. plural) prevents the subtle bug where a call succeeds with a `TypeError`-free positional bind to the wrong parameter, or where a same-named parameter in a different API has different semantics (a common trap when a function like `cache.set` and a helper like `set_cached_value` share identifier names but not meaning).
  - Verify a same-named parameter means the same thing in the target API before reusing it — e.g. `value`/`instance` in `set_cached_value` may not carry this codebase's semantics.
<!-- rule:576 -->
- Name internal storage/session keys by semantic role + namespace, not by mechanism or opaque value. — Descriptive names like `_password_reset_token` (not the raw URL token) let developers trace a key back to its owning component and survive refactors of the storage backend. Prefix framework-internal entries with `_` or a project namespace like `django.admin.` to signal private use and prevent collisions with user code. When the backend may vary by settings, keep the key mechanism-agnostic (e.g., `secret`/`token`) so the same key works across configurations.
<!-- rule:2861 -->
- Name UUID fields version-agnostically (`UUIDAutoField`), passing the UUID version as an argument — Version-suffixed names like `UUID4Field` force renames across `django/db/models`, `django/contrib/postgres/fields`, and DB backends once v7 support lands; only suffix when multiple UUID versions actually coexist, or when an established name like `UUID4Field` must be preserved for consistency.
<!-- rule:3737 -->
- Namespace env vars with the `DJANGO_` prefix (e.g., `DJANGO_<COMPONENT>`) — bare names like `DEBUG` collide with shell, CI, or system env vars — Project-scoped env var names avoid silent collisions with unrelated variables set by shells, CI systems, or other tools — a bare name like `DEBUG` or `TEST` can be inherited from the environment and change behavior unexpectedly. The `RUNNING_<PROJECT>_TEST_SUITE` pattern makes test-only flags obvious and distinct from production runtime configuration.
  - Name test-suite flags `RUNNING_<PROJECT>_TEST_SUITE` (e.g., `RUNNING_DJANGOS_TEST_SUITE`) — keeps test tooling flags identifiable and avoids clashes with other runners
<!-- rule:1442 -->
- Name flags and callable metadata after the exact condition they encode — `_from_testcase` not `_test_marker`, `has_transforms` not `has_lookups`; adjacent terms mislead as core mechanisms get reused — Broader or adjacent names like `_test_marker` or `has_lookups` drift from what the flag actually means, so readers get the wrong mental model once the mechanism is reused elsewhere — precise names like `_from_testcase` stay self-documenting as adoption grows.
<!-- rule:5392 -->
- Name collection-extraction helpers for their exact behavior (`first_selected`, `last_value`, `single_value`) — generic names like `get_value_from_parameters` hide whether the helper returns the first, last, or only match, forcing callers to read the body to know what they get. — Generic helper names hide whether a collection extraction returns the first, last, or only match, so callers may silently use the wrong value and introduce bugs unless they read the implementation.
<!-- rule:3668 -->
- Name template context variables after their UI label or producing accessor — keeps parallel templates (e.g. `technical_404.html`/`technical_500.html`) synchronized across refactors, avoiding passive-phrasing drift in Django admin and error-page views — The context naming rule matters because Django admin and error-page views share context shape across parallel templates (`technical_404.html`, `technical_500.html`). Aligning each context key with the accessor/attribute that produces it and with the label it renders prevents silent template breakage when one side is renamed during a refactor.
<!-- rule:2623 -->
