- Give each sentinel a purpose-specific name (e.g. `_SENTINEL`, `NOT_SPECIFIED`, `_missing_key`); define a new sentinel per semantic purpose instead of overloading `NOT_PROVIDED` or `None`. — Each sentinel must carry a single, unambiguous meaning wherever it appears. Overloading `NOT_PROVIDED` or `None` with an unrelated meaning makes call sites impossible to reason about locally — a branch may mean "argument omitted" in one place and "value is missing from the cache" in another, so reviewers and callers cannot tell which semantics apply without tracing every caller. Dedicated sentinels like `_missing_key` keep the meaning visible at the definition and prevent this class of subtle misuse across `django/db/models`, `django/db`, `django/utils`, `django/core/management/commands`, and `django/core/cache/backends`.
<!-- rule:6360 -->
- Name mapping dicts for their key→value relationship, e.g. `current_to_new_perms`, `action_to_msg` — readers can't infer contents from names like `correspondence` or `require_no_limits` — Dict names are the only documentation most call sites see; an `<old>_to_<new>` name states both sides of the mapping, while vague or condition-based names force readers to hunt down the definition.
<!-- rule:5409 -->
- Name table-filter helpers, boolean flags, and tests after the operation applied (`exclude_empty_tables`, `skip_empty`, `test_skip_empty_tables_*`), not the resulting subset (`only_nonempty_tables`) — inverse/predicate-style names fragment the introspection API and hide which helper is canonical. — The rule uses action-verb naming (matching the operation on the input) rather than naming after the resultant subset or its negation. This keeps the API composable, avoids mirror-image helpers that drift apart, and makes the filtering behavior discoverable across Django's introspection modules and SQLite backend.
  - Prefer composing existing helpers (call `exclude_empty_tables()` or pass `skip_empty` to `django_table_names()`) over adding an inverse helper — one filtering path across backends avoids divergent empty-table behavior.
<!-- rule:3584 -->
- Name JavaScript functions, object properties, and local variables in `camelCase`, not `snake_case` — this convention spans both `js_tests/` and `django/contrib/admin/static/admin/js`, so snake_case identifiers would look foreign and inconsistent across the repo's JS. — Consistent camelCase identifiers keep the JS in `js_tests/` and `django/contrib/admin/static/admin/js` idiomatic and interoperable, avoiding mixed-style naming that confuses readers and diverges from the surrounding JavaScript ecosystem.
<!-- rule:2675 -->
- Spell public namedtuple fields exactly as documented — `mimetype`, not `mime_type`, in `attachments`/`alternatives` (`django/core/mail/message.py`). Callers unpack by the documented name, so any drift silently breaks their attribute access. — The rule preserves the public-API contract for namedtuple-like return values. Documented spelling is a stable interface — users write `att.mimetype`, so introducing `mime_type` or another synonym creates a divergent API that fails at attribute access. Testing both positional and named access catches renames that only break one access path, and updating tests/docs together prevents stale references that mask breakage.
  - Exercise namedtuple fields in tests via both index and documented attribute name — positional-only tests pass even when a rename breaks attribute access.
  - Rename a field everywhere in the same change: tests and docs must match — stale names mislead users and hide the breakage until release.
<!-- rule:4816 -->
- Name cursor results by cardinality: `row` for `fetchone()` and per-row loop vars, `rows` for multi-row results — a `rows` loop variable or one named after a SQL column alias misreads as a full result set. — The singular/plural distinction carries meaning in DB-API code: `rows` signals a full result set while `row` signals one record. Naming a `fetchall()` loop variable `rows` — or after a column alias like `user_id` — makes the variable's shape ambiguous at the call site, leading to mistakes like indexing a scalar or iterating a single row. This convention recurs across backend modules (`mysql/introspection.py`, `oracle/schema.py`) and applies to any DB-API usage in the repo.
  - Never name a `fetchall()` loop variable after the SQL column alias it holds (e.g. `for user_id in cursor.fetchall()`) — hides that the variable is a row, not a scalar.
<!-- rule:2885 -->
- Reference methods by their exact defined name, correct async/sync variant, and `Class.method()` form where context helps — Log and exception messages stay greppable and unambiguous — matters in Django's `django/db/backends/base`, `django/db/models`, and `django/dispatch` where async/sync method pairs coexist, so a wrong variant sends readers to the wrong definition.
<!-- rule:5077 -->
- Name Django password-reset user identifiers `uidb64`/`uid`, not `uuid` — The value is a base64-encoded string, so use the plain `<uidb64>` path converter, never `<uuid:uidb64>` — the `uuid` converter rejects base64 and breaks reset links. Match `uid` terminology in `django/contrib/auth` views, URL patterns, `docs/ref/contrib/admin`, and `tests/auth_tests` so the identifier reflects what it actually is.
<!-- rule:2107 -->
- Name CSS classes in `kebab-case` by role, not element type — keeps styles reusable across templates. — Role-based kebab-case names like `.user-card` stay valid if the markup changes from `<div>` to `<article>`, and generic names can be shared across templates, docs, and tests that assert on them.
<!-- rule:4921 -->
- Validate Python identifiers with `str.isidentifier()`, not regexes — hand-written patterns can't express Python's Unicode-aware identifier grammar. — Regexes silently reject valid non-ASCII identifiers and mis-handle Unicode edge cases that `str.isidentifier()` covers exactly.
<!-- rule:3214 -->
- Name signal callbacks `receiver`, not after the event (e.g. `post_signal`) — matches Django's own terminology — Django refers to signal callbacks as 'receivers', so `receiver` and `receivers.py` match the framework's vocabulary; names like `post_signal` or `signal_handlers.py` drift from it, and parallel `signals.py`/`signal_handlers.py` modules create ambiguity about where registration lives.
  - Put signal receivers in `receivers.py` or `signal_receivers.py`, not `signal_handlers.py` — keeps names aligned with Django docs
  - Consolidate overlapping `signals.py` / `signal_handlers.py` into one module — prevents ambiguous naming and split responsibilities
<!-- rule:2615 -->
- In CSRF code and docs, name the unmasked session-independent value `secret`, the masked value sent in forms/cookies `token`, and reserve `nonce` for true single-use values; rename a variable when its underlying representation changes — distinct roles need distinct names or `django/middleware/csrf.py` and `docs/ref/csrf.txt` will conflate the masking layer with the session secret. — Prevents confusion between the session secret, the masked form value, and single-use nonces on both code and docs sides of the CSRF API.
<!-- rule:4306 -->
- Name locals after their source (`self.foo` → `foo`, dict/options keys verbatim), no leading underscores or ad-hoc prefixes — except when the name would shadow a symbol still in use. Keeps variable provenance traceable across layers (models, db backends, http, contrib.*) and prevents silent drift when the source is renamed. — Matching local names to their source of truth (superclass properties/methods, options/mapping keys) makes it obvious where a value came from and keeps call sites readable across the many unrelated modules that follow this pattern — `db`, `http`, `migrations`, `models`, `test`, `utils`, `contrib.admin`, `contrib.auth`. Decorative prefixes and underscore-stripping obscure provenance and break on rename; the shadowing exception exists because reusing a name that's still needed would clobber the outer symbol.
<!-- rule:4189 -->
- Name bulk ORM/DB operations by exact semantics — `bulk_update`, not `bulk_save`, for update-only paths — and give doc anchors/labels self-explanatory names like `methods-not-called-on-bulk-operations` instead of ticket shorthand; precise names let readers trust the API and docs without tracing code or hunting down old tickets. — Misleading method names (e.g. `bulk_save` on an update-only path) cause callers to assume behavior that doesn't exist, and ticket-number anchors become unreadable once the ticket context is gone.
<!-- rule:263 -->
- Derive arity from `inspect.signature` by adjusting the inspected count for an implicit `self`/`cls`, not by hardcoding — bound vs. unbound methods and C-level callables differ by one parameter, so a fixed number silently misclassifies them. — Prevents signature-matching bugs in `django/urls/resolvers.py` and `django/utils/inspect.py` when callables aren't plain Python methods.
<!-- rule:1036 -->
- Name the event parameter `event` (not `e`) and mirror parameter names in the body — mismatched or single-letter names (e.g. `filter_value` over `fv`) hide what a handler receives and invite stale-reference bugs when code is edited. — When the parameter is called `e` but the body reads `event`, or a local is abbreviated to one letter, readers and editors can't tell whether a rename was missed — copy-pasted handlers in the admin static JS tree (`cancel.js`, `nav_sidebar.js`) are a common place for this drift to go unnoticed.
<!-- rule:3173 -->
- Name task/job status enum members by outcome and keep one grammatical form — parallel adjectives (`READY`, `RUNNING`, `FAILED`, `SUCCESSFUL`) — rather than `COMPLETE` or `SUCCEEDED` — `COMPLETE` is ambiguous: a failed task has also terminated, so it conflates success with mere ending, and mixed past-participle forms like `SUCCEEDED` break the parallel grammar callers expect across Django `TextChoices` status enums. Reserve terminal success for an outcome-specific adjective so `if status == X` checks and labels read unambiguously.
<!-- rule:1181 -->
- Declare module-level `app_name` before `urlpatterns` in every URLconf module, including the `startapp` URLconf template — new apps then get URL namespace support by default, and consistent placement keeps the variable findable across all URLconf files. — Declaring module-level app_name before urlpatterns—including in the startapp template—prevents new apps from lacking URL namespace support so reverse() and {% url %} resolution work, and calling it a module-level variable rather than an attribute prevents the inconsistency of treating a module binding as a class member in code, docs, and discussion.
  - Refer to `app_name` as a "module-level variable", never an "attribute" — it is a module binding in URLconf modules, not a class member, and the wording keeps docs and discussion unambiguous.
<!-- rule:1306 -->
- When renaming or consolidating template tags/labels, use the canonical name, update every call site (templates, tag definitions, tests), and verify rendered output (class names, attributes) is preserved before dropping filters or variables. — Template tags and labels are consumed across templates, tag definitions, and tests — missing a call site silently breaks rendering, and dropping filters/variables can change emitted class names or attributes that styles and JS depend on.
<!-- rule:5145 -->
- Name lifecycle flags after the state they represent (`ready`, `loading`, `started`; follow existing conventions like `(apps_|models_|)ready`) — action-style names such as `_populate_running` describe a reset-able operation rather than the durable stage, leaving readers unable to tell when the state actually holds. Applies to one-time init modules like `django/apps/registry.py` and `django/db/models/base.py`. — Lifecycle flags in one-time initialization modules gate the transition from initialized to registered; naming them after reset-able actions (`_populate_running`) or commenting inaccurately ('yet') obscures which stage the code is in, which is exactly the distinction callers rely on.
  - In lifecycle comments, name the actual stage — write "till then" for fields that are initialized but not yet registered, not "yet" — so the comment doesn't overstate when registration completes.
<!-- rule:4769 -->
- Give non-importable template/scaffold files a `.tpl` suffix (`.py.tpl`, `.py-tpl`), never `.py` or `.pyx` — importers, linters, and test collectors would otherwise treat scaffolds as real modules, and the `.tpl` convention is already used across `tests/` and `.github/` — A plain source extension makes scaffolds look like loadable modules, so importers, linters, and test collectors try to execute them; the `.tpl` suffix sidesteps that while matching the pattern already established in `tests/` and `.github/`. Ad-hoc extensions like `.pyx` are avoided because they imply a real language variant and break editor/tooling detection.
<!-- rule:4797 -->
- Name partial-index predicate args `condition`; reserve `check` for `CheckConstraint` expressions — keeps `UniqueConstraint`/`Index` consistency. — `condition` mirrors partial-index terminology and is reusable across `UniqueConstraint` and friends, while `check` unambiguously means the boolean expression of a `CheckConstraint`; mixing them makes the constraint API read inconsistently for users and maintainers.
<!-- rule:969 -->
- Name non-prompting methods with `quit_`, `abort_`, `exit_`, or `terminate_` prefixes — not `ask_*` — so call sites reveal that they never return normally and readers don't expect a user prompt. — An `ask_*` prefix implies interactive prompting, so a method that silently exits misleads readers and hides a control-flow hazard at call sites.
<!-- rule:4543 -->
- Name user-facing CLI output-format enum values after the layout they produce (`columns`, `stack`, `tabular`, `stacked`), not vague quality labels (`aligned`, `verbose`); reuse project terms like Django admin's "inlines" when the layout matches. — Users pick a format by what it looks like, so `stack` is self-documenting while `verbose` tells them nothing about the rendered output — descriptive names also keep terminology consistent with existing conventions like Django admin inlines.
<!-- rule:5508 -->
- Name version-regex capture groups `major`/`minor`/`patch` — keeps version-parsing code consistent with semver terminology and prevents confusion from ad-hoc names like `subminor` — Capture-group names like `subminor` deviate from the semver terms everyone already knows, making version-parsing regexes harder to read and compare across the codebase (e.g. the one in `django/contrib/gis/gdal/libgdal.py`). Sticking to `major`/`minor`/`patch` lets readers map groups to semver fields without guessing.
<!-- rule:5594 -->
- Name `TimeKeeper`/`NullTimeKeeper` variables `time_keeper`, not by operation — the same variable may hold either type, so names like `run_keeper` mislead readers about what it is. — These variables can be reassigned between `TimeKeeper` and `NullTimeKeeper` (its no-op variant), so naming them after a specific timed operation obscures the actual type and its lifecycle. Naming after the helper type keeps the intent clear across both `django/core/management/commands/test.py` and `tests/runtests.py`.
<!-- rule:4366 -->
- Suffix string import paths with `_path` (e.g., `func_path`) and keep the resolved object under a distinct name (e.g., `func`) — prevents passing the raw string where the callable is expected — In deserialization/reconstruction helpers, the serialized string and its resolved runtime object are both live at once. Reusing one identifier for both hides which one flows into downstream calls, making it easy to pass the string where the callable is expected. The `_path` suffix makes the distinction visible at every use site.
<!-- rule:2600 -->
- Name security opt-out APIs/decorators after the unsafe state (e.g., `csp_unsafe`, `csp_off`), not `exempt` — `exempt` implies the view doesn't need protection, hiding the safety tradeoff; `unsafe`/`off` names make the disabled-state risk explicit at every call site.
<!-- rule:3235 -->
- Name base-class/interface modules `base.py` instead of reusing an entity name that shadows a public package symbol — e.g. avoid `django/tasks/task.py`-style collisions with the package's exported `Task` — Shadowing a public package-level symbol with a same-named module breaks `import <pkg>.<entity>` resolution and confuses callers; the `__future__` guard is required so a stdlib-named module (like `django/db/models/functions/date.py` or `datetime.py`) does not get picked up instead of the standard library on Python 2, where implicit relative imports are the default.
  - If a module basename matches a stdlib module (e.g. `date.py`, `datetime.py`), add `from __future__ import absolute_import` at the top — prevents implicit relative imports from resolving to the sibling module on Python 2
<!-- rule:6216 -->
- Prefix security-bypassing helpers with `force_` and keep the action+target explicit (e.g.. `force_login`) — a generic name like `login_user` implies real authentication and hides that the helper skips checks such as `is_active` (see `django/test/client.py`). — the bug, incompatibility, or inconsistency it prevents. Write it for a reader who only ever sees the final text.
  - Apply the same `force_` prefix to any helper that bypasses a security check (auth, permissions, active-user guards), not just login.
<!-- rule:3424 -->
- Name string utils for the full transform (`strip_quotes`), not a partial aspect — callers otherwise misread what the function actually does. — A name like `strip_quote` suggests single-character handling, so callers expect different behavior than the leading-and-trailing removal the function performs.
<!-- rule:5428 -->
- Use `parent process`/`child process` (not `master`/`slave`) in code, comments, docstrings, and docs — consistent, inclusive terminology across process hierarchies — Using `master`/`slave` in some places and `parent process`/`child process` in others splits the codebase into two vocabularies for the same hierarchy, so readers and search tools can no longer reliably connect a process reference in code to its counterpart in comments or docs — and the older terms carry exclusionary connotations that don't belong in shipped terminology.
<!-- rule:2614 -->
- Name decorator params neutrally (`obj`, `decorated`, `func_or_class`) — supports both functions and classes — Decorators that can wrap either functions or classes (e.g., in `django/test/utils.py`) shouldn't name their parameter `function` — misleading when a class is passed prevents confusion about what the decorator accepts.
<!-- rule:4202 -->
- Prefix functions and context managers that defer evaluation with `lazy_{subject}` (e.g. `lazy_deferred_annotations` in `django/utils/inspect.py`) — a short, uniform prefix makes deferral obvious at the call site and groups related helpers together in sorted listings, rather than burying the intent in a long descriptive phrase like `leave_deferred_annotations_unevaluated`. — A one-word `lazy_` marker lets readers recognize deferred work instantly and keeps names alphabetically clustered, while verbose phrasing hides the intent and produces names that are hard to scan or grep.
<!-- rule:4393 -->
- Name display-only config options with `display` in the name (e.g. `max_display_items`) — signals they truncate output, not data retrieval, preventing users from expecting fewer objects to be fetched or processed — Users and callers reading a config name like `max_items` can't tell whether it limits what's fetched/processed or just what's shown; the `display` token plus a docstring noting retrieval is unaffected prevents wrong assumptions about data volume, pagination, and side effects.
<!-- rule:1792 -->
- Use `value` for property setter parameters, not `has_z`/`has_m`-style names — setters are invoked as `obj.attr = x`, so callers never see the parameter name, and `value` keeps the signature uniform across analogous setters — Setters are invoked via assignment (`geom.has_z = True`), so the parameter name is invisible to callers. Using `value` consistently across all setters avoids gratuitous variation and makes signatures predictable when reading or reviewing code.
<!-- rule:6401 -->
- Name `__repr__` option helpers `_get_repr_options`, not `get_options` — private prefix and purpose-specific name keep it from colliding with or masquerading as a public API. — A generic `get_options` on a class reads as a public accessor and can clash with real public methods, while `_get_repr_options` signals it is an internal formatting helper tied to `__repr__`. The `_` prefix discourages external callers and the specific name makes its role discoverable across any class that defines `__repr__`.
<!-- rule:209 -->
- Name metaclasses with a `Meta` suffix (e.g., `FooMeta`) — reserve `Base` for actual base classes so metaclasses aren't confused with the classes they create. — A metaclass describes itself as a base class of its instances through the `Base` suffix, which is misleading; since `FooMeta` creates `Foo`, readers scanning class names can't tell whether `FooBase` is a parent to subclass or a metaclass. The `Meta` suffix is the Python convention (cf. `abc.ABCMeta`, `type`'s subclasses), so following it makes metaclasses greppable and unambiguous.
<!-- rule:2913 -->
- Use `media type(s)` (never `format` or mixed `format`/`type`) for HTTP content-negotiation APIs and docs — name methods/params after existing `accepted_types`/`accepted_type` (e.g. `get_preferred_type`, not `get_preferred_format`) and write 'specificity from 0-3' instead of 'level' — mixed or historically-loaded terms contradict the shipped API names and confuse readers navigating code and docs together. — The HTTP content-negotiation surface already exposes `accepted_types`/`accepted_type` and `get_preferred_type`. Introducing `format`/`type` synonyms or an Accept `level` term splits the vocabulary between code and docs, making the canonical API hard to discover and search for.

## Dependencies

<!-- rule:1527 -->
- Never edit files under `vendor/` paths (e.g. `django/contrib/admin/static/admin/js/vendor/`) — changes are lost on the next vendor-sync. Land fixes upstream, apply project-specific overrides in project-owned files, and exclude `vendor/` from bulk search-and-replace or refactors. — Vendored assets are third-party copies refreshed by the dependency/vendor-sync process; local edits are silently overwritten and diverge from upstream, so project-specific behavior belongs in override files and upstream bugs in the upstream repo.
<!-- rule:569 -->
- Guard new runtime/dependency features behind the minimum supported version — verify support before importing, else raise the floor or add a version check with fallback — Importing features newer than the minimum floor (e.g. `AsyncSingleThreadContext` from asgiref PR 511) breaks installs on older Pythons/deps; guard `uuid.uuid7()` with `PY314` and fall back to `uuid.uuid4()`, and only use `@dataclass(slots=True)`/`@dataclass(kw_only=True)` once the floor reaches 3.10+.
<!-- rule:445 -->
- Guard optional deps (`colorama`, GDAL in `django/contrib/gis/db/models/fields.py`) with `try: import dep` / `except ImportError`, recording availability as `dep = None` or a `HAS_<DEP>` flag (e.g. `HAS_COLORAMA`) — keeps core importable without third-party packages installed. — The rule prevents Django core from requiring optional third-party packages. Detect availability once at module import (try/except ImportError, set `dep = None` or `HAS_<DEP>` flag), then branch on that flag wherever the dep is used. Lazy imports in core code like GIS fields are acceptable only if guarded. This avoids runtime crashes and unnecessary import overhead.
  - Branch on that availability check at every downstream use (attribute access, init, behavior paths); lazy imports (e.g. in GIS fields) are fine but must still be guarded — prevents `ImportError`/`AttributeError` at runtime when the dep is absent.
  - Avoid re-importing inside hot functions or referencing the dep module unconditionally — repeated imports add overhead and unguarded references crash when the dep is missing.
<!-- rule:3588 -->
- Check transitive deps before bumping a `package.json` version — a newer release can pull in a vulnerable transitive dependency — Condensed the multi-step upgrade/skip/pin workflow into scannable bullets with the *why* attached to each: transitive-vuln check, same-major pinning to avoid breakage, auditability of security skips, and the tracking-ticket-vs-inline-comment distinction (inline test comments and upstream URLs rot silently). Preserved project-specific references (`package.json`, tracking ticket, pin location) while dropping the file/dir scope archaeology that doesn't help a reader acting on the rule.
  - If an upgrade introduces a vulnerable transitive dep, stay on the latest safe version within the existing major range — avoids both the vulnerability and a breaking major bump
  - For a skipped upgrade due to a security advisory, record the specific vulnerable package and dependency path — makes the decision auditable and reviewable
  - For dependency-driven blockers that can't be fixed via config (e.g., tooling upgrades causing CI-only failures tied to environment or cache behavior): pin the last known-working version, flag any prerequisite so the pin isn't lifted early, and document the reason/upstream status in the tracking ticket or next to the pin — not as inline test comments or raw upstream URLs, which go stale and hide intent
  - When the root cause is upstream, prefer filing a fix against the dependency over a local workaround — workarounds accumulate and mask the real bug
<!-- rule:4298 -->
- Commit lockfiles (`package-lock.json`, `uv.lock`) only if the project explicitly tracks one — delete any that slip into a PR. Stray lockfiles in root, subpackages, or nested tooling silently pin transitive deps and contradict the project's install/reproducibility policy, which is set per-project rather than per-directory. — Lockfiles can appear at root, in subpackages, or under nested tooling, so a directory-scoped check misses them. Committing one the project hasn't adopted pins transitive dependencies unexpectedly and diverges from the intended install flow.
<!-- rule:699 -->
- Reuse existing classes, helpers, and stdlib features before writing custom code. — Duplicate implementations drift, miss edge cases, and add maintenance surface that upstream code already covers.
<!-- rule:3941 -->
- Order assets dependencies-first — prepend dependency-free files (images), list libraries (`jQuery`) before the scripts that use them — so `staticfiles._topological_sort` and admin `media` definitions don't emit `<script>`/`<link>` tags in an order that loads a dependent before its dependency. — Asset ordering in `django/contrib/staticfiles` and `django/contrib/admin` is purely ordinal — nothing downstream re-sorts it. If a dependency is emitted after its dependent, browsers execute scripts against undefined globals (e.g., a script calling `jQuery` before jQuery loads), producing runtime errors that are invisible in Django's own tests. Applying the same dependencies-before-dependents rule in both modules keeps their output consistent and avoids per-call-site fixes.
<!-- rule:4824 -->
- Pin CI service container images to explicit version tags and reuse the same tag across all jobs — unpinned or mismatched images make test environments non-reproducible. — Service containers running different image versions per job cause flaky, environment-specific test failures that are hard to reproduce locally.
<!-- rule:5169 -->
- Register psycopg loaders/dumpers per-connection (via Django's `connection_created` signal), not globally — avoids cross-connection state leaks across Django backends (`postgresql/base.py`, `postgis/base.py`, `json.py`). — Global adapter mutation leaks between connections and breaks isolation; per-connection registration via `connection_created` keeps each backend self-contained.
<!-- rule:4082 -->
- Derive external data from dependency APIs/source; never hardcode copies — local snapshots (e.g. `eslint.globals.mjs`-style globals, `django/contrib/admin/tests.py` fixtures) silently drift when the dependency changes. — Copies of package globals, datasets, or third-party mappings go stale on dependency upgrades and cause bugs that look like local logic errors. If a copy is truly unavoidable, keep only the subset actually consumed so the sync surface — and the blast radius when the dependency changes — stays small.
<!-- rule:1627 -->
- Treat undocumented upstream behavior as unsupported — add a regression test or document the assumption — Prevents silent breakage when upstream libraries change behavior users never agreed to depend on; an upstream regression test or explicit comment makes the assumption visible and fixable.
<!-- rule:6002 -->
- Prune unused deps from `package.json` — keeps installs lean and cuts supply-chain/audit noise. — Unused entries still get installed, inflate `package-lock.json`, slow CI, and show up in vulnerability/audit reports even though nothing imports them — making it unclear which deps are real.
  Only keep packages actually imported by source, scripts, or build/test config; stale entries still get resolved in `package-lock.json` and expand the audit surface.
<!-- rule:6093 -->
- Use explicit `https://` URLs in widget `Media` and `js_tests/tests.html`; update both on asset/CDN bumps — a stale fixture tests a different asset than production ships. — Protocol-relative `//` URLs can silently resolve differently in test environments, and a fixture left behind after a version or CDN URL bump means JS tests exercise a different asset than production serves.
<!-- rule:466 -->
- **Match module/symbol existence to actual usage** — no orphaned modules or classes with zero references anywhere (remove them or wire them into the relevant code path in the same change), and never delete a module still imported elsewhere (grep all usages across related subsystems and update them before deletion). Orphaned code drifts from reality and dead imports break the build when files shift. — Both adding unreferenced modules and deleting still-imported ones create the same failure mode: the codebase's actual dependency graph diverges from what the files claim. Orphaned classes rot silently (no tests exercise them, no callers catch their bugs), while deleting a module still imported in a sibling subsystem — e.g. `django/db/backends/oracle` vs `django/db/models` — breaks imports the moment the tree is loaded. Keeping existence aligned with usage is the invariant, not the direction of the edit.
<!-- rule:6411 -->
- Don't discover or recursively reload related models in `ProjectState.reload_model` when `delay=True` — let field operations (`AlterField`, `RenameField` in `django/db/migrations/operations/fields.py`) decide which related models to reload by checking `field.primary_key`/`ForeignKey` usage; eager recursion breaks deferred state reconciliation. — The `delay=True` contract exists so field operations, not state reloading, own related-model dependency decisions. Reloading eagerly from `ProjectState.reload_model` (e.g. on `AlterField`/`RenameField` in `django/db/migrations/operations/fields.py`) double-processes or misorders dependencies that should have been gated by `field.primary_key`/`ForeignKey` checks in the operation, corrupting migration state.
<!-- rule:1105 -->
- **Test suite dependencies:** — Unpinned `npx` fetches arbitrary versions at test time, and forcing every contributor to install Node just to run `django/test/selenium.py` breaks the plain-Python workflow; explicit `package.json` declarations, CI wiring, and docs keep the extra tooling reproducible and skippable.
  - Declare non-Python test prerequisites (e.g. the Node/npm CLI used by `django/test/selenium.py`) in `package.json`, invoke them directly instead of via `npx`, wire the install (Node.js/npm) into the GitHub Actions workflow, document the local setup steps, and keep the dependency opt-in — unpinned `npx` invocations and mandatory setup break reproducible, self-contained Python test runs.

## Type System

<!-- rule:1017 -->
- Do not introduce inline Python type hints (PEP 484/526) into Django code — this requires a formal DEP, not a PR. — Signatures across `django/`, `tests/`, and `docs/` extensions are intentionally untyped; adding annotations piecemeal fragments the style and blocks any future project-wide adoption decision. Use `# type:` comments instead, and do not add `typing` imports to modules that lack them.
<!-- rule:1558 -->
- **Keep variables single-typed for their whole lifetime** — reset collections to `[]`/`deque()`, never `None`, and use separate variables for each type a value might represent. — Reassigning a name to a different type breaks type checkers and forces defensive `None`/`isinstance` checks throughout callers; a collection that flips to `None` silently turns later `.append()`/iteration into runtime errors.
<!-- rule:5690 -->
- Match literal type to data type: use `b'...'` with `bytes`, `'...'` with `str` — mismatched compare/`startswith`/`endswith` literals raise `TypeError` or silently never match. In helpers handling either type, branch on `isinstance(line, bytes)` instead of assuming one. In tests, pass a real `bytes` object (never a `str` repr like `"b'...'"`) so bytes-guarded branches actually execute. — Matching literal type to data type prevents Python 3 bytes/str mismatches from raising `TypeError` or silently failing to match, while branching on `isinstance(line, bytes)` in mixed-type helpers and passing real `bytes` objects in tests ensures the bytes code path is actually exercised instead of skipped via a `str` repr.
<!-- rule:4619 -->
- Keep declared return/yield types consistent across every branch of a helper or generator — callers must be able to treat all results uniformly — Inconsistent branch types in ORM field/SQL helpers surface as hard-to-trace failures when callers iterate, unpack, or resolve the result, since tuple-vs-scalar and `Col`-vs-scalar mismatches only break on the branch path that wasn't exercised.
    - For batched/recursive vs single-item paths (e.g. `django/db/models/fields`, `django/db/models/sql`), initialize accumulators with the same container type as the single-item return (a tuple when the single path returns `fetchone()`), so downstream unpacking doesn't break
    - In column/type-dispatch helpers (`django/db/backends/sqlite3`), yield `Col` expression nodes on every branch — never mix `Col` instances with raw scalars like `expr.name` or `F`-resolved values, which fail when callers expect expression nodes
    - When a mismatch is found, fix the implementation (or the stale comment) and add a regression test covering the corrected return type and each branch
<!-- rule:1590 -->
- Key param-dedup caches on `(type(param), param)`, never the bare value — `True == 1` and (Py2) `b'foo' == u'foo'` collapse distinct types, mis-binding params (cf. `django/db/backends/oracle/base.py`) — Python equality treats some cross-type values as equal, so a bare-value cache key silently merges distinct parameters; unify on the type-tagged key to keep each typed param separate.
<!-- rule:2643 -->
- Normalize booleans/sentinels at construction, not at use (`inline`, `DatabaseDefault`, `[True, CSP.NONCE]`) — downstream code shouldn't special-case mixed types. — Storing mixed-type values (e.g. `inline = "inline"` vs `False`, a `DatabaseDefault` where only `True`/`False`/`None` are expected, or `[True, CSP.NONCE]`) forces every consumer to filter, dict-lookup, or type-check later — normalize at parse time, dict construction, or loop setup instead.

When building consumer-facing dicts, assign boolean flags unconditionally (e.g. `model_dict['view_only'] = not perms.get('change')`) so the key is always present and readers need no missing-key handling.
<!-- rule:637 -->
- Match helper return types to consumption context: byte-level helpers (binary assembly, concatenation, binary formats) return `bytes` and own their encoding; text helpers like `format_message()` return `str`, with `.encode()` deferred to the I/O boundary (e.g. `write_message()`). — Keeps encoding at a single, predictable boundary — avoids mid-path str/bytes churn that causes double-encoding and UnicodeDecodeError bugs.
<!-- rule:3673 -->
- Use `@overload` with `Literal[True]`/`Literal[False]` for boolean flags that change return type — Lets type checkers resolve the exact return type per call site instead of a union or `Any` — callers get accurate autocomplete and type errors around flag-dependent results.
<!-- rule:5560 -->
- Join aggregated validator `help_text` into one string, never return a list — `help_text` is string-typed, so a list breaks form rendering; add tests asserting the result is a `str` and contains each validator's help text. — Django's `help_text` field option expects a string; helpers that stack validator help texts (as in `django/contrib/auth/models.py`) must return a joined string or form/field rendering breaks. Tests guarding `str` type and inclusion of validator texts catch regressions when validators are added or refactored.

## Deprecation

<!-- rule:3667 -->
- Treat template context as public API — preserve existing key names and value shapes when refactoring overridable context-building views (`history_view`, `render_change_form`, `each_context`) and error/debug templates; downstream templates and view subclasses depend on them. — Renaming, deleting, or silently changing context values breaks user templates and overrides without warning. Keep keys present (`None`/falsy placeholders are fine) and expose new or enriched data under new keys (e.g. `*_debug`) or in a structurally compatible form. If a key must be removed or renamed, retain the old name as a deprecated compatibility alias that emits a `DeprecationWarning` on access so downstream code can migrate.
<!-- rule:185 -->
- Gate `@python_2_unicode_compatible` on actual Python 2 support — in Py2 codebases, decorate Django classes that define `__str__` (imported from `django.utils.encoding`), but omit it where `__str__` isn't defined; in Py3-only codebases (Django 2.0+), drop the decorator and its import and rely on native `__str__`. — The decorator exists solely to bridge Python 2's `__unicode__`/`__str__` split; applying it to classes without `__str__` adds dead indirection, and leaving it in Django 2.0+ code (e.g. `django/db/models`, `django/channels`, `tests/`) carries a removed dependency and obscures native `__str__` behavior.
<!-- rule:2932 -->
- Gate the deprecated-email migration warning on `has_providers = hasattr(settings, "EMAIL_PROVIDERS")` and `has_deprecated` booleans in `django/conf/__init__.py` / `django/core/mail/deprecation.py` — emit only when neither is set; avoids spurious warnings for projects that never configure email. — Projects that never touch email settings would otherwise see a spurious deprecation warning about migrating to `EMAIL_PROVIDERS`. Splitting into named `has_providers` / `has_deprecated` booleans (instead of a combined `needs_warning` intermediate) keeps the settings-detection logic in `django/conf/__init__.py` aligned with the guard in `django/core/mail/deprecation.py`, so both modules agree on exactly when the warning fires.
<!-- rule:760 -->
- In Django field `formfield()` overrides, delegate to the base `formfield()`/`to_python` instead of reimplementing coercion — hand-rolled versions hide deprecations users need to see. — Form-field overrides that bypass the base `formfield()`/`to_python` or inject compat shims suppress deprecation warnings users rely on to prepare for behavior changes; warnings must fire across all form-construction paths (including admin `form_class`/`formfield_for_dbfield`) so no users are caught off guard.
  - Never add compat shims (e.g. `assume_scheme='http'` in `URLField.formfield()`) to silence deprecations — users on default form fields must be exposed to breaking behavior changes.
  - Surface deprecations on every form-construction path, including admin `form_class`/`formfield_for_dbfield` — partial coverage leaves admin users blindsided at upgrade.
<!-- rule:6227 -->
- Don't pass positional `current_app` args to Django view/URL constructors — use `request.current_app` only where still required, and delete docs telling users to set `self.request.current_app` in class-based views. — Positional `current_app` is deprecated and breaks on newer Django, and CBVs resolve `current_app` automatically — stale docs recommending manual assignment push users into redundant overrides that shadow Django's resolution.
    Positional `current_app` is deprecated and breaks on newer Django, and CBVs resolve `current_app` automatically — stale docs recommending manual assignment push users into redundant overrides that shadow Django's resolution.
<!-- rule:6284 -->
- When manager-handling behavior is removed, delete orphaned internals like `Options.abstract_managers` and `Options.concrete_managers` in `django/db/models/options.py`, and reclassify the `docs/releases/*.txt` entry as 'Removed', not 'Fixed' — users scanning release notes for breakage rely on 'Removed' to flag dropped APIs; mislabeling it as 'Fixed' hides the removal and leaves dangling internal attributes that no code path reads. — Dropped manager APIs and their backing options attributes (`Options.abstract_managers`, `Options.concrete_managers`) become dead code once the behavior is gone, and release notes that call the change 'Fixed' mislead upgraders who scan for 'Removed' entries to find breaking changes.
<!-- rule:2781 -->
- Only apply `@deprecate_posargs` to documented public APIs, not undocumented constructors or internal backends — deprecation warnings are a public compatibility promise, and emitting them for internals forces users to migrate APIs they were never meant to rely on. — Deprecation warnings are a public compatibility promise. Emitting them from undocumented constructors or internal backends (e.g. `django/core/mail/backends`) implies those APIs are part of the supported surface, which forces users to migrate interfaces they were never meant to rely on and locks Django into maintaining them.
<!-- rule:6395 -->
- Delete whitelist/allowlist workarounds and their parameters (e.g. `EmailValidator.whitelist`) once validation is relaxed — don't keep no-op or deprecated stubs — A no-op or deprecated parameter still appears in signatures, docs, and stack traces, so callers keep passing it and new code keeps discovering it; removing it lets users migrate to the now-relaxed validation instead of an empty flag.
<!-- rule:5379 -->
- Exclude weak algorithms (e.g., `MD5`) from allowed sets; legacy algos only as explicit, removable exceptions — prevents insecure defaults. — Ship replacement algorithms alongside the old default before removing it, and gate any legacy algorithm behind an explicit, temporary exception with a planned removal — silently allowing weak algorithms locks in insecure defaults across all code that selects algorithms.
<!-- rule:3293 -->
- Use `glob.glob(pattern, root_dir=directory)` for base-dir globbing — `glob.glob1` is removed in Python 3.13, and `os.path.join`-ing the directory into the pattern makes wildcard chars in the path (`*`, `[`, `?`) parse as pattern syntax. — `glob.glob1` is deprecated and removed in newer Python, so scoping requires `root_dir`. Passing the base directory via `os.path.join` breaks when the directory itself contains glob metacharacters. Because `root_dir` yields relative names, naively returning them violates APIs whose docstrings promise absolute paths.
  - Re-join results with `os.path.join(directory, fname)` when the helper's contract promises full paths — `glob.glob(..., root_dir=...)` returns only relative filenames.
<!-- rule:4258 -->
- Explicitly reject the old RFC format when migrating a header parser (e.g. `ETag` from RFC 2616) — silent acceptance keeps legacy clients alive and lets malformed values parse as valid values — A parser that accepts both formats can't tell a legacy client from a malformed one, so old-format senders never get fixed and bad input silently becomes a valid parsed value downstream (see `django/utils/http.py` for the `ETag` case). Rejecting the superseded format makes the migration boundary explicit.

## Imports

<!-- rule:4759 -->
- Don't instantiate Django `Field` subclasses at module import time (e.g. `_output_field = fields.XField()`) — field init needs `settings` configured, so build them at runtime inside functions/methods (pass `output_field` to `__init__`). — Import-time `Field()` construction breaks any import path where settings aren't yet loaded; if imports fail with `ImproperlyConfigured`, fix the entry point with `django.setup()` before other Django imports instead of adding lazy-resolution indirection.
<!-- rule:4975 -->
- Compare class objects or real paths (`module.__file__`, `os.path.samefile`, `Path.resolve`) when resolving from `sys.modules`/`sys.argv[0]` — name and `str.endswith` matches silently pick the wrong target — Name-only or suffix-based matching produces false positives when modules/classes share names or path fragments; object/path identity comparisons (`os.path.samefile`, `Path.resolve`, direct class check) guarantee the correct target is resolved.
<!-- rule:3191 -->
- Auto-discover per-app modules (e.g. `tasks.py`) via `django.utils.module_loading.autodiscover_modules` — not a custom `import_module` loop; default to the conventional name and allow override via a setting or `autodiscover_*(related_name=...)` parameter — reuses Django's loader and avoids clashes with third-party conventions like Celery. — Reuses Django's loader instead of a custom `import_module` loop, and the override path keeps discovery compatible with established third-party conventions (e.g. Celery's module names).
<!-- rule:3177 -->
- Restrict dynamic imports (`import_string`) to trusted static paths; extract repeated dynamic import patterns into a helper instead of hard-coding target strings — DB and task-result values are untrusted input that can resolve arbitrary code paths, and per-call-site strings drift over time — DB and task-result values are untrusted input  resolving them via `import_string` can execute arbitrary code, and repeated hard-coded import strings drift across call sites, causing inconsistency and making the import surface hard to audit
<!-- rule:2549 -->
- Only reconstruct imports for functions/classes exposing `__module__`; skip dotted `__qualname__` — nested classes/methods have no valid top-level import statement to emit (e.g. `shell.py`'s `--verbosity` auto-import). — Nested classes and methods have no importable top-level name, so emitting their dotted `__qualname__` as an import path produces broken or unimportable statements; restricting to objects with `__module__` and non-dotted `__qualname__` keeps reconstructed imports valid.
<!-- rule:5758 -->
- Update `deconstruct`/import paths for every genuine class-family member, special-casing non-members — partial or over-broad moves break imports; verify each resolves from the target module. — Inconsistent moves leave some classes unimportable from the new module while others silently ride along, breaking downstream consumers.
<!-- rule:5028 -->
- Import `reduce` from `functools` (`from functools import reduce`) — it's not a Python 3 built-in. — Referencing bare `reduce` raises `NameError` in Python 3, so any module using it (e.g. `django/contrib/admin/options.py`) must import it explicitly from `functools`.
<!-- rule:2761 -->
- Count dots in relative imports to match package depth — `polls/management/commands/foo.py` imports `polls.models` as `...models`, not `.models`; wrong dot counts cause `ImportError` at runtime, not at lint time. — Relative import depth is easy to miscount in deeply nested directories like Django's `management/commands/`, and mistakes only surface at runtime.
<!-- rule:2560 -->
- Prefer `types.SimpleNamespace` over `argparse.Namespace` for lightweight attribute containers — with a minimal local fallback on Python versions lacking it — so modules don't import `argparse` purely to hold attributes. — Importing `argparse` just to instantiate `Namespace` pulls in a heavy CLI-parsing module for something `types.SimpleNamespace` already provides, obscuring intent and adding unnecessary import weight; the fallback keeps older supported Python versions working.

## General

<!-- rule:1991 -->
- Scope to Django management commands that process, omit, or auto-import batches: report with progressive disclosure — a single trailing summary line at default verbosity (e.g., `"Imported N objects"`, `"N objects not displayed"`) rather than per-item, per-level, or inline per-type messages — Preserves the rule's specifics: progressive disclosure, default vs -v 2 levels, the "Imported N objects"/"N objects not displayed" example phrasings, the per-model/per-type breakdown requirement for cross-type batches, and the "use -v 2 for details" failure hint. Scope is anchored to Django management commands handling batches. Each bullet states what to do and why (readability, ambiguity avoidance, discoverability), not just "the rule says so."
  When omitted/processed items span multiple models/types, include the per-model/per-type breakdown inside that same summary line — a total-only count is otherwise ambiguous
  Reserve itemized per-item details for `-v 2`, and append a `"use -v 2 for details"` hint to the summary when failures exist — keeps default output scannable while pointing users to detail on demand
  Rationale: Matches Django's CLI verbosity conventions; keeps large batch runs readable and avoids noisy per-item output that obscures the outcome.
<!-- rule:4973 -->
- Rule: In `deconstruct()` and migration serializers, emit the public import path for built-in/re-exported API classes whose `__module__` differs; remap only known internal modules and leave custom or unknown subclasses on their real `__module__`, normalizing sibling public classes the same way (e.g. `django/db/models/expressions.py`, `django/contrib/postgres/serializer.py`). — Keeps previously-written migrations loading when a class is re-exported or moved internally, while custom subclasses still round-trip to the module users actually imported them from — inconsistent normalization would break one side or the other.
  Rationale: Keeps previously-written migrations loading when a class is re-exported or moved internally, while custom subclasses still round-trip to the module users actually imported them from — inconsistent normalization would break one side or the other.

## Topic Guides

Check these when working in specific areas:

- **[Test Coverage & Regression Tests](agent_docs/test-coverage-and-regression.md)**: When adding or changing behavior and deciding what tests to write (branches, boundaries, edge cases, regression cases)
- **[Pythonic Idioms & Iteration](agent_docs/pythonic-idioms.md)**: When writing Python control flow, iterations, or value handling and choosing the idiomatic construct

## File-Specific Rules

### `**/*widget*.js`

<!-- rule:6037 -->
- Co-assign `width` and `height` inline styles from `data-width`/`data-height`, adding `!important` when external CSS may override — a half-applied dimension stretches the widget — Setting only one dimension leaves the widget with a mismatched aspect ratio and lets external CSS silently win; co-assigning both (with `!important` where needed) keeps the dataset-driven size intact.

### `**/.eslintrc.json|.eslintrc.js|.eslintrc.cjs|eslint.config.js|eslint.config.mjs`

<!-- rule:376 -->
- Enable `eqeqeq` in `smart` mode in the ESLint config — flags `==`/`!=` typos, keeps the `== null` idiom legal — Loose equality silently coerces types (e.g. `'' == 0` is `true`), whereas `smart` mode still permits the deliberate `== null` / `!= null` null-or-undefined check, so enabling it catches accidental `==` without forcing rewrites of that idiom.

### `.github/pull_request_template.md`

<!-- rule:2041 -->
- Require UI-change screenshots in `.github/pull_request_template.md` — light and dark, at mobile/tablet/desktop sizes, via a `# UI Changes` section or final checklist item — Size classes instead of exact pixel breakpoints keep the template valid as breakpoints change; light/dark and responsive screenshots catch theme and layout regressions before review.

### `.gitignore`

<!-- rule:361 -->
- Keep repo `.gitignore` for project-wide build/test artifacts only — keeps it relevant to every contributor, not just your machine. — Concentrating only project-wide artifacts in the shared `.gitignore` keeps the file meaningful to all contributors and avoids imposing one developer's IDE or local virtualenv layout on everyone else. Routing personal paths to global gitignore, `.git/info/exclude`, or the tool's own `.gitignore` isolates machine-specific noise without polluting version control. Using exact entries such as `.venv/` instead of prefix globs like `venv*/` prevents accidental over-matching that could hide real files or directories from git.
  - Don't add developer-local paths (`.idea/`, `.vscode/`, `*.iml`, `venv/`, `.venv/`, `env/`) to `.gitignore` — put them in a global gitignore, `.git/info/exclude`, or the virtualenv tool's internal `.gitignore` so contributors' setups stay untouched.
  - If an environment ignore is genuinely required, use exact convention entries like `.venv/` rather than prefix globs like `venv*/` — broad globs can silently hide legitimate project paths.

### `.pre-commit-config.yaml`

<!-- rule:2469 -->
- In `.pre-commit-config.yaml`, keep hook entries canonical: current upstream `repo` URL, `rev` matching the repo's tag format (e.g. `v`-prefixed), and the base hook `id` (e.g. `biome`) unless multiple command hooks from that `repo` are expected — stale URLs or mismatched `rev` values break `pre-commit autoupdate` and hook resolution, and non-base `id`s silently duplicate hook config. — Pre-commit resolves hooks by `repo` + `rev` + `id`. A stale `repo` URL makes the entry unresolvable; a `rev` that doesn't match the upstream tag scheme (e.g. plain `1.5.0` where the repo tags `v1.5.0`) fails clone/install; and pinning a command-specific `id` when the base hook suffices means `pre-commit autoupdate` won't refresh it correctly and the entry drifts from upstream defaults.
<!-- rule:2468 -->
- List all required npm packages explicitly in `additional_dependencies` for local `language: node` pre-commit hooks in `.pre-commit-config.yaml` — pre-commit runs hooks in isolated environments and ignores `package.json`, so missing entries break hook execution. — Pre-commit builds each local `language: node` hook’s environment in isolation and does not consult the repo’s `package.json`; omitting a package from `additional_dependencies` causes the hook to fail at runtime even though the repo’s dependencies include it.

### `AUTHORS`

<!-- rule:2259 -->
- Keep `AUTHORS` entries alphabetically sorted and unique with valid emails — appending or duplicating breaks the file's ordering convention and lets invalid contributor data slip in. — The `AUTHORS` file maintains contributors in alphabetical order (by surname or first name). Insert new entries at their correct position rather than appending or grouping near similar names, and check for existing entries before adding so each contributor appears exactly once — duplicates and misplaced entries make the file hard to scan and unreliable as a contributor record. Ensure each email contains `@` so entries stay valid.

### `README.rst`

<!-- rule:5764 -->
- Keep `README.rst` free of emoji and GitHub-only markup like `:green_heart:`, using plain-text headings and content — PyPI, terminals, editors, and static docs must render it readably outside GitHub. — GitHub-only markup such as `:green_heart:` shortcodes and headings with emoji show up as literal text in PyPI, terminals, editors, and static doc builders, so the README becomes unreadable for users who never visit the repo on GitHub.

### `eslint.config.js`

<!-- rule:2567 -->
- Declare ESLint globals inline in `eslint.config.js` (`languageOptions.globals`) — avoid the `globals` package dependency. — Keeping globals in `eslint.config.js` avoids an extra dev dependency in `package.json` and keeps the config self-contained, so anyone editing the file sees every global without cross-referencing `package.json`.

### `pa11y.json`

<!-- rule:581 -->
- Define URL-specific actions inside the matching `urls[]` entry in `pa11y.json` as `{ "url": "...", "actions": [...] }`, not a top-level `actions` array — top-level actions run against every URL, and the entry's `url` already handles navigation so a `navigate to` action is redundant. — In pa11y/pa11y-ci configs like `pa11y.json`, a global top-level `actions` array is applied to every URL, causing unintended post-navigation steps to run on unrelated pages. Scoping actions to their matching `urls[]` entry keeps them tied to the page under test, and because the entry's `url` field drives navigation, a separate `navigate to` action for that same URL is duplicative.

### `setup.py`


### `tox.ini`

<!-- rule:51 -->
- Set `basepython = python3` in non-CI tox envs (docs, lint, local tests); pin e.g. `python3.8` only when CI or reproducibility requires it — unpinned interpreter keeps local envs runnable across contributors' machines. — Scoped to local/auxiliary tox environments in tox.ini; avoids the breakage where a pinned minor version is missing on a contributor's machine.
<!-- rule:4537 -->
- Wrap long comma-separated values in `tox.ini` (e.g. `passenv`, `deps`) across lines — matches file style and stays readable — Keeps `tox.ini` list values diff-friendly and consistent with surrounding entries while remaining valid tox syntax, avoiding unwieldy single-line declarations.
<!-- rule:4538 -->
- In `tox.ini` `setenv`, use `VAR=value` (no spaces) when the tox version supports it, else `VAR = value` per official tox docs — the two forms are parsed differently across versions, so wrong spacing can yield a stray-whitespace value or leave the variable unset, silently breaking tests — The `setenv` format changed between tox versions (`VAR=value` vs. ini-style `VAR = value`). Using the form the installed tox version doesn't expect can set the variable under the wrong name, include stray whitespace in its value, or drop the entry entirely — the tests then read a missing or malformed env var and fail (or pass incorrectly) with no config error to point at. Matching the spacing to the supported tox version keeps `setenv` behavior identical on developers' machines and CI.

<!-- /braindump -->
